*As I reflect frequently and ever more deeply in my mind, Most Serene and Most High Princes, Most Merciful Lords, upon the vast heaps of Alexandrine* <note>original: "Alexandrinæ" — a reference to the legendary generosity of Alexander the Great, a common compliment for royal patrons</note> *generosity with which you have most mercifully and often fulfilled and blessed me, and by which Your Highnesses* <note>original: "Cel: V." abbreviating Celsitudines Vestrae</note> *have bound me to you until my final breath, the thought occurred to me: with what veneration, or with what industry of my intellect I might...*

# DEDICATORY LETTER

<meta>catchword: co-Lu-</meta>

...Your Highnesses, in accordance with the respect of my most devoted soul, so that I might at least declare some attempt at gratitude for the favors bestowed upon me by some testimony. Others have been accustomed, when they dedicate their scholarly labors <note>original: "lucubrationes," literally "work done by lamplight," implying diligent study</note> to Princes and Great Men, to concern themselves primarily with weaving their genealogies, recounting their praises and their brilliant and heroic deeds in an elegant style of writing, and—as the saying goes—proclaiming them with the "fullest trumpets" <note>original: "plenissimis tubis," a rhetorical metaphor for loud, public, or ostentatious praise</note>, raising and extolling them even to the stars. I, however, knowing that Your Highnesses find anything inflated or turgid with boastful fame to be distasteful—especially since that glorious and memorable excellence of yours, combined with great-souled generosity <term>magnanima munificentia: the classical virtue of "magnanimity" (greatness of soul) expressed through lavish and noble giving</term>, has flourished through the ages of so many centuries in the most August House of Brunswick—

<summary>In this portion of the dedication, the author expresses his desire to show gratitude for the Dukes' favors and contrasts his sincere approach with other writers who use exaggerated praise and complex genealogies to flatter their patrons.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, gratitude, House of Brunswick, patronage, rhetoric, magnanimity</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory letter to the Dukes of Brunswick-Lüneburg. The previous page ended with the author noting how the noble reputation of the House of Brunswick has flourished for centuries.</meta>

# DEDICATORY LETTER

->* 4<-

...and Lüneburg House, has shone and flourished; nay, and by Divine favor, it shall shine and flourish into eternity. For this reason, I forgo all those aforementioned praises; instead, I approach bearing a gift. It is not, to be sure, a gift of great beauty or size—though You are most worthy of the finest and greatest things—nor one gleaming with gold, silver, gems, or pearls. Such riches Your Highnesses already possess in abundance through the generous bounty and favor of the Divine <note>original: "Numinis," referring to the divine will or the power of God</note>, whereas Fortune has denied them to me and to men of my humble station. It is a gift, I say, made of paper, yet one unearthed from the true, most ancient, and venerable art of <term>Proto-Chemistry</term> <note>original: "Proto-Chimica"; the author refers to the foundational or "first" chemistry, likely what we now call alchemy or early chemical philosophy</note>. This art holds more in its hidden depths than its outward appearance promises; indeed, it is the very means by which the uncreated Spirit, the Craftsman of the World <note>original: "mundi faber," a classical way of describing God as a divine architect or smith</note>, separated the tangled elements of nature. Through this art, indeed, by...

<meta>catchword: suasi</meta>

<summary>The author explains that instead of offering the Dukes gold or jewels, which they already possess in abundance, he offers a humble book containing the ancient secrets of "Proto-Chemistry." He describes this art as the fundamental method used by the Creator to organize the natural world.</summary>

<keywords>House of Brunswick-Lüneburg, dedication, patronage, Proto-Chemistry, alchemy, Divine Craftsman, humility, gift-giving</keywords>

<header>L E T T E R</header>

I persuaded myself that I could give nothing more pleasing to Your Highnesses <note>original: "Cels: V:", an abbreviation for *Celsitudinibus Vestris*</note> than this new work of this Natural Science—an art most ancient and venerable, yet marvelous and utterly essential. It is a work, I say, of the utmost curiosity <note>In this context, "curious" implies something highly detailed, expert, or requiring great care</note>, which until now no one has attempted to restore because of the envy of others. Now, although unconquered truth is commendable by its own strength alone and is sufficiently fortified by its own excellent powers—for nothing can endure that is not solidified in the very foundation of Nature and equipped with this invincible protection—nevertheless, the purity of my writing, being dedicated and consecrated to Your Highnesses and armed with such splendor, shall go forth more boldly into the light and the gaze of men with doubled strength. Receive it, therefore, with a serene countenance, Most Serene and

->Most High<-

<summary>The author argues that while the "Natural Science" he presents is powerful enough to stand on its own merits, the prestige of the Dukes' patronage will protect the work from the envy of critics. He asks the nobles to receive his restored ancient knowledge with favor.</summary>

<keywords>Natural Science, restoration of knowledge, patronage, truth, House of Brunswick, protection against envy, dedicatory preface</keywords>

## DEDICATION:

*Most High Princes, I offer this small little work <note>original: "opusculum," a humble term for a short book or treatise</note>, by its title alone, so that if you do not love it; at least you might continue to favor it, Your Highnesses:*

*Venice, on the Ides <note>In the Roman calendar, the Ides of May falls on the 15th day of the month</note> of May, 1666.*

->*Your Most Loving and Most Obedient,*<-

->Otto Tachenius<-

->TO THE<-

# MOST LEARNED 
# AUTHOR,

->*Truly a Philosopher through fire and water.* <note>An alchemical reference to the dual processes of purification: distillation (water) and calcination (fire).</note><-

->I.<-
->TACHENIUS.<-
->By anagram, removing the breathing sound <note>The letter 'H' was often treated as a mere "aspiration" or breath in Latin and could be omitted to facilitate the creation of anagrams.</note><-
->But you shall conquer,<-
->or<-
->Go this way until now<-

Who would deny ancient Chymistry <note>original: "Chimia," the 17th-century precursor to modern chemistry, often focused on distilling medicines</note>, and the holy discovery,
Which they say the divine Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician; Tachenius argues that Hippocrates used chemical principles in his medicine.</note> cultivated?
By THIS way the path to the secrets of holy nature lies open,
Without this you will scarcely prepare a work worthy of praise:
Without this the Physician provides nothing for the languishing body,
Without this the great pharmacist <note>original: "pharmacopola," meaning an apothecary or medicine-seller</note> will give nothing.
GO THIS WAY by this path; she <note>Wisdom or Chymistry</note> teaches the labors of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophia"</note>,
Let neither the labor nor foul monsters frighten you at once.
Industrious one, you should shake out the secrets of hidden nature;
Here the nourishing priest of nature teaches the way.
I confess there is sweat at the beginning, but with time YOU SHALL CONQUER,
And it is given to pluck the apples of the Hesperides <note>In Greek mythology, golden apples guarded by nymphs; in this context, they symbolize the precious fruits of alchemical success.</note> by hand.

->I I.<-

The Chymist, pregnant with Fire, Water, Earth, and Air,
Could not fail to have brought forth a good birth.
For he encloses these four <note>The four classical elements</note> in one center,
By Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire, representing the alchemist's furnace</note>, by wit, and by the dexterity of the hand.

->I I I.<-

The Chymist by his art encloses the medicine in one
Center, from which the whole crowd of diseases flees.

<meta>This page continues the sequence of laudatory poems (encomia) written by peers and friends of Otto Tachenius. While the previous page focused on the "Chemical" art as a path to wisdom, this page shifts to praising Tachenius himself as a "Titan" of knowledge who brings light to the darkness of medical ignorance, concluding with a personal tribute from Nicolaus Zaffius.</meta>

What use is it to hear the countless crowds of doctors
When a single medicine is sufficient for diseases?
When the ailing body has felt this within,
With strength regained, the man is restored to life.
The most learned Otto—the praise and glory of the **Apolline art** <note>original: "Artis Apollineæ," referring to medicine, as Apollo was the Greek god of healing and the father of Asclepius.</note>—teaches this to everyone with rare genius.

### IV.

If you unlock the true gate, **Chymist**, you find hidden
Treasures, and you put poverty to flight;
Nor will you only be able to dispel poverty, but also those diseases
Which ravage the work of the divine finger <note>A poetic reference to the human body as a creation of God.</note>.
The most learned Otto delivers the key with which you unlock the gate,
And he instructs you with his immense art.

### V.

->**Otto Tachenius**<-
->By anagram:<-
->**O Titan, be present here!** <note>original: "O Titan huc esto!" A Latin anagram of the author's name.</note><-

Hitherto true doctrines lay in darkness;
What use is it to have held a shadow instead of the truth?
But a new **TITAN** <note>A common metaphor in this period for a person of great intellect who "illuminates" a field; also a reference to the Sun.</note> who rises from the **Adriatic Olympus** <note>A reference to Venice, situated on the Adriatic Sea, where Tachenius lived and practiced.</note>
Will scatter the **Cimmerian darkness** <note>original: "Cimmerias tenebras," a classical reference to a mythical land of perpetual mist and darkness.</note> with his light.
O pure seeker, whoever you are who seeks the inner sanctum of truth,
**BE PRESENT HERE**, where the **TITAN** strikes everywhere with his rays.
If you continue to scatter your lights to the world, Titan,
Both poles, radiating light, shall sing of you.

### VI.

->*To the little golden book*<-

Where am I being carried? Behold, most learned little book, go with me;
If you go with me, I shall not be alone.
Thus, with me shall go the most learned crowd of wise men,
All of whom you enclose within this small work.

# TO THE UNBIASED READER,
## AND THE STUDENT
## OF THE MOST ANCIENT
### *Hippocratic Doctrine.*
## PREFACE.

<margin>
The writings of the ancients are enigmatic and like the oracles of Apollo.
</margin>

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'H' containing a figure of a man (likely a scholar or physician) seated at a desk within an architectural frame.</image-desc>
Hippocrates, the most radiant light of Medicine, veiled his divine oracles in enigmas, and delivered his precepts laconically <note>original: "laconicè." Refers to the style of the Spartans (Laconia), known for using few words; here, it means Hippocrates was intentionally brief and cryptic.</note> throughout all the works of his teaching; so much so, that his obscure brevity and his sayings have been distorted by various writers into many different meanings. Some of these, Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), the most influential physician of antiquity after Hippocrates, who systematized medical knowledge into a rigid framework that dominated Europe for 1,500 years.</note> arranged into chapters with wonderful skill of art and in graceful order; others, however, and especially the **GOLDEN LITTLE BOOK ON DIET** <note>original: "AVREVM LIBELLVM DE DIÆTA." This refers to the Hippocratic treatise *De Victu*, which Tachenius believes contains hidden chemical secrets.</note>, which is full of mysteries, he left untouched. For the divine old man <note>A respectful reference to Hippocrates.</note> assigned these to the practitioners of chemistry <note>original: "chimiæ cultoribus." Tachenius is arguing that Hippocrates was an early chemist whose true meaning can only be understood by chemists.</note> alone, an art which of old—and perhaps in the age of Hippocrates—was called **natural philosophy**. Who...

<meta>catchword: For</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that Hippocrates intentionally wrote in riddles to hide medical secrets, claiming that while Galen organized the basic teachings, the deeper "chemical" mysteries were left for future specialists to uncover.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, Galen, medical history, alchemy, chemistry, De Dieta, natural philosophy, medical enigmas</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, where Tachenius argued that Hippocrates intentionally hid chemical secrets in his writings—specifically in the "Book on Diet"—which Galen and other traditional physicians failed to grasp because they lacked chemical training.</meta>

...for how will he perceive with his mind the most rare eloquence of the old man <note>A respectful reference to Hippocrates.</note>, or the gentle fire <note>original: "ignem mollem." In the chemical tradition, this refers to *ignis mitis*, a controlled, moderate heat used for the slow maturation or "digestion" of substances.</note> mentioned there, unless he is practiced in this hidden Hippocratic natural doctrine? 

Raymond Lull <note>Raimundus Lullius (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and mystic. Many alchemical texts were attributed to him, and he was a hero to later "chymists" who valued experimental results over abstract logic.</note> testifies to this:

<margin>a Testament, chapter 26.</margin>
[a) *However much,* he says, *a logician may possess a profound talent for argument, or a natural talent regarding external matters, yet he will never be able to directly know—nor judge by any reasoning that reaches the senses—how a seed sprouts in the earth, grows, and gathers its fruit, unless he first enters into* **OUR NATURAL PHILOSOPHY** *by means of experimental doctrine. He must not follow that sophisticated, talkative philosophy born of logicians through various fantastical presumptions. Such men, with their forecasts of logical consequences that go against the force of nature, cause many to stubbornly err in the sophistry of the mind. This is because through our mechanical science <note>original: "mechanicam scientiam." Here, "mechanical" refers to "manual" or laboratory-based work—knowledge gained by the hands and eyes rather than just the tongue.</note>, the intellect is rectified by the power of experience, in view of the eye and true mental knowledge; indeed, our experiences stand above...*]

<meta>catchword: pro.</meta>

<summary>Tachenius quotes the medieval authority Raymond Lull to argue that true understanding of nature and Hippocratic medicine comes only through "mechanical science" (experimentation) rather than the abstract logic of schoolmen.</summary>

<keywords>Hippocrates, Raymond Lull, natural philosophy, experimental science, logicians vs. practitioners, chemical fire, seed growth</keywords>

# TO THE CANDID READER,
->AND THE STUDENT<-
->OF THE MOST ANCIENT<-
->*Hippocratic Doctrine.*<-

## PREFACE.

<margin>The writings of the ancients are enigmatic and like the oracles of Apollo.</margin>
Hippocrates, the brightest light of Medicine, veiled his Divine oracles in enigmas, and reported his precepts laconically <note>original: "laconicè." This refers to a brief, concise style of speech or writing, named after the Spartans of Laconia who were famous for their short, pithy statements.</note> throughout all the works of his teaching; so much so, that his obscure brevity and his utterances have been distorted into various meanings by writers. Some of these Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the Greek physician whose system of "humors" dominated Western medicine for over a millennium. The author suggests Galen was a great organizer but missed the deeper chemical truths.</note> arranged into chapters with wonderful skill of art and a graceful order; but others, and especially the *GOLDEN LITTLE BOOK ON DIET* <note>original: "AVREVM LIBELLVM DE DIÆTA." This refers to the Hippocratic treatise *De Victu*, which many 17th-century "chymists" believed contained hidden alchemical instructions regarding the balance of fire and water.</note>, which is full of mysteries, he left untouched. For the divine old man <note>A common respectful title for Hippocrates.</note> entrusted these to the cultivators of chemistry alone, which art formerly—and perhaps in the age of Hippocrates—was called natural philosophy. For who

<meta>catchword: enim (for)</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that Hippocrates intentionally used "laconic" and enigmatic language to hide his true medical wisdom from the uninitiated, claiming that his "Book on Diet" can only be understood by those practiced in chemistry.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, Galen, Book on Diet, alchemy, natural philosophy, medical history, laconic style</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous leaf, where the author asserts that the true meaning of Hippocrates is hidden from those who only study logic. He relies on the authority of Raymond Lull to argue that "mechanical science"—what we would call experimental chemistry—is the only way to understand the vital processes of nature, such as the growth of a seed.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

*...for [who] will perceive in their mind the most rare eloquence of the old man* <note>referring to Hippocrates, the "father of medicine."</note>*, or the* **gentle fire** <note>original: "ignem mollem." This refers to a specific chemical technique of using low, steady heat (like a water bath) to transform substances without destroying them.</note> *in that same place, unless they are practiced in this hidden Hippocratic natural doctrine?* As Raymond <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and logician to whom many influential alchemical and chemical treatises were later attributed. He is cited here as a champion of experimental knowledge over mere theory.</note> attests:

<margin>a) Testament, chapter 26.</margin>
"However much a logician," he says, "may have a profound talent for argument, or a natural talent regarding external things, he will nevertheless never be able, through any reasoning that reaches the senses, to directly know or judge how a seed sprouts in the earth, grows, and gathers fruit, unless he has first entered into **OUR NATURAL PHILOSOPHY** with experimental doctrine. He must avoid that sophisticated, talkative philosophy born of logicians through various fantastical presumptions. Such men, with their predictions of consequences that run contrary to the force of nature, cause many to stubbornly err in the sophistication of their own minds. Because through our **MECHANICAL SCIENCE** <note>original: "mechanicam scientiam." In this period, "mechanical" refers to manual, laboratory-based work—the "hands-on" practice of chemistry as opposed to abstract speculation.</note>, the intellect is rectified by the power of experience, in view of the eye and true mental knowledge; indeed, our experiences stand above...

<meta>catchword: ...above [pro...]</meta>

<summary>The author quotes Raymond Lull to argue that abstract logic cannot explain natural growth or chemical change. He asserts that only "mechanical science" (experimental chemistry) provides the direct experience necessary to understand the hidden doctrines of Hippocrates.</summary>

<keywords>Raymond Lull, Hippocrates, experimental doctrine, natural philosophy, mechanical science, logic vs experience, alchemy, seeds of nature</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence started on the previous page, finishing the critique of purely theoretical logic in favor of "mechanical science" or experimental chemistry. It introduces the term "Spagyria" and references the famous "Emerald Tablet" of Hermes Trismegistus to justify this experimental approach. It then transitions back to the works of Hippocrates.</meta>

# PREFACE

...fantastical proofs of conclusions, and therefore they do not tolerate them; instead, they show that all other sciences enter vividly into the intellect. From this, we subsequently understand through nature the inner essence of what a thing is and what kind of thing it is. Because through such science, the intellect stands stripped of the superfluities and errors that usually remove it from the truth, due to the presumptions and prejudices believed in their conclusions. For this reason, our people <note>original: "nostri," referring to the practitioners of this chemical philosophy.</note> have directed themselves toward entering, through any science, into every experience by means of art, according to the course of nature, in its **UNIVOCAL PRINCIPLES** <term>Univocal Principles: rules or foundations that have one single, clear meaning and application across all of nature, as opposed to ambiguous theories.</term>.

For **Spagyria** <term>Spagyria: the alchemical art of separating and recombining substances to extract their purest medicinal essences; the precursor to modern chemistry.</term> alone is the mirror of true understanding; it allows one to touch and see their truths in a clear light. And so the **Emerald Tablet** <note>original: "tabula smaragdina." A legendary, brief foundational text of alchemy attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, famously teaching the principle "as above, so below."</note> holds: "By this kind of demonstration, all obscurity shall flee from you," etc.

<margin>a) From Book 1 of *On Regimen* (De Dieta).</margin>
Hippocrates points to the foundations of this most ancient art in the beginning of the book cited above (a),

<summary>The author concludes a quote from Raymond Lull arguing that experimental chemistry (Spagyria) is the only way to strip away intellectual errors and see nature clearly. He cites the Emerald Tablet and begins to link these "ancient arts" back to the specific writings of Hippocrates.</summary>

<keywords>Spagyria, Emerald Tablet, Tabula Smaragdina, Hippocrates, experimental science, logic vs experience, univocal principles</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction of Hippocrates’ theories, specifically moving from the general principles of nature mentioned on the previous page to his specific elemental theory of life. The catchword "Mul-" on the previous page anticipates "Mulieres" (women) in the quotation from the Book of Esdras found here.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

He <note>Referring to Hippocrates, cited on the previous page from his work *On Regimen*.</note> says: all other living things, as well as man himself, are constituted from two things, different indeed in their power, but harmonious in their purpose and useful in their application: **FIRE** and **WATER**. Both of these together are sufficient for all other things, as well as for each other; yet either one by itself is sufficient neither for itself nor for any other, etc.

*This fire—a gentle one, to be sure—and this coagulable water, which have been obscure until now, I have decided to show to the eye in this small manual, solely out of a desire to recognize the truth. In this age, truth is miserably suppressed by those who hate the **Hippocratic doctrine** <term>Hippocratic doctrine: The ancient medical system attributed to Hippocrates, which emphasizes that health comes from a balance of bodily fluids and elements.</term>; nonetheless, it is impossible for it to be crushed, for truth is so powerful, unshakeable, and triumphant over all things in the world, as is also proven by the sacred writings. ᵃ*

<margin>ᵃ 1 Esdras, chapters 3 and 4.</margin>
*For Zorobabel <note>original: "Zorobabel." ...and the blending of other colors emerged from it. But who can recount all the benefits that have arisen from this art for human use?

<margin>beer.</margin>

Now, as **Galen** <note>A famous Greek physician (c. 129–216 AD) whose theories on "humors" dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> teaches, barley produces gas <note>original: "ventositates," or windiness in the stomach.</note> if it is undercooked, and causes blockages <note>original: "oppilationes," referring to obstructions in the body's internal channels.</note> if it is boiled only slightly better. They transformed it into a new kind of seed, which they no longer call barley, but **malt** <note>original: "maltham."</note>.
<margin>To brew</margin> <note>original: "Brasser," a French term for the brewing process.</note>
They cook the crushed malt and once again rouse the internal instruments toward transformation; this action is called **fermentation**, because it appears to boil <note>The Latin *fermentatio* is derived from *fervere*, meaning "to boil" or "to seethe."</note>. By this method, following the teachings of Hippocrates, a most healthy drink is produced from it.

<margin>wine.</margin>

# PREFACE

<margin>Bread.</margin>
Nor would **bread** <note>original: "panis."</note>, if this art were lacking, ever be made, unless the instruments of nature within the flour were awakened by a proportionate external fire and water. But when these elements begin to act upon one another, if there is a lack of new material, the mass first turns sour, then gradually decays, and finally breeds worms. Art, however, delays or hinders the operation of these natural "instruments" <note>The author uses "instruments" to refer to the active, internal forces or enzymes within a substance that drive chemical change.</note> through intense heat or cold, and the work is brought to completion by the craftsman to provide the life-sustaining nourishment necessary for mortals.

But not only food, but even healthy medicines of great power cannot do without this art. This is shown, among other things, by **Theriaca** <note>A famous ancient "universal" medicine or antidote, often containing dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh, which required long periods of aging or fermentation.</note>; for unless the infinite natural instruments of the **simples** <note>original: "simplicium." ### TO THE READER.

...for through **fermentation** <note>original: "fermentationem" (split across pages). The author argues that fermentation is a universal process of natural change.</note> all natural things are transformed, as is well known in the case of grape juice, beer, and bread.

<margin>Objection.</margin>
Reason, they say, and truth itself, prove that all these things are so. However, we attack the **novelties** in philosophy and medicine that you promote—specifically your use of mineral and metallic remedies. These are quite contrary to human nature and were unknown to the ancients.

<margin>Response.</margin>
<margin>(a) Nothing is made that did not exist before, but things are mixed and [separated]. See Book 1, *On Diet*.</margin>
It is impossible to introduce true "novelties" into the world. However, **Hippocrates** (a) taught—and it is permitted for Art to do—that we may perfect, alter, mix, and compose those things already introduced by the Creator. Thus **flax**, through various alterations, becomes **paper**; **grass**, through various alterations, becomes a **glove** <note>original: "chiroteca." This refers to the biological cycle where grass is eaten by livestock, whose hide is then processed into leather.</note>; and even **glue**. The juice of flowers, through alterations, becomes **honey**, and this, through secret alterations, becomes **mead** <note>original: "hydromel."</note>. This is a most pleasant drink among the Lithuanians, yielding nothing to Spanish wine; eventually, it becomes **vinegar**, which then easily returns to elemental water. **Grain**, through alteration, becomes **spirits** <note>original: "aqua ardens," literally "burning water," an early term for distilled alcohol.</note>. So too, various **simples** <note>Individual medicinal herbs or ingredients.</note>, gathered into one, mixed, and through fermen-

<meta>catchword: -tation</meta>

<summary>The author defends the use of chemical and mineral medicines by arguing that "Art" (chemistry) does not create new things, but merely transforms existing natural substances, just as grass becomes leather or grain becomes alcohol.</summary>

# PREFACE

...then, having been altered, they become **Theriac** <note>original: "Theriaca." A famous ancient polypharmaceutical "cure-all" or antidote, often containing dozens of ingredients including opium and viper flesh.</note>.

Thus after **Hippocrates**, **cassia**, **senna**, **mechoacan** <note>original: "mechoacanna." A root from Mexico used as a purgative. Its mention here signifies the introduction of New World medicines into the European pharmacopeia.</note>, **cream of tartar**, and infinite other things were discovered, which he did not mention; yet these are not truly "new," but rather have been called into use through study and long experience, not without benefit; so much so that,
<margin>There is nothing new under the sun, nor is anyone able to say: "See, this is new." *Ecclesiastes chapter 1, verse 10*</margin>
we dare to exclaim with the Wise Man: *there is nothing new under the sun.* Whatever, therefore, a follower of **Hippocrates** finds and brings forth with a midwife's hand <note>original: "obstetricia manu." A metaphor for the scientific or alchemical process aiding nature to "give birth" to a discovery.</note>, that thing existed from the beginning; but your eyes were not yet opened to recognize them.

But to this objection, **Étienne Pasquier** <note>A French lawyer and man of letters (1529–1615) who often wrote on the tension between tradition and innovation.</note> responds quite elegantly:

->They say **Paracelsus** is "new" to us, and for that<-
->crime, he is driven into obscure exile;<-
->But **Hippocrates** was new once, as was **Chrysippus**, and<-
->even **Asclepiades** in Rome, each in his own time.<-
->You who condemn new things, must condemn the ancients,<-
->Or else there is nothing "new" in this novelty.<-

## TO THE READER.

As for mineral and metallic remedies, these were not recently introduced into medicine, but were used by the ancients. For **Theriac** <note>original: "Theriaca." An ancient, complex "universal" antidote. The author argues it requires minerals to be effective.</note>, a most ancient compound, would be clearly ineffective without **chalcitis** <note>original: "calcitide." A mineral byproduct of copper ore (likely a type of iron or copper sulfate) used in ancient pharmacology.</note>. **Alkermes** <note>A famous medicinal cordial or syrup, often dyed red with kermes insects; the author notes it includes mineral components.</note> includes **lapis lazuli** and **gold**, the most perfect and noble of metals. Likewise, **Mesue** <note>Referring to Mesue the Younger, a legendary but influential figure in medieval Islamic medicine whose texts were standard in Europe.</note> composed pills from that same stone [lapis lazuli] for **melancholy** and **insanity** that were not without use, which even more recent physicians coat in gold or silver.

<margin>Silver.</margin>
Among the Lithuanians in the city of **Vilnius**, I knew a silversmith—a robust, unmarried, and unlearned man, yet possessed of such a fortunate memory that, in the language familiar to him, he could recite almost word-for-word whatever he had heard but once. From his youth until his 47th year at that time, he had daily (out of habit, I believe) ingested a small amount of **silver filings** while working, and he was firmly convinced that this practice had increased his memory.

<margin>Gemstones.</margin>
Do not **emeralds**, **rubies**, and **hyacinths** <note>original: "hyacinthi." In this context, a precious stone—likely blue zircon or sapphire—rather than the flower.</note> enter into various compositions which we call **cordials**? <note>Medicines intended to stimulate the heart and vital spirits.</note>

<summary>The author defends the use of mineral and metallic remedies by citing their presence in ancient formulas like Theriac and the works of Mesue, while providing an anecdotal account of a silversmith's use of silver to aid memory.</summary>
<keywords>mineral remedies, pharmacology, Theriac, Alkermes, Mesue, silver, memory, gemstones, Vilnius, cordials</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that "chemical" or mineral-based medicines are not modern innovations but have been validated since antiquity. Having previously discussed gold, silver, and gemstones, he now moves to common minerals like sulphur and salt.</meta>

# PREFACE
<meta>catchword: simi- (Likely the start of 'similia', meaning 'similar things')</meta>

<margin>Sulphur.</margin>
*Sulphur is prescribed daily for lung ailments; it was praised by **Dioscorides** <note>original: "Dioscoride." Pedanius Dioscorides was a 1st-century Greek physician whose work "De Materia Medica" was the primary pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note>, a most ancient writer.*

<margin>Iron.</margin>
*Iron was celebrated by the ancients for ailments of the **spleen** <note>In ancient and medieval medicine, the spleen was the seat of the "black bile" humor; iron, associated with the strength of the god Mars, was believed to treat its disorders.</note>, and we still use it today with great benefit. Among the ancients, **mineral waters** <note>original: "aquæ minerales." Referring to natural thermal or mineral springs used for therapeutic bathing and drinking.</note> were held in great veneration, and we too seek refuge in them annually for desperate cases.*

<margin>Salt.</margin>
*But what will they say about **salt**? Without it, human life cannot endure. Is this substance, which we use daily to season our food, a new invention? Or did the ancients lack knowledge of it? Yet, salt is not only boiled out from the sea and springs; there are also mountains and mines of native solid salt, much like marble, where it is quarried like stone and regenerates. I have seen these myself in **Wallachia** <note>A historical region of modern-day Romania.</note> and near **Kraków** <note>Referring to the massive and wealthy Wieliczka salt mines in Poland.</note>, where it provides a greater tax revenue for the Kings than any other thing. Since salt can be called neither a vegetable nor an animal, it must therefore be a mineral.*

*Likewise, the ancients applied minerals to external uses: ointments, plasters, **eye-salves** <note>original: "collyria." These were medicinal preparations specifically for the eyes, often containing mineral powders.</note>, and*

<summary>The author lists sulphur, iron, and salt as ancient mineral remedies, specifically citing salt mining in Poland and Romania to prove that minerals are a natural and necessary part of human life and medicine.</summary>

Therefore, the Hippocratic chemists do not attempt to produce new things; rather, they recall from destruction those things most proven by antiquity.

I, for my part, do not wonder at ignorance; I observe it in all professions, and especially in medicine, which of necessity must have taken its origin from this most ancient art <note>The "ancient art" refers to the chemical or spagyric art, which the author believes predates and informs medical practice.</note>. For this reason, Hippocrates was moved in his work *The Law* 
<margin>b from *The Law*.</margin>
(b) to call medicine the most noble of all arts, yet because of the ignorance of those who practice it badly, it has become the most worthless of dreams <note>original: "somnium vilissimam." A reference to the Hippocratic *Lex*, which laments that while medicine is the most illustrious art, it often has a poor reputation because of unskilled practitioners.</note>.

It is ignorance, therefore, which generates every evil in medicine; hence nowhere is there greater envy than among those who are alienated and severed from Hippocrates. Their faces are usually livid with that crime, so much so that the physician is now everywhere exposed to mockery, having become a joke for comic actors and a proverb to the world.

Alas!

## TO THE READER.

Alas! Where is the majesty with which the followers of Asclepius <note>original: "Aesculapij." Asclepius was the Greek god of healing; his "followers" were the respected physicians of antiquity.</note> once shone among their own? For if they had learned the principles of **chemistry**—that is, the instruments of nature—alongside Hippocrates, from our furnaces <note>The "furnaces" refers to the chemical laboratory, where the "spagyric" or chemical secrets of nature are unlocked through fire.</note> as the foundation of medicine, then medicine would not have collapsed into so many errors and sects, nor would this division cease today.

For a petty sect arises, establishing a "dual medicine"—namely, the **chemical** and the **Galenic** <note>The followers of Galen relied on herbal "humoral" medicine, while the new chemical physicians used minerals and metals. Usually a respectful reference to Hippocrates as the "Old Man" of medicine.</note>, who taught only one. Nor did Galen make mention of such a sect. ### PREFACE

...each one acts, and tends in both directions (it grows and flourishes), and thus they imitate the nature of man.

The chemical philosopher <note>The author uses "chemical philosopher" to describe a scientist who seeks to understand the hidden laws of nature through laboratory work.</note>, having been taught by Hippocrates, takes the seeds already brought to maturity by nature and considers within them the two instruments of nature—the salts—namely fire and water. He compels these, through art, to act by liquefying the salts according to the law of nature, just as alchemy teaches; and something new is created that did not exist before: for example, **BREAD**. In that bread, the Hippocratic and chemical philosopher again considers the instruments of nature, which he further compels with a midwife’s hand <note>original: "obstetricia manu." A metaphor for the scientist assisting nature to "give birth" to a new substance.</note>, and he liquefies the salts—imitating nature—and again produces something new that did not exist before, and this we call **VINEGAR**. In that vinegar, the chemical philosopher notes that fire overcomes water; for, as the teacher commands (a) <margin>a *On Ancient Medicine*</margin> <note>The author cites Hippocrates' famous treatise *De prisca medicina*.</note>, no single flavor should dominate by its own power. ## TO THE READER.

...they bring forth, or liquefy by either a gentle or a strong fire, all depend on this most ancient chemical philosophy <note>original: "chimica philosophia." In this era, "chemistry" and "alchemy" were often interchangeable terms for the science of matter and its transformations.</note>. Thus, they betray a gross ignorance and strike a heavy blow against the truth—following the view of Hippocrates—who exclude the Pharmacist <note>original: "Pharmacopoeum." A maker of medicines.</note> from this art. For when he pounds vegetables and expresses the juice, then he truly pours out the salts; indeed, he thrusts out foreign and alien things that do not have the same character from the region (these are the words of Hippocrates). But when he thickens the liquefied salts with sugar into a syrup, then he mixes and alters them. Just so is the nature of man, says the Old Man <note>original: "Senex." This likely refers to Hippocrates as the venerable authority of medicine.</note>: and all arts communicate with human nature.

<margin>a See chapter 13 of this booklet.</margin>

This refers to traditional doctors who relied strictly on established university textbooks (the "canon") and rejected newer chemical or alchemical medical theories.</note>, who, out of ignorance of this most ancient art, hates the dogmas of Hippocrates; for he is most ignorant of this divinely given science. # THE REASONS
## FOR WRITING
### CHAPTER I.

<margin>A person knows only as much as they have clearly perceived by applying their own mind and hand to the work.</margin>
The perfect knowledge of all the <term>sciences</term> <note>original: "scientiarum." In this context, it refers to organized bodies of knowledge or branches of learning.</note> is so difficult to achieve that a person's entire life might fail before we are able to investigate the fundamental principles of even one discipline—at least, principles that would not be open to dispute—based only on paper books (a).
<margin>a Books are not, however, to be rejected.</margin>
When Socrates had scrutinized almost every field of study in this way, he was only then declared the wisest of all men by the oracle when he openly admitted that he knew nothing. I believe that the Preacher <note>original: "Ecclesiastes." The traditional name for the author of the biblical book of the same name, often translated as "the Teacher" or "the Preacher."</note> confirms this for me here (b).
<margin>b Chapter 8, verse 17.</margin>
*I understood,* he says, *that of all the works of God, man can find no explanation for those things that are done under the sun: and the more he labors to search, the less he finds; even if the wise man says he knows, he will not be able to find it.* This [difficulty] of knowing— <note>original: "Hanc sciendi difficul-" This is a catchword, an old printing practice where the first syllable of the next page is printed at the bottom of the current one to ensure the pages are bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>The author opens his first chapter by arguing that true knowledge is nearly impossible to attain through books alone, citing the humility of Socrates and the biblical wisdom of Ecclesiastes to show that human understanding is limited when faced with the works of God.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Ecclesiastes, limits of knowledge, philosophy of science, wisdom, human understanding, empiricism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory argument from Page 1, moving from the philosophical limitations of knowledge (Socrates and Ecclesiastes) to the specific challenges of the medical art as expressed by Hippocrates, before transitioning to a contemporary critique of a specific pharmacopoeia.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>TACHENIUS'S</header>
<meta>catchword: non</meta>

—this difficulty was even recognized by the <margin>In his Letters.</margin> divine Hippocrates (a) in our own field of medicine. He was not afraid to confess it honestly to Democritus <note>Democritus was a Greek philosopher known for the atomic theory; early traditions include a series of fictional letters between him and Hippocrates.</note> through letters: *For though I am now an old man,* he said, *I have not yet reached the pinnacle of the medical art.* <margin>b Book 1, Aphorism 1.</margin> And the same man (b) recognized that *life is far too short for even a single science to be learned thoroughly from its foundations.* For this reason, he was moved <margin>c Book 1, dedicated.</margin> (c) to command that all those who have attempted to investigate the *secret works of wise nature* should be honored and praised; he adds that *it is unjust for any of them to be blamed because they were unable to find these things out.*

However, the writer who reformed the <term>Augsburg Pharmacopoeia</term> <note>original: "Augustanam pharmacopœam." This refers to the official manual of medicinal recipes used in Augsburg, one of the most influential medical texts of the 17th century.</note> feels differently. That book was printed at Gouda, by Willem Vorhoeven, in the Year of our Lord 1657. On the occasion of this book's appearance a few days ago, my most esteemed friend, Jean De Lanou—a very famous bookseller of this city—asked me whether I judged it worth the effort to have it submitted to the press here? and so on. I had indeed heard the title, but until then I [had not seen] the writing...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of a contemporary medical writer who had "reformed" the Augsburg Pharmacopoeia. <header>*HIPPOCRATES.*</header>

I had not seen the work, for I did not have so much leisure that I should waste my time on such matters. On this occasion, however, I leafed through the said volume in my spare hours—not without some shock—and in it I noted that wicked habit, so common to some and yet so contrary to the decree of Hippocrates. This habit is that whenever someone has discovered something worthy through great labor and study and has committed it to writing for the benefit of posterity and the public good, these people immediately—not by means of reason or experience, but through monstrous slanders and abuse—defame, tear apart, and belittle it before the ignorant masses. They do this so that they might hunt for a bit of fame (much like <note>Herostratus was an arsonist who burned the Temple of Artemis in 356 BCE solely to ensure his name would be remembered by history.</note> Herostratus) built upon the ruins of another’s reputation.

The Reformer of the *Augsburg Pharmacopoeia* walks in these very footsteps. He rants so violently against not only the ancients but also the moderns—the followers of Hippocrates and <note>Galen was a 2nd-century Greek physician whose work dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> Galen—that there is almost no insult imaginable with which he does not bespatter them. Once, when [he taught] the method of perfecting <term>viper’s salt</term> <note>original: "viperini salis." In 17th-century chemistry and pharmacy, preparations made from vipers were highly valued as "essences" believed to cure various ailments and drive out toxins.</note> under the guise of a metaphor...

Now indeed I take up this labor more willingly, so that I may show the right path to those wandering, and recall them to that same path if they have happened to stray. I have not poured myself into disputes or hateful altercations, but whatever appeared more hidden to the senses, I have stripped of its coverings—guided by experience—and I have placed the truth of things, plainly naked, before the eyes of the beholders; so much so, that it might be known to every single person: truth desires brevity, not dispute; and this rule I have kept.

Therefore, come here, lovers of truth, you will see with your eyes and touch with your hands wonderful and pleasant things, which until now have stood hidden and concealed from the senses and from knowledge. Now the work itself must be undertaken.

<margin>a Whatever something can be resolved into, it must necessarily consist of that.</margin>
According to Aristotle's axiom (a), it is divided into an acid spirit of salt, and also into a volatile alkali (all salty substances are divided into two substances, namely alkali and acid, as I shall show little by little). These two, having been separated and then reunited, become the <term>sal ammoniac<note>original: "sal armoniacum." Ammonium chloride, a key substance in early chemistry used as a flux for soldering and in various medicines.</note></term> that it was before. 

The acid part, or acid spirit, has all the properties of that which is distilled from common salt. However, the alkali is of the same condition as that which is <term>sublimated<note>A process where a substance is heated until it turns into vapor and then collects as a solid again, used here for purification.</note></term> from human urine, though it is less foul-smelling. ### *What Alkali Salt Is*

### Chapter 3.

<margin>I call the senses the guides of life and of all actions.</margin>
It is not my intent here to begin the matter "from the egg" <note>original: "ab ovo." A classical idiom meaning to start from the very beginning.</note> or from the basic elements, as the saying goes, but rather to begin a demonstration of things that are natural, physical, material, and known to the senses.

Thus, Kali salt is made from the <term>Kali herb<note>The saltwort plant (*Salsola kali*), which grows in salty soils; its ashes were the primary source of soda ash.</note></term>, which grows in great abundance in Egypt. There, it is burned while still green and then transported to us. Some also call it <term>soda<note>Sodium carbonate.</note></term>, <term>potash<note>original: "alumen catinum." Literally "vessel alum," a term for the alkaline salts obtained by leaching plant ashes.</note></term>, and indeed...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the nature of alkali, specifically defining it as a product of human artifice rather than a raw substance found directly in nature. Tachenius moves from the broad definition of "Kali" (the plant-derived salt) to the universal category of "Alkalis" found across the three kingdoms of nature.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
# HIPPOCRATES.
<meta>catchword: my son,</meta>

...is called by the name <term>Kali salt<note>original: "sal Kali." Derived from the Arabic word *al-qaly*, referring to the ashes of the saltwort plant.</note></term>; however, Kali salt is not found in nature, nor in any of the <term>three kingdoms<note>The animal, vegetable, and mineral kingdoms.</note></term>, unless it is produced by a craftsman through the help of fire. For this reason, the ancients wrote that this "virgin" has three fathers: namely Nature, Fire, and the <term>philosopher<note>In alchemical texts, "the philosopher" refers to the chemist or practitioner who understands the hidden workings of nature.</note></term>.

<margin>Alkali is empty and void.</margin>
Kali salt is also a part of a compound, just like other salts, yet it is deprived of <term>seminal virtues<note>original: "virtutibus seminalibus." The vital, generative principles believed to be inherent in living things; here, Tachenius suggests that the process of fire has removed the plant's "life" leaving only the mineral salt.</note></term>; it possesses at least a cleansing power, a <term>saponaceous<note>original: "saponariam." Soap-like; having the ability to emulsify fats or oils.</note></term> quality, and a <term>resolutive<note>original: "resolutiuam." The ability to dissolve or break down other substances.</note></term> property. Because of this "emptiness," as if in molds, it absorbs and "drinks up" all kinds of acids (the <term>fathers<note>The ancient authorities of medicine and alchemy.</note></term> called this <term>impregnation<note>original: "impregnationẽ." The process of an alkali becoming "filled" or neutralized by an acid.</note></term>). Hence, all salts that <term>mortify<note>original: "mortificant." An early chemical term for neutralizing the sharp or corrosive power of an acid.</note></term> acids are called by the single and general name Alkalis. These are found not only in the vegetable kingdom, but also in the animal and mineral families. In these three, they are of many kinds: namely fixed, volatile, manifest, hidden, and of a middle nature, derived from the parts of animals, which are to be demonstrated to the eye <note>Meaning he will provide empirical proof or visual demonstration.</note>; and first, I shall begin with the better-known variety, which is found in

<margin>Flint.</margin>

<summary>Tachenius defines Alkali (Kali salt) as an artificial substance produced by fire from natural materials, describing it as an "empty" vessel that neutralizes acids. He establishes that alkalis exist in various forms (fixed, volatile, etc.) across the animal, vegetable, and mineral kingdoms.</summary>

### HIPPOCRATES .

<margin>Acid and Alkali are nature's instruments. I shall show in their proper places that acid is of fire and alkali is of water.</margin>
...for a [gr]eater understanding, we shall everywhere name **ACID** and **ALKALI** according to their effect.

<margin>a Men have not learned to consider obscure things from those that are manifest. Hippocrates, Book 1, *On Diet*.</margin>
Therefore, <term>lime<note>original: "calx." In 17th-century chemistry, this specifically refers to quicklime (calcium oxide), produced by burning limestone or shells. It was a primary subject for studying chemical reactions due to its heat-producing reaction with water.</note></term> contains within itself two salts—or as Hippocrates says, two faculties—namely, **acid** and **alkali**. Both are most stable in fire, yet one is the contrary of the other. When lime is moistened, those salts are then dissolved and act upon one another: the alkali absorbs and acts upon the acid, and the acid in turn takes up the alkali. This is not for destruction (a), but for a new generation; and whatever they seize during that action, provided it does not depart far from their nature, hardens with them into a stony toughness. Thus, a contrary is coagulated by its contrary.

<margin>b Pharmacy, folio 485</margin>
If the "reformer"<note>Tachenius often uses this term to mock contemporary chemists, likely referring to Paracelsians or those who deviated from what he saw as the true ancient wisdom of Hippocrates.</note> had learned this knowledge about lime from Hippocrates, the wretch would not have attempted the <term>fixation<note>original: "fixationem." The process of making a volatile or "spiritual" substance stable and solid so it can withstand fire.</note></term> of **viperine alkali**<note>original: "alcali viperini." Viper flesh was a prestigious ingredient in early modern medicine, believed to contain powerful salts used in *Theriac*, a universal antidote.</note> with lime, as he declares with a lamentable voice in his discourse on **Theriacal salt** (b).

<margin>Contraries are coagulated by contraries.</margin>
After the follower of Hippocrates noted, according to the laws of nature, that lime is turned to stone by contrary faculties, he recalls the master's and

### Chapter 4.

<margin>c Like things are dissolved by like.</margin>
<term>Lime<note>original: "Calx." Specifically quicklime (calcium oxide), produced by burning limestone.</note></term>, as I have said, possesses two different faculties: **acid** and **alkali**. I have already stated that alkali possesses at least a soap-making and cleansing virtue, which the Hippocratics taught how to extract and separate by using its "like" (c). Thus, soap-makers add to the lime a manufactured **Alkali** made from burned vegetables <note>original: "Alcali factitium ex vegetabilibus exustum." This refers to potash or pearl ash, obtained by leaching the ashes of burnt plants.</note>, in a triple proportion, because it <term>mortifies<note>original: "mortificat." In early chemistry, to "mortify" a substance meant to neutralize its active properties or change its chemical state, effectively "killing" its original form to create something new.</note></term> the acidic part in the lime and liquefies the other part with its like. Therefore, they slightly moisten the alkalized ashes, with which they then cover the lime.

unslaked <term>lime<note>original: "calcem." Specifically quicklime.</note></term>, they leave it in this state until the lime bursts open as a sign of their mutual action. ## Chapter 5.

Glass is made from the same flint and the same <term>alkali salt<note>A basic substance, such as potash or soda ash.</note></term>, following a rule of opposites; however, it is not made by the "wet way" <note>Using liquid solutions.</note> like soap, but by a "dry way" through fiery fusion and melting. Sometimes three parts, or at least two, of powdered flint are mixed with one part of alkali salt—and not the <term>acidic table salt<note>original: "sale... cibarij acidi." Common sea salt (sodium chloride). Tachenius considers it "acidic" and therefore unsuitable for this specific reaction.</note></term>. This alkali melts the flint by eroding it and absorbing it in the heat of the furnace; I say the alkali part absorbs that acid which the flint contains in the place of lime. A substance then floats on top of the molten flint,

<margin>
a. Anatron <note>Naturally occurring sodium carbonate (natron).</note> according to Crollius <note>Oswald Croll (1563–1609), an Alchemist and physician.</note> in his work *The Medicinal Stone*.
</margin>

and it is called **glass grease** (a) or **glass gall** <note>original: "axungia vitri... fel vitri." This is the scum of sulfate and chloride impurities that rises to the top of the melting pot during glassmaking.</note>. The other part of the alkali unites with the flint and is saturated by it. Because they no longer remain in the same state as they were, as Hippocrates teaches, they both transform into a transparent mass; at this point, the craftsmen say the glass is "cooked."

->TACHENIUS<-

Now the alkali floating on the surface, which is saturated with acid, they remove with an iron spoon; they call this <term>glass gall<note>original: "fel vitri." Also known as sandiver, this is the salty scum consisting of sulfates and chlorides that rises to the top of the melting pot during glass production.</note></term> and cast it aside as useless for glassmaking. It has, however, been changed into a salt by this mixing; therefore shepherds offer it to their flocks to lick, especially in those places where salt is sold at a higher price. This substance, in a humid climate, easily dissolves into a liquid, and the flint powder then falls away of its own accord. This liquid is coagulated by heat into true **common salt**, which is proven by its granulation and subsequent distillation. But the dregs, or the sediment, are a remedy not to be despised for <term>urinary difficulties<note>original: "vrinae difficultatibus." This refers to conditions like kidney stones or painful urination, which 17th-century chemists believed could be dissolved by specific mineral salts.</note></term>, just like flint that has been ignited and then quenched in water.

However, this water <note>Likely referring to a solution of "water glass" or the liquid described above.</note> was sometimes administered in vain, especially when the flint glows less intensely with its own heat and that diuretic power is lost; it is lost, moreover, when the heat of the fire **alkalizes** the flint—that is, when both faculties <note>The acidic principle of the flint and the alkaline principle of the salt.</note> unite into one—for then it does not reach the urinary tracts by reason of its <term>fixity<note>original: "fixitatis." In early chemistry, "fixity" describes the state of a substance that has become resistant to the action of fire and will no longer evaporate or react easily.</note></term>; indeed, and other things that stimulate urine at that time...

### TACHENIUS

...I will vindicate him who taught the **calcination** <note>original: "ustionem," the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its chemical properties.</note> of glass correctly, showing that he judged rightly; and I will defend him from the slanders with which the Reformer has bespattered him, and especially against this claim:
> *That glass prepared in this way cannot be administered to the sick without danger or harm.*

<margin>
a. From whatever a glass has its composition, it must be resolved back into that same thing.
</margin>
Take, therefore, clear, transparent glass—that which is most similar to glass (a)—and with **liquated alkali** <note>A liquid form of an alkaline salt, like potash or soda.</note>, expose it afterwards to humid air. You will soon find the whole glass resolved into water. Onto this, pour a sufficient amount of **acid**, which is the opposite of **Alkali**, and a **powder of flints** <note>original: "silicum pulvis." This refers to silica. Tachenius is describing a chemical precipitation: glass dissolved in alkali creates "water glass" (sodium silicate), which then returns to solid silica when neutralized by acid.</note> falls to the bottom. This powder is no longer glass, but powder of flints, which the Reformer abhors and asserts cannot be administered to the sick without harm; he is forgetful that he himself recommends powder of flints (b)

<margin>
b. In the appendix of the Royal Pharmacopoeia, page 136.
</margin>
to **Queens** for the purpose of generating milk <note>Tachenius is pointing out a contradiction: the Reformer claims this powder is "poisonous" glass when Tachenius makes it, yet the Reformer's own textbook recommends it as a remedy for nursing noblewomen.</note>.

The ancients did not have the art of separation <note>Chemistry or alchemy.</note> divinely granted to mortals; nevertheless, they recognized this through reasoning. Therefore, they began to **quench** <note>The rapid cooling of a hot substance in liquid.</note> the fine dust of glass in the ashes of **bean stalks**, the salt of which is derived from **acid**.

### HIPPOCRATES. 23

Ru.

<margin>A meddlesome **reformer** who, while keeping an eye on the hearts of the ancients, meanwhile does not see his own errors.</margin>
...false regarding **acid**, as I will soon show. They repeated the **quenching** <note>original: "extinctionem," the process of cooling a hot substance rapidly in liquid to make it brittle or change its chemical state.</note>, always with new ash, and they washed the powder of glass with water, so that every quenching was a destruction of the glass or the **Alkali** <note>Tachenius is describing a process where the alkaline salts that help make glass are washed away, leaving only the silica.</note>. They washed it and quenched it again until the **ash of beans** <note>original: "fabarum cinis." Ash from bean stalks was historically used as a source of potash in early chemistry.</note> had destroyed all the alkali and left behind an impalpable powder. This powder consists of **flints** <note>silica</note>, just like that which was melted above with alkali and **precipitated** with acid—the very same which the **reformer** thinks cannot safely be administered by mouth to the common people, yet, as I mentioned, he promises it will generate milk in **Queens**.

<margin>(a) The true preparation and knowledge of **simples** <note>Basic medicinal ingredients, usually derived from a single plant or mineral.</note> almost requires the lifetime of a whole man.</margin>
In this preparation (a), which the reformer, out of laziness and ignorance, calls **flimsy** <note>original: "leuidensem," literally thin-threaded or slight; used here as a derogatory term for a superficial or weak chemical process.</note>, there lies a deep knowledge of natural things, as will gradually become clear. Its consumption is in no way to be feared, despite the reformer's false teachings. Indeed, it is necessary for those who are profoundly ignorant to fear many things.

<summary>Tachenius explains the chemical process of reducing glass to a fine silica powder using bean ash and water, arguing that this "powder of flints" is safe for medicinal use despite the Reformer’s contradictory warnings.</summary>

<keywords>alkali, silica, Jean Zwelfer, chemical precipitation, bean ash, medicinal preparation, quenching, glass-making</keywords>

### Chapter 6.

There is also a method circulating for drawing out a **diuretic** power from **calcined flint** <note>Silica that has been heated to high temperatures until it becomes a crumbly powder.</note> through the **destruction of alkali**, that is, by the rule of the alkali's contrary, using **acidic spirits**, in this manner:

Into **spirit of salt** <term>Spirit of salt: An early name for hydrochloric acid.</term>, they cast **quicklime** <term>Quicklime: Calcium oxide, a caustic mineral obtained by heating limestone.</term> piece by piece; the acid is immediately absorbed by the **alkali** of the lime with great force and a violent boiling <note>original: "ebullitione." This refers to the intense chemical reaction that occurs when acid and base meet.</note>. They draw off the tasteless **phlegm** <note>In early chemistry, "phlegm" referred to the watery, non-active part of a substance produced during distillation or reaction.</note>, and they melt the lime, now saturated with the acidic salt, in a **crucible** over a light fire, and strain it into a cold mortar. Once cooled, they pound it into a powder—at which time it scatters **fiery sparks**—and from this, using hot water, they extract a saltiness. They purify and **coagulate** <note>To thicken or solidify a liquid into a solid salt.</note> this, and then allow it to dissolve of its own accord into a salty liquor, which is not without use. 

However, there are certain **sophists** and **impostors** who promise that this liquor can be distilled through an **alembic** <term>Alembic: A classic distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube.</term>; they undoubtedly mix...

<summary>Tachenius describes a chemical procedure for creating a medicinal "salty liquor" by reacting hydrochloric acid with quicklime. He concludes by warning the reader against fraudulent chemists who claim this mineral-based substance can be easily distilled.</summary>

# ->HIPPOCRATES.<-

<margin>B</margin>

...the said liquor with calcined and powdered **flints**, in a fourfold proportion, and they distill it over an open fire into an **acidic spirit**; this they call, by a perverse name, **Basil’s spirit of lime** <note>original: "spiritum calcis Basilij." This refers to Basil Valentine, a legendary and semi-mythical alchemist whose recipes were highly influential but often criticized by Tachenius for being chemically misunderstood.</note>.

Now let us see whose "son" this so-called distilled spirit actually is <note>Tachenius uses the metaphor of parentage to discuss chemical origins and compositions.</note>. First, the **spirit of salt** <term>Spirit of salt: An early name for hydrochloric acid.</term> was absorbed by the **vitrifying alkali** <note>The alkaline component used in glass-making to lower the melting point of silica.</note> in a most steady fire.

Next, the acidic part of the flint was separated by water and discarded under the mask of **dregs** <note>original: "fæcum larua." Tachenius suggests the useful part of the stone is being thrown away as waste because the chemists do not understand the reaction.</note>.

Third, the **fixed alkali** of the flint or the lime "drinks up" the acidic spirit of salt, and a salt is formed from the **volatile acid** and the very **fixed alkali**.

Fourth, they mix this salt with calcined flints—that is, with **quicklime**—which is richer in alkali than in acid, as I have demonstrated in the cases of **glass** and **soap**.

And even if this mixture were pressed by the strongest fire, what else could trickle out but a **volatile acid**? That is to say: it is the very same **spirit of salt** which the alkali had previously absorbed.

This spirit, famous men...

**TACHENIUS**

...and learned men recommended it for **gout** <term>Gout: A painful form of arthritis often associated with "rich" diets in the early modern period.</term> and **lithiasis** <term>Lithiasis: The formation of stony concretions, such as kidney or bladder stones.</term>, as if it were something extraordinary; yet in practice, the result was completely vanishing. But **Basil** <note>Referring to the legendary alchemist Basil Valentine.</note>, having been misunderstood, did not write for the benefit of impostors; for that reason, he is unfairly proclaimed a forger by them. Thus, the comic poet <note>A reference to the Roman playwright Terence in his play *Eunuchus*.</note> rightly advises: **"If you are wise, what you know, you do not know."** <note>original: "si sapis, quod scis nescis." ->I add to this examination.<-

<margin>Nature has nothing hidden in this **sublunary globe** <note>The "sublunary globe" refers to the Earth, which in ancient and medieval philosophy was the realm of change and decay located below the sphere of the moon.</note> regarding medicine that will not be made manifest to a follower of Hippocrates through this method.</margin>
By this method, any process—whether **chemical** or **medical**—can be proven through intellect and reasoning, allowing one to discern the true from the false without any expense or loss of time. Once you have been imbued with this **Hippocratic doctrine**, which I deliver here in a brief compendium, you shall no longer be circumvented by impostors, sophists, and deceivers. Indeed, by this method, you will be able to progress without error along the right path of this natural, true, and most ancient Hippocratic philosophy, both in your practical work and in writing books across all **three kingdoms**—namely: the **animal**, **mineral**, and **vegetable**.

## Chapter 7.

For greater light and understanding, it will be worth the effort to demonstrate to the eye the difference between **alkalis** <term>Alkali: In 17th-century chemistry, this referred to substances (like wood ashes) that react with acids; Tachenius was a pioneer in the theory that all things are composed of "acid" and "alkali."</term>, and what is meant by "contrariety," or the absorption and soaking up of acids, before I proceed further.

<margin>
"Things heard irritate the mind more slowly than those subjected to the faithful eyes." <note>A famous quote from the Roman poet Horace's *Ars Poetica*, suggesting that visual demonstrations are more convincing than words.</note>
</margin>

There are, however, as I have said, various degrees and species of **fixed salt of alkali** <term>Fixed salt of alkali: The solid residue left after burning plant matter and washing the ashes; primarily potassium carbonate.</term>, such as that of **tartar** <term>Tartar: A salty crust found in wine barrels, highly valued by alchemists for its purity.</term>—which has a unique privilege—linden, hazel, all kinds of oaks, and those which are burned into alkalized ashes from green wood in the wildernesses of **Norway**, **Lithuania**, and **Muscovy** <note>Muscovy: An early modern name for the Grand Duchy of Moscow or Russia.</note>. Likewise, all hot and oily herbs, such as rosemary, lavender, rue, etc. All of these, in a single word, are contrary to acids and are destructive of them; the following mechanical demonstration shows their difference.

<margin>
a Hippocrates: Book 1, *On Regimen*
</margin>

Dissolve half an ounce of **mercury sublimate** <term>Mercury sublimate: Also known as corrosive sublimate (mercuric chloride), a highly toxic and reactive compound.</term> (which has been changed from an acid and an alkali into a salt (a) by mixing) in water...

...of common distilled [water], fifty ounces; into a portion of this clear solution, drop by drop, but in rapid succession, drip **fixed alkali of tartar** <term>Fixed alkali of tartar: A salt (potassium carbonate) obtained by burning the crusty deposits found in wine barrels. Tachenius considered it one of the purest alkalis.</term> (this is the liquid of tartar spontaneously dissolved) <note>original: "liquor tartari sponte resolutus". This refers to "oil of tartar per deliquium," a process where the dry salt absorbs enough moisture from the air to turn into a concentrated liquid.</note>, which in an instant divides the compound, absorbs the **acid** <term>Acid: In 17th-century chemistry, the sharp, reactive principle that "combats" with alkali.</term>, and the **Mercury** <term>Mercury: Here, mercury has been dissolved in an acid (likely nitric acid). When the alkali is added, the mercury "precipitates" or falls out of the solution as a solid.</term> immediately seeks the bottom, appearing a dark red.

<margin>
The wonderful difference of **fixed alkalis**.
</margin>

From the **calcined** <term>Calcined: Reduced to a powder through high heat or burning.</term> dregs of vinegar, alkali of tartar is drawn out with water; and although it is produced from the same vine as the wine, it nevertheless precipitates the Mercury as a glowing and shining red.

When, however, the same alkali has been calcined by art to a state of redness, then the same Mercury falls like crushed **cinnabar** <term>Cinnabar: A bright scarlet mineral (mercury sulfide). Tachenius uses it here to describe the specific shade of red produced in the reaction.</term>.

If you add **lime** <term>Lime: Calcium oxide, produced by burning limestone. It was known to make alkalis more "caustic" or aggressive.</term> to burnt tartar and extract the alkali with simple water, then the falling Mercury glows.

[Alkali] extracted from the **herb Kali** <term>Herb Kali: *Salsola kali* or saltwort. The ashes of this plant were used to produce soda ash, which Tachenius notes behaves differently than the alkali from wine tartar.</term> precipitates Mercury much darker than the former.

The **lye** <term>Lye: A strong alkaline solution used for cleaning or soap-making.</term>, which soap-makers call the **master lye** <note>original: "magistram". This refers to the most concentrated and potent lye in the soap-making process, usually strengthened with lime.</note>, which consists of lime and the alkali of oaks, gives a dark yellowish Mercury.

...is] <note>original: "sum", the conclusion of a word from the previous page</note> Mercury thus dissolved; it makes the solution cloudy indeed, but does not cause it to precipitate. However, when a certain **fixed and dried alkali** <term>Fixed alkali: A stable alkaline substance that does not evaporate easily, in this case "alkali of tartar" or potassium carbonate.</term> (of **tartar**) is added to this powdered mixture before it deflagrates <note>burns away rapidly</note>, and it is held over a gentle fire, then the **sulfur** <term>Sulfur: Here referring to the combustible chemical element.</term> first melts. The oily acid of the sulfur, preparing itself for flight <note>evaporation</note>, is greedily "drunk up" by the oily alkali, just as happens in the preparation of **milk of sulfur** <note>original: "lactis sulphuris"; a precipitate of sulfur used in medicine</note> via the **wet way** <note>original: "humida via"; chemical reactions performed in liquid solution rather than by dry heat.</note>.

While the heat continues, the **nitre** <term>Nitre: Also known as saltpeter (potassium nitrate), a key ingredient in gunpowder.</term> is also melted, whose sulfurous and inflammable part associates with its own kind—namely, with the oiliness of the sulfur. Indeed, it is greedily received by the alkali. Half of this alkali, in proportion to the other ingredients, is sufficient, so that:

<margin>(a) Fulminating salt.</margin>
(a) **Nitre**, three parts;
**Alkali of tartar**, two parts;
**Sulfur**, one part.

And so it becomes a salt. In this salty mass, both "sulfurs" <note>Referring to the added sulfur and the combustible principle Tachenius believed resided within the nitre.</note> boil; as the fire persists and is increased, they catch flame. Upon ignition, this flame cannot endure the narrow space within the salt, and so, with a **thunderous crash** <note>original: "fulmineo fragore"</note>, they burst forth into the air.

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s argument that the chemical behavior of salts depends on their state. TACHENIUS
</header>

**broom**, **St. John’s wort**, **rupturewort**, **nettles**, etc.—all of these, I say, are of an **acid-salty** <note>original: "acido-falsi"; a term Tachenius uses to describe substances that share properties of both acids and salts.</note> nature, and they do not precipitate **Mercury** from the stated solution; however, when they are **alkalized** <note>Transformed into an alkali, usually through high-heat burning or melting.</note> by a sharp flame or by fusion, then it [the precipitate] falls down reddish.

From this it is clear that the most learned of the ancients, who ordered that glowing glass be extinguished in the ashes of **bean-stalks**, and not in its salt, correctly established the burning of the glass: so that, clearly, the salt would not imprudently take on the nature of an alkali from an unskilled refiner, as I mentioned above in Chapter 5.

The salts of the ashes from the class of **vulnerary** <term>Vulnerary: Herbs used for healing wounds.</term> plants, such as **wood betony**, **agrimony**, **wintergreen**, **speedwell**, **stitchwort**, **lady’s mantle**, etc.—all produce a clearly salty salt. For this reason, they are unsuitable for **vitrification** <note>The process of turning a substance into glass.</note>, and they do not precipitate Mercury (which is of the same "taste") as will be discussed in its proper place. However, all the mentioned salts change their nature through the fire of fusion; they become alkalis, they can be vitrified, and they precipitate Mercury as a reddish-orange color. Hence the **Zohar** <note>original: "Zoar"; Tachenius refers to the foundational work of Jewish Kabbalah.</note>, in its commentary on **Exodus**, notes,

<meta>catchword: that [quod]</meta>

...because glass is prepared from any ash: that is, when they are reduced to an **alkali**.

<margin>The constancy of salt. Of it, it can be said: *You have tested me through fire*, etc. <note>original: "probasti me per ignem"; an allusion to Psalm 66:10, used here to illustrate the chemical stability of common salt under heat.</note></margin>

**Common salt** <note>original: "Sal commune"</note> does not precipitate **Mercury** from the stated solution; and even if it were burned for a long time and with a swift flame, it would never become an alkali, just as it does not change its nature under **digestion** <note>In alchemy and early chemistry, digestion refers to the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat, often for weeks at a time.</note>. It is, therefore, the most perfect of all salts, and it was called the *highest of salts* <note>original: "salium summus"</note>—not without a singular distinction—by **Helmont** <note>Jean Baptiste van Helmont (1580–1644), an influential chemist and physician whom Tachenius frequently cites.</note>.

Likewise, the salt from **vipers** does not precipitate Mercury, unless the acid and alkali are once again separated from one another, concerning which [more is written] below.

<margin>a. [See] page 788 of the *Pharmacopoeia*.</margin>

**Salt of wormwood** <note>original: "Sal absinthij"</note> is "salty-from-acid" <note>original: "ex acido salsus"</note>; for that reason, it does not precipitate Mercury, nor does it absorb **spirit of vitriol** <term>Spirit of vitriol: A historical name for sulfuric acid, usually obtained by distilling green vitriol (iron sulfate).</term>, as the Reformer [Helmont] (a) teaches under the title *Coagulated Spirit of Vitriol*. However, when it is **alkalized**, it precipitates Mercury as a yellow powder, and then it also absorbs the acid spirit of vitriol and **vitrifies** <note>The process of turning a substance into glass through heat.</note> it.

From this it is learned: by a fire stronger than the nature of the thing allows, it is fixed and turned into a neutral state. Thus they become
