### Chapter 8.

All minerals, of whatever kind they may be, which carry hidden metallic bodies within them, are associated with acids (namely, various types of sulfur <note>In early chemistry, "sulfur" often referred to the flammable or acidic principle within a substance, rather than just the element sulfur.</note>) which prevent the metal from being separated from the impurities of the mines. In this difficult state, it is necessary to turn to alkalis, which provide quick assistance.

<margin>The precipitation <note>original: "praecipitatio"</note> of metal by alkali.</margin>
Therefore, they reduce the mineral to a powder, mix it with an alkali, and melt it in a crucible <note>A ceramic or graphite container used for melting metals at very high temperatures.</note>; they accelerate the fusion with nitre <note>Saltpeter (potassium nitrate), used as a flux to speed up the melting process.</note>, which is ignited with the sulfur so that part of it burns away, while another part, with the tartar <note>A byproduct of winemaking (potassium bitartrate) that, when heated with nitre, creates a powerful alkaline flux for smelting.</note>, is turned into an alkali. This alkali absorbs every acid with which the metal would otherwise escape, but by this way...

## The Privileged Alkali of Tartar.

### Chapter 9.

<margin>The special status of Salt of Tartar.</margin>
I have said that among the more **fixed alkalis** <term>Fixed Alkali: A chemical substance that remains stable and does not vaporize when exposed to high heat. Salt of Tartar (potassium carbonate) was considered the "king" of these substances.</term>, **salt of tartar** <note>original: "sal tartari"; a salt produced by burning the crusty deposits (tartar) found in wine barrels.</note> enjoys a unique privilege. Even though it is a heavy, earthy, and impure body, defiled by dross, nevertheless, when it is **digested** <term>Digestion: An alchemical process where a substance is subjected to gentle, consistent heat over a long period to encourage chemical transformation.</term> with any metal for a sufficient length of time, <margin>a. Gold is almost entirely sulfur; it yields the least amount of mercury to all things.</margin> it converts that metal into **running mercury** <note>original: "Mercurium currentem"; liquid quicksilver or the essential metallic soul.</note>. Having experienced this myself, I affirm it as the truth to the incredulous; if there are any who truly refuse to believe it, I am not at all concerned.

A certain friend of mine, seeking tartar from a noble, mature, and clear wine, drew off the **spirit** <note>The volatile, alcoholic essence of the wine.</note> and allowed the **phlegm** <note>The watery, inactive portion of the distillate.</note> to evaporate. The thick residue, which remained like a syrup, he allowed to congeal in the cold into a transparent tartar. This he then burned and, according to the custom, extracted the **alkali** using common water. He then allowed this alkali to spontaneously dissolve into a liquid <note>This refers to deliquescence, where the salt absorbs enough moisture from the air to turn into a concentrated liquid solution.</note>. An opportunity then presented itself for him to go, quite unexpectedly, to Rome

<meta>This page continues the narrative of an accidental alchemical discovery. The previous page described the preparation of a tartar-based liquid. This page recounts how a servant's error led to the unexpected production of mercury from silver.</meta>

->HIPPOCRATES<-

<margin>Mercury from silver.</margin>
...he was obliged to depart with a man of great reputation. So that the precious liquid would not be lost in the meantime, he ordered his servant to keep the liquid in a specific vessel that he pointed out. By mistake, the servant poured it into a different nearby vessel, similar to the first one. This vessel contained silver dissolved in **aqua fortis** <term>Aqua Fortis: Literally "strong water," this is nitric acid, a powerful solvent used by alchemists to dissolve metals like silver.</term>, to which had been added sublimated **sal ammoniac** <term>Sal Ammoniac: Ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to aid in the sublimation and dissolution of metals.</term> dissolved in distilled vinegar, which had been prepared for a different project.

The friend returned after nine months and asked for the liquid of tartar, but the **cucurbit** <term>Cucurbit: A glass or ceramic vessel shaped like a gourd, used as the lower part of a distillation apparatus.</term> assigned to the servant was empty. The servant then showed him what he had poured into the neighboring vessel. However, instead of liquid, he found only dry earth (having dried out over the nine months). When he touched this earth with a small rod, a large amount of **running mercury** <note>original: "Mercurij currentis"; this refers to liquid quicksilver, often considered the "soul" or "primordial matter" of metals.</note> revealed itself.

At that time, he did not believe that running mercury could be produced from **perfect metals** <note>"Perfect metals" refers to gold and silver, which were traditionally thought to be immutable and impossible to "destroy" or break down into simpler components like mercury.</note>. Thus, being convinced by the axiom that it is easier to construct perfect metals than to destroy them, he neglected the experiment and persuaded himself that the mercury had simply fallen into the vessel by some accident.

### TACHENIUS

Finally, he learned from Basilius Valentinus <note>Basilius Valentinus: A legendary 15th-century alchemist, likely a pseudonym, whose writings on antimony and salts were highly influential in the 17th century.</note> that the **salt of tartar** <term>Salt of Tartar: Potassium carbonate, a white salt obtained by heating the "tartar" or sediment found in wine barrels. Alchemists valued it as a powerful alkali.</term> possessed this special prerogative above all others.

<margin>Treatise: *The Power of Medicines*, section 40.</margin>
The ingenious Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644): A pioneering chemist and physician who bridged the gap between alchemy and modern chemistry.</note> confirmed this experiment in these words: *“I perceived,”* he said, *“that the crudity of **Saturn** <term>Saturn: The alchemical name for lead, associated with the slow-moving planet Saturn.</term> is soluble by the fatness of fixed salts; thus the parts of the compound are divided, so that it permits the crude silver to run.”* <note>original: "argentum currere"; in this context, "running silver" refers to the production of liquid mercury (quicksilver) from a solid metallic state.</note>

Then it came to my memory: a strong lye, with which old women cook **litharge** <term>Litharge: Lead(II) oxide, a byproduct of refining silver from lead ore, often used in ancient cosmetics and hair dyes.</term> to darken hair (a recipe which Porta also includes in his *Natural Magic* <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615): An Italian scholar whose book *Magia Naturalis* was a famous compendium of scientific and occult wonders.</note>), into which I dripped **spirit of vitriol** <term>Spirit of Vitriol: An old name for sulfuric acid, a highly corrosive mineral acid.</term>. Immediately, the **alkali** <term>Alkali: A substance that neutralizes acids; in this context, the salt of tartar or the lye.</term> allowed the metal to fall into a white powder, which I reduced back into lead. Thus I knew for certain that alkalis hide calcined <note>Calcined: Reduced to a powder or "ash" through the application of intense heat.</note> metals invisibly within their "belly."

Having obtained this experiment, Helmont continues: *“The sharpest calcined substances,”* he said, *“are sweetened with dissolved **sal ammoniac** <term>Sal Ammoniac: Ammonium chloride, a salt used to help dissolve metals and promote chemical reactions.</term> and putrefied tartar; that is, they are turned into **running mercury**.”* <note>original: "Mercurium currentem"; liquid quicksilver, which alchemists believed was the fundamental component of all metals.</note>

# HIPPOCRATES.
## 45

...spirit, which is no longer sharp or biting like a calcined <term>Calcination: The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical or chemical properties, often reducing it to a powder.</term> substance, but tasteless to the tongue.

Now I return to the **salt of tartar** <term>Salt of Tartar: Potassium carbonate, an alkaline salt obtained from the crusty sediment in wine barrels. It was a foundational reagent for early chemists.</term>, which was poured in liquid form into a **cucurbit** <term>Cucurbit: A gourd-shaped glass vessel that forms the lower part of a distillation apparatus.</term>, in which there was silver corroded by **aqua fortis** <term>Aqua Fortis: Literally "strong water," the alchemical name for nitric acid, famous for its ability to dissolve silver.</term> with **sal ammoniac** <term>Sal Ammoniac: Ammonium chloride, a pungent salt used as a powerful solvent in metallurgy.</term>. 
<margin>From the book *On the Great Miracle of the World*.</margin> 
All these things changed their nature into neutral salts, different from their prior state; thus the silver no longer reflected the appearance of silver, about which Basilius (a) <note>Basilius Valentinus: A semi-mythical 15th-century alchemist whose writings were highly influential in the development of 17th-century chemistry.</note> says regarding tartar:

> ->*Through my spirit, they*<-
> ->*stretch themselves out*<-
> ->*and all metals entirely*<-
> ->*break apart*<-
<note>original German: "Durch meinen geist, das sie sich rechen und alle metalle gantz zerbrechen." In alchemical literature, "spirit" refers to the volatile essence of a substance that can penetrate and transform solid matter.</note>

For just as all things beneath the moon <note>Beneath the moon: A reference to the "sublunary" or earthly realm in ancient cosmology, which was believed to be subject to change and decay, unlike the perfect heavens.</note>—as experience witnesses—consist of two things, namely **ACID** and **ALKALI** (as I shall show mechanically in this writing), I wish this also to be understood regarding silver: by this silver, the alkali of tartar, through proper **digestion** <term>Digestion: The alchemical process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat, similar to how food is broken down in the stomach.</term> and cooking, has absorbed as much acidic **Sulphur** <term>Sulphur: In alchemical theory, "Sulphur" was not just the yellow element but a universal principle representing flammability, heat, and the "soul" of a metal.</term> as the mercury in the state of silver...

## *He shows by examples that alkaline bodies are empty.*

### Chapter 10.

<margin>
The vacuum in nature is demonstrated through the chemical art.
</margin>

**Salt of tartar** <term>Salt of Tartar: Potassium carbonate, typically derived from the charred residue of grape skins and seeds in wine barrels; it was a primary alkali in early chemistry.</term> is therefore the medium in which not only metals, but also minerals come back to life; even though, through the chemical art and strong fire, they may appear to be plainly destroyed.

<margin>
The regeneration of vitriol.
</margin>

Pour the **spirit of vitriol** <term>Spirit of vitriol: An early name for sulfuric acid.</term> drop by drop onto dissolved salt of tartar until the noise <note>original: "strepitus." This refers to the audible fizzing or effervescence produced by the reaction between the acid (spirit of vitriol) and the base (salt of tartar).</note> ceases; reduce the liquid into crystals, or dry it out completely, and you will find a salt having the nature of vitriol. It induces vomiting, and when it is consumed, it purges through the bowels, just as white vitriol is accustomed to do. If it is mixed with **nitre** <term>Nitre: Potassium nitrate, or saltpeter.</term> and **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: Mercury.</term>, it is sublimed into a red substance; if you add common salt to this, it becomes **corrosive sublimate** <term>Corrosive sublimate: Mercury(II) chloride, a highly reactive and toxic compound used in various chemical and medical applications.</term>. It can be distilled by itself just like vitriol. The whitish mucilage settling at the bottom is the same dregs <note>original: "fex." Literally "feces," used here to mean the sediment or precipitate.</note> which, in dissolved raw vitriol, falls down black upon the addition of salt of tartar. It remains constant in the fire, just like **colcothar** <term>Colcothar: The reddish-brown iron oxide residue left after the distillation of vitriol.</term> or

->TACHENIUS<-

...until it flowed down into a clear liquid. From a **retort** <term>retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</term>, placed in a **sand bath** <term>sand bath: a container of sand heated to provide even, controlled temperature for glass vessels</term>, I distilled all the moisture; and once the vessel was broken, I dissolved the salt remaining at the bottom in the open air. This was done upon an iron plate (which is not affected by the substance), which took about 40 hours. Nothing remained on the plate except a tiny bit of muddy **dregs** <term>dregs: the "faeces" or solid residue left over after a chemical process</term>.

<margin>
**Salt of tartar** is turned into simple **Elements**.
</margin>

I distilled the liquid again, increasing the fire as before, until nothing more—neither drips nor smoke—came out. Once more, I broke the retort and dissolved that same salt on the iron plate. I repeated these operations twelve times, and all the **salt of tartar** <term>salt of tartar: potassium carbonate, an alkaline salt derived from burnt wine lees</term> was converted into the element of water.

I collected the dregs left on the plate each time, and using **distilled water**, I extracted all the saltiness from them. I followed the same process with this as I had above, to such an extent that all the salt of tartar—just like vinegar, oil, and **burning water** <note>original: "aqua ardens," typically referring to alcohol or a flammable spirit</note>—was converted into tasteless water and **dead earth** <term>dead earth: "terra mortua," the inert, mineral residue remaining after all volatile or "vital" components have been removed</term> possessing no flavor or smell. Yet, shortly before this, that same earth and water had been a **caustic** <term>caustic: a substance capable of burning or corroding organic tissue</term>. Wondrous to say! Where has its fiery nature gone?

<summary>Tachenius describes a rigorous experiment where he repeatedly distills and dissolves salt of tartar until the caustic substance is reduced to its basic, inert elements of tasteless water and "dead earth." This demonstrates his theory that even the most aggressive chemical agents can be broken down into simple, neutral elemental components through persistent laboratory labor.</summary>

Likewise, those who dissolve the body of the soap and claim it benefits those with gout are in the greatest error. The flavor, which is found to be plainly false, teaches us what kind of fruit they can expect to harvest; for it does not even precipitate **mercury** from the aforementioned solution.

Therefore, the **alkali of tartar** seems in some way to represent a type...

It acts in almost the same way that the fixed alkali of herbs <term>alkali: in 17th-century chemistry, a salt derived from the ashes of plants, believed to possess specific medicinal and transformative powers</term> extracts from calcined <note>calcined: reduced to a powder through the application of high heat</note> flint another similar alkali. Indeed, it is impossible to draw out that substance by any other quality than through its own likeness. It makes the substance much more potent and truly converts it into a fiery nature. Thus, progress must be made from the known to the unknown, as *Aristotle* rightly taught.

## *Now concerning the Volatile Alkali of Animals.*

### Chapter II.

<margin>a. What a ferment is, I shall show a little later.</margin>
Just as fixed alkalis absorb all acids and change them into a salt <note>original: *falsum*, likely a printer's error for *salsum* (salt), as Tachenius's central thesis is that acid and alkali combine to form salt</note> according to the nature of the acid, as I have shown above, so also the volatile alkalis of all animals—extracted from whatever parts they may be—are also turned into salt, imitating the property of the acid. And just as urine contains volatile alkali through the ferment (a) of putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the chemical process of decay which early chemists believed "unlocked" the volatile salts within organic matter</term>, so too do sweat, blood, flesh, bones, and the tissues of all animals through various ferments, each according to the power innate to its location. And just as [I demonstrated] an intrinsic difference regarding fixed alkalis when using dissolved mercury sublimate <term>mercury sublimate: mercuric chloride, used here as a chemical reagent to distinguish between different types of alkaline substances by the color of the precipitate it produces</term>, and a unique excellence in the salt of tartar; so also in these volatile [substances]...

[volatile] substances, I shall show their commonality in what follows.

<margin>
*Alkalis of all animals are similar, and are empty bodies.*
</margin>
Indeed, the alkali of all animals—whether from urine, blood, sweat (and even the "stones" <note>original: *calculorum*; refers to kidney or bladder stones, which early chemists analyzed to understand bodily chemistry</note> found in all animals), or from serpents, frogs, etc.—precipitates Mercury from the aforementioned solution with a white color. Thus, no difference can be discerned by this mechanical test. Furthermore, this alkali does not have a salty taste, as the "reformer" <note>likely a reference to a contemporary medical critic or a specific school of thought Tachenius opposed</note> teaches by mere opinion; for salty things, as I have demonstrated, do not precipitate Mercury from the said solution. Instead, the alkali takes on a form according to the specific property of the acid it meets, as will appear more clearly in what follows.

<margin>
*Hidden quality is the ferment of the place, about which [I will write] later.*
</margin>
The ancients noted this mummial <term>mummial: relating to "mumia," a vital force or "essence" extracted from organic matter; in Paracelsian chemistry, it refers to the specific curative power of a substance</term> or specific difference (which they call "hidden"). They observed, I say, that the volatile alkali drinks up only as much of the Cyrenaic salt <term>Cyrenaic salt: an ancient name for sal ammoniac (ammonium chloride), traditionally sourced from the region of Cyrene in Libya</term> as would suffice for its own stability and saturation, to create artificial sal ammoniac <margin>*Artificial sal ammoniac.*</margin>. This happens to such an extent that the alkali of urine and the acidic part of the said salt are so tightly embraced with each other, that [they cannot be separated] by themselves <meta>catchword: uor- [diuortantur / be separated]</meta>

...separation <note>original: *uortium*, likely the conclusion of *divortium*; referring to the chemical bond between substances</note> they do not suffer. For the **salt of urine**, when it attempts to flee during the **torture of fire** <note>a common alchemical term for high-heat distillation</note>, it snatches up the **acidic part** it has absorbed; together, they are sublimated into **artificial sal ammoniac**.

That **common salt**—whether our own or the **ammoniac of the ancients**—possesses an acidic part that can be transferred even without the use of fire is proven by this mechanical experiment:

<margin>Common salt is salty because of acid.</margin>
Indeed, drop **liquor of tartar** <term>liquor of tartar: a concentrated solution of potassium carbonate, used here as a strong alkali to displace other substances</term> into dissolved salt, and in a few moments you will see the **earthy part** separate; the **alkali of tartar** then embraces the **acid**, about which more will be said clearly later. Thus, without the art of distillation, the ancients attempted to combine **viperine alkali** with **natural sal ammoniac**.

Therefore, once the ancients noticed the hidden difference between **volatile alkalis** (derived from animals) and the acidity of **common** and **Cyrenaic salt** <term>Cyrenaic salt: an ancient term for ammonium chloride, named for the region of Cyrene</term>—observations they drew from **Hippocrates** (a) under the name of "faculties" <note>original: *facultatum*; referring to the inherent properties or "powers" of medicinal substances</note>—they began to **stratify** <note>the process of layering materials in a vessel for a chemical reaction</note> vipers with natural sal ammoniac or Cyrenaic salt, as **Pliny** says, above...

For there will never be a lack of opportunity—even after a thousand thousand years—to find and learn new and better things, for the reason that *the nature of things* <margin>a Seneca, book 7 of *Natural Questions*, chapter 31.</margin> (a) *does not hand over its sacred mysteries all at once: its secrets are not open to everyone indiscriminately; they are withdrawn and shut away in an inner sanctuary. From these, this age will receive one thing, and the age that follows after us will receive another.* <note>original: *rerum natura sacra sua, non simul tradat...* Tachenius quotes Seneca to emphasize that scientific discovery is a gradual process through history.</note> Listen also to my Hippocrates, who speaks much more modestly on this matter <margin>b Book 1 of *On Diet*.</margin> (b). *He says: None of them should be undeservedly blamed because they were unable to find [the truth]; on the contrary, they are all rather to be praised because they attempted to investigate.*

With these things understood, let us now see what sort of prerogative and excellence the **viperine salt** <term>sal viperinum: a salt distilled from the flesh of vipers, believed in 17th-century chemistry to have high medicinal value as a volatile alkali</term> of the reformer has over the inventions of the ancients; what they attempted to prepare by an easy operation, he [prepares] at great expense, with laborious toil, and with pompous and solemn ostentation and triumph.

<meta>This page continues the technical critique of the "Reformer" mentioned on page 61. While the previous page established a philosophical defense of ancient science using Seneca and Hippocrates, this page moves into a practical laboratory demonstration. Tachenius is arguing that the "Reformer's" supposedly secret medicinal salt is nothing more than a common chemical compound produced through unnecessarily complex means.</meta>

->TACHENIUS<-

<margin>
a From the Pharmacology, folio 486.
</margin>
<margin>
b The reformer's viperine salt is common salt.
</margin>
...under its own proper title (a) from the **retort** <term>retort: a glass vessel with a long, curving neck used for distilling substances</term>, it absorbs the **acid spirit of common salt** <term>spiritum salis communis acidum: an early name for hydrochloric acid</term>, which I have demonstrated—even without the art of distillation—is separable if an alkali is added. From that coupling of the salt’s acid and the **viperine alkali**, that very **common salt** (b) is produced. If you press this common salt with the heat of a **sand bath** <term>ignis arenae: a method of heating a glass vessel by burying it in sand to ensure a steady, even temperature</term>, it is raised up and becomes **artificial sal ammoniac** <term>sal armoniacum artificiale: ammonium chloride, a volatile salt used in both chemistry and medicine</term>, possessing all its properties, which is proven by this mechanical demonstration.

<margin>
Pure viperine alkali.
</margin>
Mix the powder of **salt of tartar** <term>sal tartari: potassium carbonate, a "fixed" alkali that does not evaporate easily, often derived from wine deposits</term> (that is, the fixed alkali of tartar) with an equal part of the reformer’s **viperine salt**. Distill this mixture through a retort placed in sand, and you will immediately see the alkali from the vipers ascend through the neck of the vessel. This occurs because the fixed alkali of tartar seizes the acid salt; at that point, the viperine alkali gladly leaves its acid companion behind, while it is forced by the heat to take flight <note>original: *in fugam abire*. Tachenius uses the metaphor of "flight" to describe evaporation or sublimation.</note>. Perform the same experiment with ordinary sal ammoniac, and you will find no difference. Thus, what the ancients [did] with the body of the salt, the reformer [does] with the spirit of the salt, though with much greater labor...

...[from the] **volatile [salt] of urine** and **distilled vinegar**—which two substances never solidify <note>original: "concrescunt"</note> together—a distillable, salty, and highly penetrating liquid is formed. I doubt whether there is anything in nature more subtle (resulting from a simple mixture) for treating ancient **obstructions** <term>oppilatione: a medical term for blockages in the body's vessels or organs</term>. For that reason, the most ingenious **Mindererus** <note>Raymond Minderer (c. 1570–1621), a military physician whose name is still attached to ammonium acetate solution</note>, rightly and with sincere judgment, praises and proposes this for hardness of hearing and difficulty in hearing. He does so because **sweat** is salty (as will be proven), and by virtue of **correspondence** <note>original: "symbolum"; a reference to the Hermetic principle that "like cures like" or that substances with similar properties interact</note>, this distilled salty and volatile water penetrates the membrane of the **eardrum** <note>original: "tympani"</note>; indeed, it is willingly absorbed. I have said that this **spirit** is salty, and it is proven by experience: for it does not **precipitate** <term>precipitat: to cause a solid to separate from a liquid solution</term> **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurium"</note> from the solution mentioned so often, just like other salty substances. According to **Galen's** <note>The influential 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories dominated medicine for 1,500 years</note> opinion, such substances cut through, thin out, and disperse waste matter, dry it out, gather strength, and invigorate. If a crude mass of salt can do this, why should this most subtle, salty spirit not do the same? Let the experts now judge: is that most learned Mindererus not unworthily slandered here by impostors?

<margin>
Without injury or pain, it penetrates the corneal membrane and dissolves the white spots left in the pupil by smallpox.
</margin>

<summary>Tachenius defends "Spirit of Mindererus" (ammonium acetate) as a powerful, penetrating medicine for ear blockages and eye spots, arguing that its salty, volatile nature allows it to work where other remedies fail.</summary>

## 66
### TACHENIUS

The Reformer <note>Tachenius's unnamed opponent, who advocated for a different system of chemistry</note> has misunderstood **Hippocrates** <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Medicine" (c. 460–370 BCE)</note>. He says: *A physician—*
<margin>
From the book of *Precepts*.
</margin>
(a) *who uses reason will never enviously slander another, for to do so betrays an inability to control one's own mind.* <note>original: "animi impotentiam"; suggesting that professional jealousy is a sign of a weak or unbridled character</note>

Let us now consider the **salt of tartar** <term>sal tartari: potassium carbonate, often used as a strong alkaline base in early chemistry</term>, which, by soaking up the acid, had separated from the **viperine alkali** <note>original: "alcali viperino"; a volatile salt often derived from vipers, but here referring to the alkaline component of the mixture</note> and remained in the retort. They call that residue (though unworthily) the **dead head** <term>caput mortuum: the solid remains left in a flask after all volatile components have been driven off by heat</term>, especially the residue from common **sal ammoniac** <note>original: "sale armoniaco"; ammonium chloride</note> from which the **spirit of urine** <note>ammonia solution</note> has been drawn out. This "dead head" is composed of acid and alkali and will undoubtedly have a salty taste, just like the Reformer's viperine salt, and like common sal ammoniac, as has been frequently stated and shown. I repeat here another mechanical experiment for the sake of confirmation.

Dissolve the aforementioned "dead head" in water; let the dregs sink to the bottom. Coagulate the clear liquid with gentle heat until it is dry, and mix it with **bolus** <term>bolo: a type of medicinal clay used here as a medium for heating</term> in a four-to-one proportion into an extremely fine powder. Force this, using a glass **retort** <term>retorta: a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distillation</term> coated with **lute** <note>original: "luto obduta"; 'lute' was a protective paste of clay or dung used to prevent glass from shattering in high heat</note>, over an open fire into a large

## 67
->HIPPOCRATES.<-

...the receiving vessel, and the **acid of salt** <term>acidum salis: spirit of salt or hydrochloric acid</term>, which found a body in the **alkali of tartar** <term>alcali tartari: potassium carbonate derived from burnt wine lees</term>, is forced to flee from the powerful heat; however, in a great storm of fire <note>a "storm of fire" refers to the intense, high-temperature heat applied during the final stages of distillation</note>, it cannot flee alone. Because it is absorbed by the fixed alkali, it carries with it the fixed **salt of tartar**; thus, from the mixed acid and alkali, pressed by violent fire, a **spirit** <term>spiritus: a volatile liquid produced by distillation</term> is elicited that is semi-acid-salty. Because this spirit is an artificial generation from a diverse mixture, it does not precipitate **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurium"; here referring to mercury in a dissolved solution</note> from the aforementioned solution. Nevertheless, it is not useless as a medicine on its own, and it is useful for destroying certain minerals. Once this single operation is completed, part of the tartar returns to the element of water, and the acidity of the **sal ammoniac** <term>sal armoniacum: ammonium chloride</term> from the other part of the tartar takes on a new body for itself, which is again sublimated and separated.

<margin>The spirit of **sal ammoniac** reassumes a body in the tartar.</margin>
The curious reader asks: why, in this instance, is a spirit elicited from the fixed alkali of tartar and the volatile acid, when above it did not succeed with the alkali of flints and the acid spirit of salt? To which I respond: the sharpness of the **salt of tartar**, in a fire that is not overly vehement, plainly...

### Chapter 12.

<margin>*I call "sweat" either that which transpires imperceptibly or that which washes away; it is all the same here for now.*</margin>
I said in the examination of **Mindererus’s water** <term>aqua Mindereri: a solution of ammonium acetate, traditionally used to induce sweating</term>, that sweat is made salty (by nature's first chemist <note>original: "protochimico"; Tachenius refers to the internal biological processes as a "chemist"</note>), just as every compound of acid and alkali is, not excluding urine; this mechanical demonstration proves it.

Take that wash-water <term>lixivium: a solution of salts or lye</term> in which dirty undershirts have been soaked and washed, but not boiled. Put this wash-water into a vial with a long, very narrow, and even neck; attach an **alembic** <term>alembic: the top part of a still used to collect condensed vapors</term>, even if loosely, and place it in a bath <note>balneum: likely a "balneum Mariae" or water bath</note> or sand-bath for digestion. Then, in a few days, you will see the alkali of the sweat ascend, though it will not be foul-smelling like that which...

# HIPPOCRATES.

...is sublimated from urine.

<margin>Pure alkali of sweat.</margin>
This alkali is not separated from the sweat unless its salty taste is divided; this taste consists of an acid and an alkali, both of which are volatile, as I have demonstrated regarding **Mindererus’s water** <term>aqua Mindereri: a solution of ammonium acetate used as a diaphoretic</term> and elsewhere. Therefore, the **lye** <term>lixivium: a solution of alkaline salts</term> is a fixed alkali; it absorbs the acidic portion of the sweat during the heat of digestion, and the fleeting alkali, once separated from the acid, seeks the high and cold place <note>i.e., it evaporates and condenses at the top of the distilling vessel</note>.

<margin>Salty and fatty sweat.</margin>
I observed that sweat is salty, volatile, fatty, and therefore penetrating and dissolving, while I was traveling with post-horses <note>original: "currilibus equis (postam vocant)"; referring to the system of changing horses at stations to travel quickly</note>. Although I protected my legs with boots sewn from the finest leather and smeared with wax—which allowed neither rain nor external water to pass through—the sweat of the horses, exhaling in the manner of a vapor, had nevertheless penetrated them. I noted that this also happened to my companions of high rank. To avoid this kind of inconvenience, I devised an ointment, like a **varnish** <term>vernix: a resinous coating</term>, which at other times resists **strong waters** <note>original: "aquis fortibus"; usually refers to nitric acid or other corrosive mineral acids</note>. Then, on a second journey, I certainly experienced less trouble...

70
### TACHENIUS

...I noticed in the first days; however, in the following days, the sweat not only penetrated the varnish, but clearly dissolved that which it had touched in the manner of a vapor. From this I learned that sweat must be naturally salty for this very reason: so that it might resolve <note>i.e., dissolve or break down</note> the grime in living bodies, which here and there take on a **curdled form** <note>original: "coagulum"; referring to the thickening of bodily oils and dirt into a solid layer on the skin</note>.

<margin>Discovery of viper’s salt:</margin>
How, moreover, the sweat of all animals—and whatever exhales and expires insensibly, indeed the very matter of which the animal consists—acquires this saltiness; I shall explain somewhat more clearly for the sake of **viper's salt** <term>sal viperinum: a volatile salt distilled from the flesh of vipers, used in 17th-century medicine to treat various ailments</term> alone. Its acidity, just as it is hidden, so its saltiness is all the more subtle and pleasing. I profess that I am the inventor of this salt, though the **envious may burst with rage** <note>original: "vt vt crepent ilia momo"; a classical idiom referring to Momus, the Greek god of mockery and stinging criticism</note>: as the following letter testifies. And although, for the sake of a little profit, many unskilled men—men ignorant and lazy regarding the instruments of nature and this **Hippocratic** <term>Hippocratic: relating to the ancient Greek physician Hippocrates; here Tachenius refers to his own "Hippocratic Chemistry," which sought to explain medicine through acid-alkali theory</term> doctrine—have sat in this theater <note>referring to the public stage of medical practice</note>, who, not without harm to my name, have foisted I-know-not-what upon the improvident and inexperienced: let the readers nonetheless know,

<margin>A Letter</margin>
*The confidence I previously held in your benevolence, I have now found to be true in actual practice: Wherefore, as soon as I had scarcely signaled my desire for your **viper’s salt** <term>sal viperinus: a medicinal volatile salt distilled from the flesh of vipers, used in 17th-century pharmacy for its supposed purifying properties</term>, you immediately satisfied it; for which I give the greatest possible thanks to your kindness. O, if by some stroke of luck it would please you to exchange the shallow **Hadria** <note>original: "vadosam Hadriam"; referring to the Adriatic Sea and by extension Venice, where Tachenius practiced</note> for most flourishing **Parthenope** <note>original: "florentissima Parthenope"; the ancient and poetic name for Naples</note>, I would certainly think it not without purpose nor without benefit to you. It also happens that the most excellent lady, whom you visited in Naples while she was suffering from failing vision <note>original: "caligantem"; literally "dimness" or "mistiness," referring to a darkening of the sight</note>, has since that day always longed for the treatment prescribed during your travels. Since indeed you have handed me a gift of the viper, I myself shall return a viperous gift: a volume, I mean, on the nature of the viper,*

-># HIPPOCRATES.<-

But why should I bring forth the testimonies of learned men here? This present writing sufficiently demonstrates that before me—to the "Reformer"—this salt with all its necessary requirements never even entered his mind, not even in a dream <note>The author is likely mocking a contemporary rival, possibly a Galenic doctor or a competing iatrochemist, by referring to him sarcastically as the "Reformator."</note>. And not only the Reformer, but not even those of a higher rank or society [will understand], whatever they may boastfully, ambitiously, and in glorious and vain speech proclaim.

<margin>a. Pure volatile alkali of blood precipitates mercury into a white color.</margin>
<margin>b. A more fixed salt of blood: medicinal.</margin>

Just as with urine and sweat, so too blood—while it is still warm—can be mixed with lye <term>lixivium: a solution of alkaline salts leached from wood ashes, used as a powerful reagent in early chemistry</term>, and from there a volatile alkali can be sublimated ( a ). However, when the same blood is distilled without lye, from a retort <term>retorta: a glass vessel with a long, curved neck used for distilling liquids</term> using a sand fire <note>original: "igne arenæ"; a sand bath provided the steady, indirect heat necessary to prevent the glass from cracking and the substance from burning</note>, it then leaves behind in the *caput mortuum* <term>caput mortuum: literally "dead head," the chemical term for the solid residue remaining after all volatile spirits have been driven off by heat</term> a great deal of salt that is somewhat fixed ( b ). The alkali precipitates Mercury from the solution mentioned so often into a white powder; the salt, however, does not do so at all.

*I have set down the preparation of volatile salt <term>sal volatilis: a salt that evaporates easily at room temperature, usually referring to ammonium carbonate in this period</term> according to Johann Wepfer <note>Johann Jakob Wepfer (1620–1695), a Swiss physician renowned for his pioneering work in toxicology and the study of the brain's blood vessels</note>; let me know how much you approve of it, and for the rest, love me even more fervently hereafter, for I honor you greatly. And I long for Naples for no other reason [than to see you], but what hope of that happiness do you offer me, or when? For that field of yours would not be an unfruitful crop. Fare right well. From Naples, on the Ides of May <note>May 15th</note>, in the year 1650.*

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius's defense of his chemical theories. Having previously discussed the preparation of viper's salt in a letter from a colleague, he now transitions into a polemic against a rival—specifically the "Reformer" (Joannes Zwelfer)—concerning the correct extraction of salts and alkalis from biological fluids like blood.</meta>

# HIPPOCRATES .

D

But why should I bring forth the testimonies of learned men here? The present writing shows clearly enough that before me, it never even occurred to the Reformer <note>original: "reformatori"; this refers to Joannes Zwelfer (1618–1668), a prominent physician and chemist who published the *Pharmacopoeia Augustana Reformata*. Tachenius and Zwelfer were famously bitter rivals.</note>, even in a dream, that this salt could exist with all its necessary requirements. I could demonstrate this through his own flawed letters, were I not sparing his honor; and unless he produces foundations of nature and art different from those he has used so far, he will never arrive at the knowledge of this salt. And this applies not only to the Reformer, but even to those of a higher rank or society—no matter how insolently, ambitiously, or with glorious and empty speech they may boast.

<margin>
a *The pure volatile alkali of blood precipitates mercury into a white powder.*
b *A more fixed salt of blood: medicinal.*
</margin>

Just as with urine and sweat, blood can be mixed with lye <term>lixivium: an alkaline solution, traditionally leached from wood ashes, used here to react with the blood</term> while it is still warm, and from that mixture an alkali can be sublimated <note>sublimation: the chemical process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collecting the deposit as it cools back into a solid; used here to isolate the volatile parts of the blood</note> ( a ). However, when the same blood is distilled without lye, from a retort <term>retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</term> in a sand bath <term>ignis arenae: a gentle heating method where the glass vessel is placed in a container of sand to distribute heat evenly</term>, it leaves behind a great deal of somewhat "fixed" salt in the "dead head" <term>caput mortuum: a term used by alchemists and early chemists for the useless solid residue remaining after distillation</term> ( b ). The alkali precipitates Mercury, from the solution I have so often named, into a white powder; however, the salt does not do so at all.

74 TACHENII

<margin>
However, it does not precipitate noble mercury. <note>original: "tamen nobile mercuriu non precipitat"</note>
</margin>
...indeed. From this it is clear that unless the acid from the blood is absorbed by the alkaline mercury <term>mercurius lixiviale: Tachenius's term for an active alkaline principle derived from lye</term> and the fixed alkali, the volatile alkali of the blood seizes its own acidity (which is also volatile); and under the governance of fire, they are fixed into a salt <note>original: "falsum," likely a printer's error for "sal" or "salsum" (salt)</note>, as will appear more clearly in the following.

But lest this new truth should birth new hatred toward me, I think it worthwhile, before I proceed further, to show that the foundations of this salt were not hidden from Hippocrates <note>Hippocrates (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) was an ancient Greek physician known as the "Father of Medicine." Tachenius frequently cites him to prove that his own chemical theories are actually ancient wisdom.</note>. For he says in those Golden Books *On Diet*: (a)

<margin>
a Book 1, Principle: One spirit draws, the other thrusts out, yet both do the same and move in both directions. Hippocrates, Book 1 *On Diet*.
</margin>
> Indeed, all other living things, and man himself, are constituted from two things; differing in power but harmonious and advantageous in use: **FIRE** and **WATER**. Both of these together are sufficient, both for all other things and for each other mutually; but each separately is sufficient neither for itself nor for any other. Thus he says.

<margin>
When I reduced [them] to natural principles, he then recognized... so says Avicenna <note>Avicenna (c. 980 – 1037) was a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age.</note>.
</margin>
These two powers, fire and water, my philosopher and Teacher brought forward, in whose bosom all those things are contain-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Hippocratic principles applied to chemistry. Tachenius is using Hippocrates's text "On Diet" to argue that digestion is a chemical process of "soft fire" and acidic transformation, rather than just simple heat.</meta>

HIPPOCRATES.

...whatever things are necessary to complete generation and transmutation. 

He adds a little later that this fire is not the same as a kitchen fire, speaking thus: *A man pounds grain, washes it, mills it, and when he has cooked it with fire, he uses it; and indeed it is not forged in the body by a strong fire, but by one that is soft and gentle.* <note>original: "Homo frumentum tundit... uerum molli ac lento." Tachenius is quoting Hippocrates to distinguish between external fire and the internal "innate heat" of the body.</note> What this "soft fire" is in all things will become clear as we proceed. (a)

<margin>a I will demonstrate the nature of those things which no one has yet attempted to explain. Hippocrates, ibid. <note>original: "ibid," referring to the previously cited book "On Diet."</note></margin>

->*Hidden acid becomes manifest.*<-

### Chapter 13.

<margin>b Fire (according to Hippocrates) is not sufficient unto itself: hence the appetite for nutrients; for they are Alkalies, as will be clear in what follows.</margin>

In a healthy stomach, there dwells a volatile acid (b), which is never lacking. By its benefit, all foods—which, without exception, are full of volatile alkali (as I shall show later)—are turned and transmuted into chyle <term>chyle: the milky fluid formed in the digestive system after food is processed</term> or juice. This juice first becomes acidic, and then gradually turns into saltiness <note>original: "falsum," which Tachenius uses to mean a salted or "mature" state, representing the balance of acid and alkali.</note>, that is, a mature state. Whether the stomach possesses this acid from itself or from elsewhere is not to be discussed here. Certain...

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s argument that digestion is essentially a chemical process of acidification. He transitions from discussing the theoretical "soft fire" of the body to providing concrete evidence—both from ancient medical authorities and daily experience—that acid is the primary agent of health in the stomach.</meta>

TACHENIUS

...nevertheless the writer indicates this sufficiently; it is enough to demonstrate the acidity to the eye; first by Authority, and then by experience.

<margin>a Aphorisms, Book 6: 1.</margin>
Hippocrates says (a) *In long-lasting looseness of the bowels, if an acid belch occurs which was not there before, it is a good sign.* <note>original: "in longis leuitatibus intestinorum si ructus acidus superueniat, qui prius non erat, bonum." Tachenius is citing a famous Hippocratic aphorism to prove that acid is a sign of returning digestive power.</note> This means: acidity has returned to the stomach, by which it transforms food into chyle <term>chyle: the milky fluid formed in the digestive system after food is processed</term>, or vital juice; then health will necessarily also return. Hence all seasonings, and the condiments of foods used to sharpen the appetite, are acidic, such as the juice of lemon <note>original: "citri," referring to the citron or lemon family</note>, pomegranate, orange, unripe grape juice <term>omphacium: the juice of unripe grapes, often called verjuice</term>, tartar <term>tartar: the acidic crust found in wine barrels, used as a chemical reagent and medicine</term>, vine-shoots, and the like; 
<margin>Acid juices differ among themselves.</margin>
however, all these things have great differences among themselves, which I will refrain from discussing now to avoid wordiness. See Chapter 10.

For the sake of restoring this lost acid during illnesses, the ancients established acidic syrups, sugar-vinegars <term>oxysachara: a medicinal syrup made from vinegar and sugar</term>, vinegar mixed with water, and similar things, by whose benefit a weak stomach might be restored, lest the nourishment; even if small...

### HIPPOCRATES.

...when [food is] consumed, it would putrefy from simple warmth alone; this is a fact noticed by few, especially those who convince themselves that putrefaction and chylification <term>chylification: the process of turning food into chyle, the milky fluid used for nutrition</term> are the same thing. I recall a certain man from the "new sect" <note>likely a reference to the prevailing Galenic school of medicine which Tachenius, as a chemist, often opposed</note>, who considers himself of no small importance, who—under penalty of death—prohibited lemon juice to a feverish patient who was strongly craving acidic things, as if the juice were something "raw." Yet, he prescribed a simple acidic syrup. I wondered why he allowed vinegar cooked with sugar as an alterative <term>alterative: a medicine intended to gradually restore healthy metabolic functions</term>, but would not allow lemon juice in food! He replied with a raised voice that the acidic julep <term>julep: a sweet medicinal drink, from the Persian word for rose-water</term> was made from water and sugar. 

My Hippocrates speaks of this type of doctor in 
<margin>a Book 1, *On Diet*</margin> 
(a): *If he does not understand the constitution from the beginning, and that which dominates in the body, he will not be able to offer those things that are beneficial to the person.* <note>original: "Si constitutionem ab initio non cognoscet, & id quod in corpore dominatur non poterit ea quæ homini conducunt offerre." Tachenius argues that the patient's craving for acid was a sign of what the body's "constitution" actually required.</note> However, the acid returns to the stomach—after being suppressed by a long illness—either spontaneously or by the skill of an ingenious physician.

So that we may recognize the aforementioned acid through experience...

### TACHENIUS

...if any healthy person drinks a draft of milk on an empty stomach and immediately vomits it up, then the tongue will perceive an acid. One will see the milk, upon its return, partially turned to cheese <note>original: "semi caseatum." This refers to the curdling process where the stomach acid separates the solids from the whey.</note>, as if vinegar had been sprinkled into it. (A nearly identical acid is found by the helping hand of nature, which—in a glass vessel and with only simple heat applied—transforms all food into a milky chyle <term>chyle: the milky fluid formed from food in the digestive tract during the first stage of digestion</term> from an acidic salt <note>original Latin: "ex acido salso." The OCR reads "falsum," but in Tachenius's chemical system, the "acid-salt" is the primary digestive agent.</note> within four or five hours. This is a delightful sight to see; I can demonstrate it to anyone who asks, though reason suggests it should not be described in detail here). By the help of this obscure yet most beneficial acid, the alkaline essence of the viper <note>Tachenius was famous for his "Viperine Salt." He believed the volatile alkali in vipers needed to be balanced by acid to be medicinal.</note> is gathered and restrained within its proper limits.

<margin>He who is ignorant of natural principles has no true root upon which to found his intention. — Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a famous alchemist whose works were foundational to European chemistry.</note></margin>

However, no flavor coagulates milk except for the acidic, of which there are three kinds.

First, in the healthy stomach <term>ventriculo: the stomach or primary digestive cavity</term> of all living animals, milk is not coagulated by a simple acid alone. Instead, it is necessary that the milk be impressed during coagulation with the "vital virtue" of the animal itself. This virtue, although it consists of an acid, nevertheless differs from any other common acid...

# HIPPOCRATES. 79

(a) and then it is turned into chyle <term>chyle: the milky fluid formed from food in the digestive tract during the first stage of digestion</term>, and is received as nourishment; and the same happens in a dog!
<margin>The vital instrument is acid; it is also the bond of the Ruler <note>original: "Rectoris." In the medical philosophy of Tachenius and Paracelsus, the 'Rector' or 'Archeus' is the internal spirit that governs the life-functions of an organ.</note> with the body. It suffices to have touched upon this here once and for all.</margin>
It takes on the nature of the dog (b) etc. However, it suffices to have pointed out this Hippocratic doctrine here, since this is not the proper place to discuss it in full.

Secondly, in the stomach of sick animals, milk indeed turns sour, but it is not impressed with the vital character <note>original: "vitali caractere." This means the acid in a sick person lacks the "life-force" necessary to transform food into healthy blood, leading to decay instead of nutrition.</note>; therefore, the acidity becomes putrefactive <term>putrefactive: causing rot or decomposition</term>, which does not nourish but increases the illness. Hence my Hippocrates (c)
<margin>Book 5, Aphorism 64. <note>Hippocrates' Aphorisms were the standard medical textbook for centuries; Book 5, 64 warns against giving milk to those with fever or certain pains.</note></margin>
prohibits the use of milk in various fevers (though not in all, as the common crowd of doctors would have it).

Thirdly, milk is coagulated <note>curdled or turned from liquid to solid</note> by obvious means, and by all acids, whether from the mineral class—such as acidic spring waters, vitriol <term>vitriol: a historical name for sulfate minerals, like iron or copper sulfate</term> in its solid form, or the spirits <note>In alchemy, "spirits" refers to concentrated acidic liquids obtained by distillation.</note> of vitriol [sulfuric acid], of sulfur, of salt [hydrochloric acid], or of nitre [nitric acid]; or it is coagulated by a single and solitary part of animals:
<margin>Various ways milk is curdled.</margin>
I say, the stomach of all creatures, and no other part. This serves as a sign that perceptible acid is not permitted in the other parts of the body, and if it should creep in by chance, it becomes a cause of disease and death. Therefore, acid dwells at least in healthy stomachs; and because this acid [curdles] milk...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 79 regarding the nature of acids in the digestive process. Tachenius is explaining how various substances, both internal to the body and external, cause milk and other fluids to coagulate or curdle.</meta>

# TACHENIUS

<margin>What is a curdling agent? <note>original: "coagulum quid sit?"</note></margin>
...generally it coagulates. By Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD) was a Greek physician and the author of "De Materia Medica," the primary source for herbal medicine for 1,500 years.</note> it is called a *coagulum*, and in the common tongue of Italy it is called ***conaglio*** <note>In modern Italian, "caglio" refers to rennet, the enzyme used to curdle milk for cheese.</note>. In the stomachs of all birds, they possess said acid so potent that unless they frequently eat small grains of sand or fragments of brick to restrain it, the over-abundant acid escapes from the stomach and disturbs the rest of the digestive processes. Consequently, they suffer from <term>atrophy: a wasting away of the body or its tissues due to lack of nutrition</term> and do not grow fat. It is also coagulated by the juices of vegetables, such as ***lemons, oranges, and barberries***, as well as by vinegar and whatever else breathes forth acidity.

<margin>Acid and alkali in milk.</margin>
Milk also turns sour of its own accord (just like the other juices of both plants and animals that tend toward <term>putrefaction: the process of decay or rotting</term>; indeed, nothing can rot, nor can anything new be generated, unless acidity precedes it). In that state, it also coagulates and the whey is separated. This whey carries a hidden <term>volatile alkali: in 17th-century chemistry, a substance (like ammonia) that reacts with acids and evaporates easily</term>, by reason of which it has a cooling effect, as will be shown in its proper place.

Both of these faculties—the acidic and the alkaline—lie hidden secretly in healthy milk; and therefore, a hidden salt also in...

<summary>Tachenius explains that birds must ingest grit to neutralize their potent digestive acids and describes how milk naturally separates into curds and whey through a hidden balance of acid and alkali.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of chemical reactions involving milk and acids, specifically moving from how acids curdle milk to how salts and minerals interact with it. Otto Tachenius (c. 1610–1699), the author, is using the example of milk to explain his broader "Acid-Alkali" theory of medicine and chemistry.</meta>

# HIPPOCRATES.

<margin>Salty things do not curdle milk. <note>original: "Salsa, lac non costringunt."</note></margin>
...they prepare with milk; this is the reason why salt of whatever kind it may be, whether natural or artificial, does no harm to milk. Indeed, even <term>sublimate of mercury: a chemical compound (mercury(II) chloride) produced by heating mercury with salts</term>—which is composed of the aforementioned mineral acids: namely <term>vitriol: a sulfate-based mineral acid</term>, <term>nitre: saltpeter or potassium nitrate</term>, and salt—does no damage to milk. This is because its acid is absorbed by <term>quicksilver: the metallic element mercury</term> acting as an alkali, and is thus transformed into an excellent saline substance. From this it is clear that salty things do indeed dissolve <term>mucilaginous: thick, gluey, or slimy substances</term> compounds, but they do not destroy harder, compact bodies, as we shall demonstrate more clearly as we proceed.

### Chapter 14.

<margin>Analysis of the stone. <note>original: "calculi analysis."</note></margin>

Take...

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the chemical analysis of a "calculus" (kidney or bladder stone), which the previous page began by introducing. Tachenius is demonstrating his theory that even the hardest pathological formations in the human body can be resolved into their basic alkali and acid components through the art of "vulcanian" (fire-based) chemistry.</meta>

# OF TACHENIUS
<meta>catchword: gris [likely beginning of "griseis" or similar in next page]</meta>

...a stone cut from the bladder, or one that has passed spontaneously from the kidneys; one or two <term>drachms: an ancient unit of weight, approximately 3.9 grams</term> is sufficient. Place these into a new and clean glass <term>retort: a spherical glass vessel with a long, downward-sloping neck used for distillation</term>. The stone is not to be ground into powder, but should be of such a size that it does not stick in the neck of the retort. Place the retort in <term>sand: a sand-bath used to distribute heat evenly</term>, to which you should attach a clean and new receiving vessel. Apply a moderate fire, and first an <term>alkali: a basic substance, which Tachenius believed was the fundamental opposing principle to acid</term> will distill into the receiver; it will look like urine, yet it will have no odor. Shortly after, you will see a more fixed part of that same alkali <term>sublimate: the process of a substance turning into vapor and then solidifying again on a cool surface</term> in the neck of the retort. Mix both "spirits"—the liquid and the solid—and carefully preserve them in a glass vessel sealed with wax; otherwise, if left open, the mixture would vanish into the air. Concerning this, Hippocrates <margin>Book 1 on Diet <note>original: "lib. 1. de diæta"</note></margin> says that when separated, it is sufficient neither for itself nor for any other.

In the cooled retort, you will find the fragments of the stone in the same shape as they were when they were first put in. However, they will be so light and <term>friable: easily crumbled or reduced to powder</term> that if you touch them even lightly with a finger, they will fall into dust—just like the corpses in <term>underground Rome: a reference to the ancient catacombs, where remains were famously said to turn to dust upon being disturbed</term>. With these fragments...

### HIPPOCRATES.

...ish <note>original: "griseis" (grayish), completing the word from the previous page</note>, if you pour back onto it the <term>alkali: a basic salt substance that Tachenius believed was one of the two pillars of all matter</term> reserved from the <term>calculus: a stone formed in the bladder or kidney</term>, or another alkali derived from urine, it immediately hardens just as it was before. This serves as a sign that the alkali finds a hidden <term>acid: the chemical opposite of alkali</term> in the said "corpse" <note>original: "cadavere"; in chemistry, this refers to the "caput mortuum" or the lifeless solid residue remaining after distillation</note> which absorbs it. For unless it finds an acid, it does not persist alone in the open air, but according to its nature it would vanish into the breezes; for when separated, it is sufficient neither for itself nor for any other, as Hippocrates <note>The ancient Greek physician (c. 460 – c. 370 BC), whom Tachenius cites to give his chemical theories medical authority</note> teaches:

<margin>
"I KNOW," says the author of the *Rosarium* <note>Referring to the *Rosarium Philosophorum* (The Rose Garden of the Philosophers), a famous 16th-century alchemical treatise</note>: "because by necessity, the work will arrive at such a nature."
</margin>

From this we learn that, surely, a stone is not congealed—neither in the bladder nor in the kidneys—except by the alkali of the urine, or the alkali of the organ itself, combined with an acid that has improperly, unseasonably, or by error descended from the stomach and there degenerated. When acid and alkali are joined there, they must of necessity (a) combine into a solid mass, as I shall demonstrate everywhere; and whatever they seize that is not far removed from their own nature, that substance undergoes a <term>coagulation: the process of a liquid becoming a solid or semi-solid mass</term> along with those two, just as I said regarding lime and as daily experience teaches. Hence, reddish stones are congealed from a small amount of blood, which in that place, by...

...rate. By means of <note>completing "demonstrate" from the previous page</note> acid, it flows down from an eroded vein; they are therefore more brittle. This is what my Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician of antiquity</note>, who was most skilled in chemistry, exclaims:

<margin>
(a) Book 1, *On Diet* <note>original: "de diæta"</note>.

*“These things happen thus by divine necessity, yet they know not what they do; each fulfills its destined fate.”* This means: both these spirits or faculties, joined together in any place, must of necessity grow together and congeal; but when separated! they accomplish nothing in nature, nor can they persist, being *“sufficient neither for themselves nor for another,”* says my Teacher. And so, manifest <term>acid: the chemical principle of sharpness and contraction</term> outside the stomach, in whatever place it may exist, is morbid and hateful to nature.

This doctrine of the old man is most true, for it is observed daily in medical practice that <term>calculi: stones formed within the body</term> grow together in all the empty spaces of the body wherever acid, by some error, has run together with <term>alkali: the chemical principle of saltiness and expansion</term>. This occurs in the lungs, in the liver, the <term>portal vein: the large vein through which blood passes from the digestive organs to the liver</term>, the ureters, the colon, and in the bile...

Therefore, the <term>calculus: a stone formed in the body</term> of all animals (which is the subject of our discussion here) has nothing in its composition except <term>acid: the sharp chemical principle</term> and <term>alkali: the salty chemical principle</term>—both of which are volatile—as well as blood or mucilage. From this, one learns how much value there can be in dangerous diseases for water poured over a **porcupine stone** <note>Also known as a "pedro del porco," this was a rare bezoar found in the gallbladder of porcupines, highly prized as a universal antidote in the 17th century</note> found in the gall of a porcupine? Yet those who are ignorant of the instruments of medicine extol this stone with the highest praises. It is a wonder, however, that they do not fear this stone as something *fiery*, since it is made bitter by the gall; a fact they have not noticed until now. Indeed, if the washing of this stone were of any benefit at all, then surely a single little drop of gall from any most cheap little fish would be much more excellent.

...perhaps would be more effective than the washings of twenty small stones.

From this chemical coagulation of the **calculus** <term>calculus: a stone or "pebble" formed within the body, such as a kidney or gallstone</term>, it now becomes clear what **stone-breaking medicines** <term>lithontribon: from the Greek, a medicine intended to crush or wear down stones</term>, **Spanish flies** <note>original: "cantharides"; these toxic beetles were used in small doses to stimulate the urinary tract</note>, the blood of a hare or a fox, and similar remedies have actually achieved in the treatment of **stone disease** <note>original: "lithiasi"</note> up until now.

Thus, Paracelsus <note>A Swiss physician and alchemist who revolutionized medicine by arguing that diseases were caused by chemical imbalances</note> rightly perceives that the stone (and also the nodules of **gout** <note>original: "nodosa podagra"</note>) is coagulated just like **tartar** <term>tartar: in the chemical medicine of this era, this refers to hard metabolic waste that crusts inside the body, named after the crust found in wine barrels</term> through two powers, according to the doctrine of Hippocrates: namely, by **acid** and **alkali**. It coagulates along with itself certain wastes found there by chance.

<margin>
(a) Galen, *The Method of Healing*, Book 7.
</margin>

And Galen (a) testifies against himself and his followers when he says that once the cause is known, it is easy to find the remedy. Therefore, whatever of **crab's stones** <note>calcium-rich "eyes" or stones found in the stomachs of molting crayfish</note>, **Jew’s stone** <note>original: "iudaici"; fossilized sea urchin spines used as a diuretic</note>, **flints** <note>original: "silicum"</note>, or stones from **carp**, **perch**, and similar creatures is taken by mouth—whether as a fine powder or dissolved in vinegar—it indeed "drinks up" the preceding cause of the coagulation (that is, the **acid** which I have already demonstrated), but it does not actually reduce the stone that has already been coagulated.

In the same way, salty things—acting in place of the acidic part—hinder the further thickening of the stone, insofar as they consume the **alkali** of the urine and ab... <meta>catchword: "ab-" likely leads to "absorbent" (absorb)</meta>

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s chemical investigation into the formation of bodily stones (calculi). ->## HIPPOCRATES.<-

...they absorb <note>original: "forbent"; referring to the medicinal substances mentioned on the previous page</note>, so that the wandering **acid** in the body does not find an **alkali** with which it can bind and coagulate itself; however, salty substances do not dissolve the hardened **stone** <term>calculus: a stone or "pebble" formed within the body, such as a kidney or gallstone</term> itself.

Moreover, I have shown above, in the preparation of **sal ammoniac** <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy and early chemistry for various reactions</term>, that **common salt** <term>sal commune: table salt or sodium chloride</term> drinks up and absorbs the **alkali of urine** and the remaining alkalis of animals; and now I will demonstrate other mechanical proofs.

<margin>
The importance of common salt.
</margin>

It is well known that the flesh of any animal is preserved with **common salt**, which in a few days flows down into a salty, bloody liquor; boil and skim this liquor, then put about a pound of it into a **glass retort** <term>retort: a bulbous glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck, used for distilling liquids</term>. With a gentle fire, draw out all the moisture; finally, bury the retort in sand <note>sand-bath: a method used by early chemists to apply even, high heat by surrounding a glass vessel with sand</note> and increase the fire. Then the **alkali of the flesh**—similar to the alkali of urine—will rise into the neck of the retort; this proves that the common salt absorbed and took away [that alkali] from the flesh through the mere **soaking** <term>maceration: the process of softening or extracting substances by soaking in a liquid</term> of only a few days. In the same way, the salt which we eat daily consumes—

<summary>Tachenius argues that common salt prevents the formation of kidney and bladder stones by absorbing the bodily alkalis that would otherwise bind with acids. He describes a chemical experiment where salt extracts alkali from meat as proof of this absorbent property.</summary>

<keywords>common salt, alkali, acid, calculus, distillation, glass retort, meat preservation, sal ammoniac, chemical medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the text from page 87, completing the word "absorbere" (to consume). Tachenius is explaining how salt acts upon alkalis in the body's cavities to maintain health and prevent stone formation.</meta>

->### TACHENIUS<-

...to consume and drink up the superfluous **alkali** collected here and there in the cavities or ventricles (as Hippocrates says), and which is unfit for transpiration <note>original: "transpiratione ineptum"; referring to waste products that cannot be expelled through the skin or breath</note>; this is both necessary and dictated by reason; indeed, experience also bears witness to it. For by an easy method, **alkali** is sublimated <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then collecting it as a solid again to purify it</term> from urine, while the **sea salt** <term>sal maris: common table salt (sodium chloride)</term> remains at the bottom, just as it was when eaten; this happens in the same way I have already shown with the salty liquor of meat. From this, it may be gathered that salt was given to mortals as a necessary support for health and life.

<margin>
The wise teach that nothing is more natural than for a thing to be dissolved by that from which it was composed.
</margin>

From what has been said and shown, it is now clear by mechanical necessity that a **stone** <term>calculus: a solid mass formed in the body, such as a kidney or bladder stone</term> is not dissolved except by a middle flavor between **acid** and **alkali**. This substance is changed into urine with its powers intact, being pleasing to the internal organs, as was mentioned; it must be subtle, so that it may freely penetrate all things. Call this method of dissolving "by opposites" or "by likes," and you will not be mistaken; for if an **alkali** dissolves the stone, it happens by "likes" <note>original: "a simili"; refers to the principle that like substances can dissolve one another</note>; if an **acid** destroys it, it happens by "opposites" <note>original: "a contrario"; refers to the principle that opposites neutralize or break each other down</note>. Who else but **Hippocrates** (of whatever medical sect) could untie this knot for us?

<margin>A remedy for the stone, as well as for epilepsy and asthma.</margin>
There is also a specific remedy known to few, which completely removes the **stone** <term>calculus: a solid mass, like a kidney or bladder stone</term> of the bladder in younger people without any recurrence; indeed, adults who take it by mouth feel no discomfort. If I did not fear the corruption of the recipe by impostors <note>original: "impostorum reformationem"; referring to charlatans who might alter the medicine for profit</note>, I would gladly share it; but in this instance, empirical experience <note>original: "casus"; literally "chance" or "event," here implying practical trial</note> is worth more than theory. By the same remedy, asthma is also perfectly cured, as is epilepsy from the lower regions <note>original: "epilepsia ex inferis"; a historical medical term for seizures believed to be caused by "vapors" rising from the stomach or lower organs rather than originating in the head</note>.

->HIPPOCRATES. 99<-

...health is restored. And thus is perfected and coagulated the **smegma** <term>smegma: in 17th-century chemistry, this refers to a soap-like substance formed by the combination of internal acids and alkalis, thought to cleanse the body's channels</term>, which itself, if it ever becomes disproportionate, becomes diseased. It must then be resolved by its like, and again reduced to health by its contrary—as I have shown above—being re-cooked and re-coagulated into salt.

These points are sufficient to indicate the origin and generation of **volatile salts** in the animal kingdom <note>original: *animalium familia*</note> regarding this matter; unless someone should wish to argue that because we eat salty foods, volatile salts are produced from them. However, I have shown above that this is impossible due to the constancy of salt. Indeed, salt is found in the urine exactly as it was eaten, unchanged in form and nature, and without any alteration to itself. This is easily proven through experience: namely, from the thickening of urine and its subsequent **calcination** <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to ash or a friable state</term>, **solution** <term>solution: the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid</term>, and **coagulation**, granulated salt is recovered, just as it is usually produced.

There are even men living in the Alps who subsist on simple milk, yet their urine does not lack **acid** and **alkali**; and although in the form of salt...

### Chapter 16.

I have shown the method for extracting alkali from sweat, and likewise from urine, from blood, and from the flesh of all animals; I have also said that alkali is changed into a volatile salt <note>original: *falsum volatile*, likely a typesetter's error for *salsum volatile* (volatile salt)</note> with acid not only in the stomach, but also in all the ventricles <term>ventricles: in early modern medicine, these are not just the chambers of the heart or brain, but any internal cavity where the body "cooks" or processes nutrients and fluids</term>, the great number of which Hippocrates indicates (a) <margin>from the book *On Art*</margin>; and when that salt has completed its task, it then transpires <note>passes through the pores of the skin or membranes</note> along with the "dissenting" (b) <margin>Hippocrates, *On Regimen*</margin> or "fighting" substance—that is, with the oily excrement—and is swept away into the elements, as happens to all sublunary <term>sublunary: referring to the Earthly realm below the moon, where all things are subject to change, decay, and recycling into the four elements</term> things. I have demonstrated this in the fixed salt of tartar <term>salt of tartar: potassium carbonate, a foundational substance in early chemistry used to study the properties of alkalis</term> and oil, and hereafter...

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s chemical demonstration of how alkalis and acids interact within the human body. ### HIPPOCRATES. 101

...more clearly <note>original: *luculentius*; this completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the salt of tartar</note>, and also concerning the artificial soap, which, after its completion and use, will return into the water and the earth. This transpiration and its recall to the depths <note>original: *in orcum*, referring to the underworld or the recycling of matter back into the elemental "grave"</note> will appear more clearly through examples.

<margin>Vitriol separates alkali from acid.</margin>
If anyone drinks, or even lightly tastes, a tiny amount of vitriol of Mars <term>vitriol of Mars: an early chemical name for iron sulfate, known for its metallic and astringent properties</term>, his tongue immediately turns black and appears as if clothed in velvet. The pores of the tongue are always swollen with hidden salty exhalations, which either mix with the saliva or are carried away into the air. Therefore, as soon as the acidity of the vitriol of Mars detects the hidden alkali in the saliva, faster than a strike, it separates the colcothar <term>colcothar: the solid residue or precipitate left over from a chemical reaction involving vitriol</term>, which sticks to the tongue and makes it black.

Lest you believe I am telling fables here, look, I proceed again to a visual demonstration.

Dissolve vitriol of Mars in clear water to create a transparent solution. Drop by drop, add the fixed alkali of tartar <term>fixed alkali of tartar: potassium carbonate, a base often derived by burning the crusty deposits found in wine barrels</term>—that is, the liquor of tartar—and then the colcothar falls <note>precipitates out of the liquid</note> as a dark greenish substance.

Salt of bean stalks disturbs the solution into a yellowish hue, and after one hour of rest, it raises ochre <note>original: *ocram*; a term for the brownish-yellow iron oxide residue that settles at the bottom</note> from the bottom.

Common salt, being the most perfect of salts, simply mixes with the vitriol; the same is true for corrosive sublimate <term>mercurius sublimatus: mercuric chloride, an "artificial" salt produced through chemical manipulation</term>, for it is an artificial salt. These, just like any other acids, do not precipitate the vitriol.

Therefore, only alkalis precipitate the vitriol to the bottom, tending toward a black color. However, fixed alkalis <note>salts that remain solid and stable even when heated</note> are not found in the living animal; therefore, the alkalis within us must be volatile <note>substances that easily evaporate or turn into gas, like the "spirits" found in urine or sweat</note>. These are bound <note>the original word *ligata* is completed by the catchword "gata" at the bottom of the page</note> incessantly with acid...
