# HIPPOCRATES

...bound; after they have served their purpose, they exhale from us and transpire with an acid under a hidden salty taste, as will be more clearly seen.

*As long as one lives, the entire human body is exhalable and breathable* <note>original: *expirabile & conspirabile*; a core Hippocratic concept suggesting the body is a porous vessel that constantly takes in and releases substances through its pores</note>, and this was first noted by Hippocrates. Whatever is exhaled is secretly salty; however, this saltiness does not only evaporate toward the outer surface of the body, but the greater part of it flows down into the intestines, which is proven thus:

Drink <term>vitriol of Mars: iron sulfate</term> dissolved in water, and you will see the waste of the bowels stained either black or green. It is precipitated in this same way by an internal alkali, just as I have already shown mechanically in the table <note>referring to the table on the previous page showing how different alkalis change the color of vitriol</note>. But on the exterior of the body, dissolved vitriol is not precipitated by these salty vapors; therefore, there must be a greater flow of these substances toward the intestines.

Thus, the urine is not stained black either, for the <term>colcothar: the dark residue or "dead head" left after the distillation of vitriol</term> is precipitated before the liquid is carried to the <term>mesenteric vessels: the veins that transport nutrients from the digestive tract to the liver</term>, so that it must necessarily remain within the intestines.

### TACHENIUS
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

<margin>Hippocrates teaches that purgatives are not sufficient for recovery.</margin>

If, therefore, the greatest part of the waste products, banished by nature into exile, flows toward the intestines, it is sometimes held back by error by the bonds of "coagulations" <note>solidified or thickened substances in the body's fluids</note> in improper places—or, to use the terminology of Fernel <note>Jean Fernel (1497–1558), an influential French physician who categorized the body's functions and regions</note>, in the "first region" of the body—it is absolutely necessary that it be moved toward its exit by a **dissolving medicine** <note>a purgative or laxative intended to "dissolve" blockages</note>. For this reason, it does not seem to me that every dissolving medicine should be rejected or renounced in times of illness, as some have supposed. However, such medicines alone are not sufficient to bring about a full recovery, as the majority of doctors believe and practice; this is taught by Hippocrates in his book *On the Art* <note>original: *libro de arte*; a treatise in the Hippocratic Corpus defending the efficacy of the medical profession</note>.

But the question arises: why do acidic springs that carry iron not turn the tongue black, in the same way they darken the waste of the bowels? This happens because the natural <term>acid spirit: a volatile acidic substance</term>, which gnaws at the vein of <term>immature iron: iron ore or unrefined metal</term> and is mixed with water, is not fully saturated but remains the dominant force in the mixture. Furthermore, the spring water is drunk while this "gnawing" <note>original: *rosio*; refers to the chemical corrosion of the metal by the acid</note> action is still occurring; therefore, there is no time or opportunity for <term>precipitation: the process where a solid settles out of a liquid</term> to take place.

<summary>Tachenius argues that while purgatives are necessary to clear blockages in the body's "first region," they are not a complete cure on their own. He also uses early chemical theory to explain why iron-rich mineral waters only discolor the stool and not the tongue, citing the active state of the "acid spirit."</summary>

He rejoiced that, by his subtle reasoning, he had learned how to "imprison" the spirit of the spring, which he considered to be the same as the **spirit of wine** <note>original: *spiritum vini*; ethanol or concentrated alcohol</note>. To such men, nevertheless—God permitting it so—we entrust our lives, fortunes, wives, children, and families, even though they are ignorant of the actual tools of medicine. He would have known, I say, that the **acid** <note>original: *acidum*; here referring to the carbonation or volatile acidic "spirit" in mineral water</note> does not simply "breathe out" <note>original: *expirare*; to evaporate or exhale</note>, but rather, through its reaction with iron, it is finally exhausted. It does not then vanish into the air, but in the form of dregs, both [the acid and the iron] sink to the bottom; this is what they call **ochre** <note>original: *ochram*; a yellowish-brown iron oxide deposit</note>. I cannot wonder enough at the nonsense of certain talkative charlatans, who promise to distill the spring and to divine secrets from the sediment with equal stupidity. Thus the rich are sent away empty-handed.

**Spa water** <note>original: *Spadana*; water from the famous mineral springs of Spa in modern-day Belgium, renowned for its "chalybeate" (iron-rich) properties</note> is much more acidic, and although it has "gnawed" <note>original: *roserit*; chemically corroded or reacted with</note> the vein of iron, a certain acidity still remains in it; for this reason, it allows itself to be transported elsewhere <note>the author suggests its high acidity preserves it, allowing it to be bottled and shipped without losing its medicinal character as quickly as other waters</note>.

The fame of this spring was so celebrated that about ten years ago, it moved a Nobleman of **Vicenza** <note>original: *Vicentinum*; a resident of the city of Vicenza in Italy</note> to diligently investigate the nature of the spring for the sake of his own health. I described to him briefly its properties and which diseases it might treat; at that time—

...it contained faults and slanders,
and accused me of introducing
a new philosophy—which,
nevertheless, since they manifest
nothing other than a malignant
spirit and stupid ignorance,
have returned to their source!
But to the matter at hand.

Just as vitriol <note>original: *vitriolum*; a sulfate of a metal, in this context specifically iron sulfate</note> and all waters that contain dissolved iron are precipitated into a black sediment; so also the acidic spirit of vitriol <note>original: *spiritus vitrioli*; sulfuric acid, produced by distilling vitriol</note> itself retains the metallic solution, especially when it is purified <note>original: *rectificatus*; a chemical term for refining a substance through repeated distillation</note>.

<margin>Spirit of vitriol stains teeth yellow.</margin>

If the purest alkali <note>original: *alcali*; Tachenius's term for the basic or "bitter" counterpart to acid</note> is poured into this, it is precipitated; not indeed into a black substance, but into *colcothar* <note>original: *colcotar*; the brownish-red iron oxide residue left after distillation</note> which remains stable in fire. For that reason, those who use this spirit for the purpose of whitening their teeth are greatly deceived; it does indeed strengthen the gums, but it infects the teeth with a most stubborn yellowness, just as vitriol itself turns them blackish. For incessantly, and by necessity, something exhales from us in the form of moisture <note>original: *madoris*; moisture or vapor secreted by the body</note> filled with alkali; this also precipitates *colcothar* from the aforementioned spirit around the sockets of the teeth, which adheres to the teeth and turns them yellow.

<meta>This page continues Tachenius's discussion of the chemical properties of vitriol, transitioning from its effect on the body (teeth and gums) to its role in industrial chemistry, specifically the production of ink and dyes. It builds on his fundamental theory that all chemical reactions are a struggle between "acid" and "alkali."</meta>

#### TACHENIUS

#### *By way of vitriol, he approaches more closely the salts of plants.*

### Chapter 17.

<margin>
Things which are most different, agree the most. <note>original: *quæ plurimum diuersa sunt, maxime concordant*</note>
Hippocrates: Book 1 *On Diet*.
</margin>
I have said that neither acid, nor salt, nor any other base flavor destroys vitriol <note>original: *vitriolum*; a metallic sulfate, usually iron sulfate here</note> except for alkalis <note>original: *alcalia*; substances that neutralize acids</note>: but the juice from unripe oak galls <note>original: *gallis inmaturis*; abnormal growths on oak trees rich in tannins</note> combines with vitriol to turn black; it destroys and absorbs the vitriol's acid, and therefore this juice must consist of alkalis. In this manner, inks <note>original: *atramenta*</note> and all black dyes are made. Indeed, when the acidity of the vitriol is not sufficiently absorbed by the alkali of the galls—which happens when the galls are too mature or have been washed out by the rain—then the alkali expires, as happens with all plant matter (which I will discuss later in its proper place). In such cases, the vitriol eats away at the fabrics until they become practically rotten, and the common people cry out: *the cloth is burned in the dye* <note>original Italian: *la robba è brusada nella tinta*</note>. Likewise, letters written with ink brewed from such galls will fade to a pale color, because the prevailing acidity of the vitriol—the weak alkali of the galls having been washed away by rain...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence with the catchword "lu-" indicating the start of the word "abluantur" or similar on the following page, regarding the washing away of the alkaline salts.</meta>

<summary>Tachenius explains the chemistry of ink-making, arguing that the reaction between vitriol and oak galls is an acid-alkali neutralization. He notes that if the galls lack sufficient alkali, the remaining acid will "burn" fabrics and cause ink to fade.</summary>
<keywords>vitriol, oak galls, ink-making, dyes, acid-alkali theory, Otto Tachenius, chemical degradation of fabric</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 110, completing the sentence regarding how rain washes away the alkali from galls, leaving behind the corrosive acid of the vitriol which "consumes the mud" (the physical substance of the cloth or paper).</meta>

->HIPPOCRATES. 111<-

...consumes the mud <note>original: *lutum*; likely referring to the physical matter of the fabric or paper substrate</note>; in the same way, as any acidic spirit smeared over writing immediately destroys and takes away the black color (that is, the alkali), which another fixed alkali then restores.

<margin>The foundation of cryptography <note>secret writing</note>.</margin>
Write in this manner: with water in which **iron vitriol** <note>original: *vitriolum ferreum*; iron(II) sulfate, the primary ingredient in historical black inks</note> has been dissolved. When it has dried, no trace of the writing will appear. Then, smear the writing with a simple infusion of **galls** <note>original: *gallarum*; growths on oak trees that contain high levels of tannins</note>; and in a moment, the alkali within these galls makes the writing turn black. This writing is immediately deleted by a potent acid or *aqua fortis* <term>nitric acid, literally "strong water"</term>, because the alkali is surely consumed by the acid. If you again soak the paper with a **fixed alkali** <note>original: *alcali fixo*; a stable alkaline salt such as potash</note>, which once more consumes the acid, the writing will reappear. From what has been said, it is now clear that vitriol does not produce a black dye unless its acid is consumed by some alkali.

<margin>The alkali of galls.</margin>
To make the alkali of galls more perfect, smear the galls with fat or a certain oil; the hidden acidity of this <insert>oil</insert> pervades the gall in the gentle heat of ashes, when the galls are placed there in a small jar. Leave them there until...

<meta>catchword: nor (nec)</meta>

<summary>Tachenius explains the chemistry of "secret ink" (cryptography) using the reaction between iron vitriol and oak galls, framing the color change as a battle between acidic and alkaline "spirits." This text is a foundational example of early modern chemical theory applied to practical technology like ink-making.</summary>

## TACHENIUS

...nor will you see galls <note>original: *gallas*; oak galls used in ink-making</note> any blacker, provided they have not been reduced to charcoal. In that state, their **alkali** <term>a basic, salt-like substance that neutralizes acids</term> will be more ready for dyeing; an ounce of these "burnt" galls is more effective than a pound of the raw kind. Indeed, it dyes on its own, because the acid from the fat <note>referring to the fat or oil mentioned on the previous page</note> has acted upon the gall's alkali through the power of the fire.

<margin>Pomegranate peels.</margin>
Many other plants abound in a similar **volatile and hidden alkali**, such as the greater houseleek <note>original: *sempervivum maius*; a succulent plant often used in traditional remedies</note>, sage, and **pomegranate peels**. All of these consume the acid found in **vitriol** <note>iron(II) sulfate</note> and precipitate the **colcothar** <term>iron oxide residue, often used as a pigment or polishing powder</term> into a more or less black color.

A certain Prince of great renown, who studied the mysteries of nature with great zeal, was deeply amazed when he heard a report regarding a certain young lady from the **Gynaeceum** <note>original: *Gynaeceo*; the private women's quarters of a royal court</note>. Nevertheless, in her case, the peels had provoked her **menses** <note>menstrual cycle</note>, which had been absent for a long time. When I showed this Prince the abundant alkali with which these peels overflow, and explained that the obstruction of the menses, origin...

<margin>Alexius’s secret</margin>
From this it is clear that **burnt copper** <term>aes ustum; copper oxidized by heat, often used as a pigment or medicine</term>, when mixed with roasted **galls** <note>original: *gallis*; oak galls used in making black ink due to their high tannin content</note>, does not produce a black dye, as **Alexius Pedemontanus** <note>Girolamo Ruscelli (1500–1566), who wrote under the pseudonym Alessio Piemontese, author of a famous book of "Secrets"</note> promises. <margin>Here also the metaphor of the coupling of Venus and Mars seems to fit not inaptly.</margin>

<margin>white vitriol</margin>
That white variety from **Goslar** <note>a historic mining town in Germany famous for its minerals</note>, as well as that from **Carinthia** <note>a region in modern-day Austria</note>, does indeed partake of the nature of both **Mars** <note>iron</note> and **Venus** <note>copper</note>; however, they are not rich in natural acid. <margin>Roman vitriol</margin>
But **Roman vitriol**, which is very rich in iron, though poor in "spirit" <note>low in free acid</note>, stains or dyes promptly because of its iron content.

<margin>artificial vitriol of Mars</margin>
In the same way, **artificial vitriol**—which is made from iron filings combined with the **Spirit of Vitriol** <term>sulfuric acid</term> or Sulfur—stains with that same readiness. Thus, when dyers lack "sweet" vitriol <note>vitriol where the acid is fully neutralized by metal</note>, they take the moist and consequently sharp <note>acidic</note> variety and add iron filings; the acid "gnaws" or consumes the iron, and the vitriol becomes "sweet."

<margin>Silver</margin>
**Silver**, corroded in **aqua forti** <term>nitric acid; literally 'strong water'</term> made from nitre and alum, when mixed with an infusion of **galls** <note>oak galls, which contain tannins that react with metals to create pigments</note>, does not change its nature. If one writes on paper with this mixture, after two days every letter appears black and is surrounded by a silvery and shining thread. This does not occur without an **alkali** <note>a basic substance used to neutralize the acid</note>. From this, will that rare person understand why our ancestors metaphorically called **gold** 'male' and **silver** 'female'? <note>In alchemy, gold is associated with the Sun and the active masculine principle, while silver is associated with the Moon and the passive feminine principle.</note> If a drop of this solution falls heavily upon the paper, it produces a silver thread followed by another of a **chestnut color**, looking as if it had been painted with great artistic effort—a delightful sight to see.

<margin>Tin and Lead</margin>
**Tin**, as well as **lead**, when reduced to a salt or **vitriol** <term>crystalline metal salts</term> and mixed with the juice of galls—as well as with all alkalis—forms a snowy-white sediment. This means the alkali of the galls absorbs the acid, and the metal seeks the bottom <note>it precipitates out of the solution</note>. However, this does not happen with gold, nor with silver, nor with copper, etc.

<margin>Mercury</margin>
**Mercury**, dissolved with acids and re-coagulated, when mixed with the aforementioned juice of galls, slowly turns yellow... <meta>catchword: ...knows [original: scit, the suffix of the preceding 'flauescit']</meta>

<margin>There are no words, etc. <note>original: *Non sunt loquela &c.*; a reference to Psalm 19, suggesting that these chemical reactions are a silent language through which nature speaks</note></margin>
Therefore, of the seven metals dissolved in acid, **iron** and **silver** stain with a black color when mixed with **galls** <note>oak galls, which contain tannins that react with metals to create ink</note>; indeed, silver does so even without galls. Consider the cause of this if you can, for it is not unpleasant to know it.

<margin>A wonderful spirit</margin>
A salty, semi-acidic **spirit** <term>a distilled volatile liquid</term>—distilled from the **caput mortuum** <term>literally "dead head"; the solid residue remaining after distillation</term> of **tartar** and **sal ammoniac** mixed with **Armenian bole** <note>a red medicinal clay from Armenia, often containing mineral impurities</note>, as mentioned above—this spirit turns green in an instant when combined with the juice of galls. From this I learned that the bole must necessarily contain an "immature copper seed," which the aforementioned spirit carried away with it during the **torture of the fire** <note>a common alchemical phrase for the intense heat of the furnace</note>. I call it a **copper seed**, for vitriol made from copper using spirit of vitriol, when precipitated with the juice of galls, appears in that same green color, as I showed above; however, when it is produced from common clay, it then turns black.

The foundation of the black color.

From this it is evident that **volatile salt** <term>a pungent, ammonia-like salt distilled from organic matter</term>, or the **alkali of galls** <note>the basic chemical components extracted from oak galls</note>, or of **pomegranate rinds**, or of **wound-healing herbs** do not stain with a black color, except when combined with **iron** that has been dissolved in a **mineral acid**.

Another thing worth noting is that a pure, natural **acidic spirit** cannot be acquired or possessed by just anyone; for even if it is distilled from **vitriol** <term>metallic sulfates, such as green vitriol or iron sulfate</term> and however much it is rectified <note>purified through repeated distillation</note>, it never truly leaves the metallic liquid. ### Chapter 18.

<margin>The mechanical instruments of vegetables. Hippocrates' two faculties. Book 1 of *On Diet* <note>original: *de diætā*; a foundational Greek medical text attributed to Hippocrates which discusses the balance of fire and water in the body</note>.</margin>
All vegetables, no less than animals, possess vital instruments which they use for their growth and propagation. In the seed, therefore—as if in an egg or a stomach—there sleeps a prevailing **acid**, along with a tiny amount of **alkali** for its own sustenance, together with a **specific director** <term>original: *rectore*; a guiding vital principle or internal "architect" that governs growth</term> (of whom I speak elsewhere). But when the seed is cast into the earth, it is dissolved by moisture and awakened by heat; the acid, acting like fire, begins to work upon the alkali. To this, moisture is added, and the director shapes the matter through the benefit of heat. Thus, invisibly, the acid takes up the alkali, and the alkali in turn absorbs the acid.
<margin>The mystery of fermentation was once hidden with great care <note>refers to the alchemical tradition of keeping the secrets of "leavening" or "transformation" from the uninitiated</note>.</margin>
They move toward one another in the manner of **ferments** <term>substances that cause a chemical change or "boiling" action, like yeast in bread</term>; indeed, they do not cease until they have completed their preordained course toward their final ends. One spirit draws, the other pushes,

-># HIPPOCRATES. 121<-

...and so forth, says Hippocrates in the place cited <note>referring to Book 1 of *On Diet* mentioned on page 120</note>.
Therefore, the moisture of every vegetable contains an **acid** and an **alkali**; in some, the alkali predominates, while in others, the acid. The acid is very well known, but the alkali has until now been understood by few. I shall now demonstrate this to the eye through the well-known example of the sweet, expressed juice of grapes. In this juice, for the first few days, the **director** <term>original: *rector*; the internal vital principle or "architect" that governs the plant's development</term> sleeps. Shortly thereafter, it sets its mechanical instruments in motion: the acid begins to gnaw at the alkali, and the alkali absorbs the acid. A violent action and struggle ensues, which we call **fermentation** because of its boiling nature,
<margin>*A ferment is that which grows by boiling.*</margin>
and this continues until the acid, as the victor, overcomes the alkali. In that struggle, both faculties—call them flavors, instruments, mechanical or architectural forces, or even the **archeus** <term>original: *archeus*; a Paracelsian term for the "internal chemist" or life-force that directs the functions of an organ or organism</term>, the innate heat, etc., as you please—suffer a great slaughter. A vigorous mutual destruction occurs; the acid is absorbed by the alkali, overwhelmed and exhausted. Both fall in a mutual embrace into a "corpse" (in relation to the wine), which is called **Tartar** <note>original: *Tartarus*; the potassium bitartrate crystals that settle at the bottom of wine vats. The author views these solid remains as the "dead bodies" of the chemical reactants</note>.

First, the **volatile alkali** <term>original: *alcali volatile*; in early chemistry, this usually referred to ammonia or similar nitrogenous bases derived from organic matter</term> in the tartar is saturated with the **acid of sulfur of antimony**, and he calls this: *Tartarized Spirit of Antimony* <note>original: *spiritus Antimonii Tartarisatus*</note>. If, by some stroke of luck, any volatile alkali should still remain—look at what he does! He then kills it with **vinegar of verdigris** <term>original: *acetum ex aerugine*; an acetic acid solution made by distilling verdigris (copper acetate)</term> and **acid of vitriol** <term>original: *acidum vitrioli*; sulfuric acid</term>. Thus, this expensive but artificial and useless composition consists of nothing more than elemental water and vinegar; the ancients called such a mixture **posca** <note>a common Roman drink made of vinegar, water, and sometimes herbs, often given to soldiers</note>. To this, the Reformer adds nothing except the stench of tartar.

However, the **spirit of tartar** <note>the liquid produced by the dry distillation of tartar</note> which is distilled with wisdom by the follower of **Hippocrates**, is not acidic, but somewhat bitter and saturated with volatile alkali. If you pour spirit of vitriol upon it, it heats up so much that the flask can scarcely be held in the hands. This is an evident proof that the acid of vitriol absorbs the alkali of tartar, just as occurs when water is poured onto **lime** <note>original: *calci*; quicklime (calcium oxide), which reacts violently and releases heat when mixed with water</note>, it hap-

Thus teaches (a) my Master, **Hippocrates**. I return now to where I departed: namely, to the chemical fermentation of wine, through which the **tartar** fell to the bottom like a corpse.

The acidity, however, spread through the liquid, dominates and protects it, and it is called **wine**. The acidity of this wine becomes pleasing to the palate in relation to the **alkali** <term>a basic substance that neutralizes acids; here referring to the potassium carbonate found in wine lees</term> with which it has entered into friendship, and which tempers the acid. This lasts until finally the acidity—weary of the alkali or excited once more by heat—strives to overcome the alkali. A new but imperceptible struggle occurs, the wine turns more sour, and it is called by the common name **vinegar** <note>original: *acetum*</note>. If the acidity is separated from this vinegar using an **alembic** <term>a classic distillation apparatus used to separate liquids by their boiling points</term>, and the dregs at the bottom are burned with fire, then alkali is found within those tartareous dregs. To many, it will seem impossible to find alkali in such a strong acid, and yet it is true;

<sig>F 3</sig>

<margin>
Certainly, a pure acid is not found in nature without being mixed with an alkali, and vice versa. Hippocrates, Book 1 of *On Diet*. <note>original: "certe non reperitur in natura acidum purum..."</note>

...the philosopher (a) saying: it is impossible for there to be matter which does not have some form underlying it. <note>original: "inpoffibile effe materiam, cui nõ sub sit aliqua forma"</note>

<margin>

</margin>

To this **alkali** <term>a basic salt, such as potassium carbonate derived from plant ashes</term>, add **distilled acid**, and draw off the watery **phlegm** <term>the tasteless, watery byproduct of distillation</term> which has the scent of "burning water" <note>original: *aquæ ardentis*; an early term for alcohol or brandy</note>; then, from the acid and alkali combined, you will have **regenerated tartar**. When this substance is processed in a **retort** <term>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</term>, it yields a fatty oil and a bitter water saturated with **volatile alkali**. From the phlegm, using a long-necked vial, draw off the watery, burning fatness, which we call **water of life** <term>aqua vitae; a term for concentrated spirit or alcohol</term>; the remaining part is elemental water.

<margin>
Thus the **ruler** <term>rector; the internal guiding spirit or chemical principle of a substance</term> gradually recedes into nothingness from our external senses; so much so that man does not find the work which God has performed. Ecclesiastes Chapter 3, verse 11.
</margin>

The fatness takes back the alkali, from which you should again draw off elemental water; repeat this operation, and the entire substance of the wine passes into elemental water and earth, deprived of all taste and smell. From this it is clear that the juice of grapes preserved the alkali and acid (in which the **ruler** dwells) through various changes and so many trials, even until its final annihilation.

### Chapter 19.

What I demonstrated in the preceding chapter regarding the juice of grapes must be understood likewise for the juices of all **vegetables** <note>In this period, "vegetables" refers to the entire plant kingdom</note>; for in all of them, there exists **acid** and **alkali**, in greater or lesser amounts, as **Hippocrates** and experience teach. There is, however, this difference: in those plants which we call "cold" <note>Referring to the ancient system of humoral qualities, where plants were classified as hot, cold, moist, or dry</note>, **alkali** predominates, and they are not fermentable unless stirred up by an external acid or by heat. But in those which are "hot," **acid** prevails, and for that reason they are easily fermentable, as will be seen in the progress of this work.

<margin>*Decay in all things is mutual among themselves; the greater from the lesser, and the lesser from the greater. <note>original: "Corruptio omnibus mutuo..."</note> Hippocrates, Book 1 of On Diet.*</margin>

Furthermore, the **alkali** of certain plants is closer to being **fixed** <term>stable and resistant to evaporation or change when exposed to high heat</term>, and therefore it unites with mineral acids, as I have shown in **galls** <note>growths on oak trees often used in making ink and medicines</note>, the **greater houseleek** <note>original: *sempervivum maius*; a succulent plant used in traditional medicine</note>, the husks of grains, and so on. In other plants, the alkali is much more delicate; on which account mineral acids consume it immediately, insofar as they are

Many skilled surgeons have observed that acid as the beginning of heat and putrefaction in the wounded; for that reason, they rightly forbid them the use of **wine**, lest the subtle and vaporous acidity of the wine increase the illness. The alkali of these plants, I say, does not become manifest when mixed with mineral acids; rather, because these plants are themselves nutritious, the aforementioned alkali associates itself with its own kind <margin>a *Nature rejoices in its own nature.* <note>original: "natura gaudet in sua natura." For just as burnt stag’s horn absorbs and drinks up the **volatile acid salt** from vinegar, they have observed with great joy that it likewise removes and consumes the acidity that arises in certain fevers.

<margin>Hidden alkalis.</margin>
Besides the wound-healing herbs, there are even more hidden and more **volatile alkalis** in the plants we call **cooling**, such as **lettuce** <note>original: "lactuca"</note>, **purslane** <note>original: "portulaca"</note>, **coltsfoot** <note>original: "farfara"</note>, and the various types and species of **endive** <note>original: "intibum"</note>. ...it is learned from this that dried herbs of this kind are of no value, because their **alkali** <term>in early chemistry, an 'alkali' was seen as the opposite of an 'acid,' capable of neutralizing it</term> has expired <note>original: "expirauit"; meaning the volatile components have evaporated or breathed out</note>.

It immediately precipitates lead from a solution more promptly and more copiously than any other cooling water. This water is to be highly valued in many conditions arising from a specific acid; for I have known a desperate **hemorrhage of the uterus** <note>original: "hæmorragiam ex utero"</note> to be successfully cured by it, insofar as it first absorbs the acidity of the blood, and afterwards inhibits and repels it. This water also, when applied to a part suffering from **gout** <term>podagra; a painful inflammatory arthritis which the author attributes to an excess of 'acid' in the joints</term>, immediately mitigates the pain, insofar as it kills and alters the sharpness of the acid.

<margin>Volatile alkalis consume the pains of gout.</margin>

<summary>Tachenius argues that water’s ability to nourish and react chemically comes from its "volatile alkali," citing ancient philosophers to support his chemical observations. He specifically challenges the medicinal efficacy of distilled rose water, suggesting that the distillation process alters its cooling properties.</summary>

<keywords>volatile alkali, Thales of Miletus, Hermes Trismegistus, distillation, rose water, Lactantius, chemical nourishment, Venice, alchemical apparatus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 134 regarding the chemical properties of distilled waters. Specifically, it concludes the argument about why "rose water" is often mistakenly believed to have certain cooling or medicinal properties due to the metallic containers used in its distillation.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>HIPPOCRATES.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<margin>Rose water does not cool unless it is distilled through lead. <note>original: "Aqua roſarum non refrigerat niſi per plumbum ſtillat."</note></margin>
(sometimes, because of age, the **tin** <term>stannum; specifically the tin lining inside a copper vessel</term> is consumed). They administer this water to children because it kills and expels worms; sometimes it even induces vomiting. The common people ascribe this action and power to the rose water itself. But rose water is not clearly cold, but **temperate** <note>In Galenic medicine, "temperate" means a balanced quality, neither excessively hot nor cold</note>, for the reason that it possesses a hidden and insensible, yet inseparable **acidity** which does not leave the water until its very annihilation. Therefore, when it flows through a **copper alembic** <term>a chemical still used for distillation</term>, its hidden acidity scrapes certain atoms from the copper, which are invisibly mixed into the water. 

Do you wish to see the copper? Drop into a small portion of this water one or two little drops of **alkali of urine** <term>an early term for ammonia or alkaline salts derived from urine</term>, and immediately the whole volume of water turns green. This happens because the acidity of the water more greedily seizes upon the light alkali (which is more similar to it) than it does the metal; because of this, the metal gradually falls to the bottom as a **green sediment**. At that point, the water no longer induces vomiting, nor does it kill worms, and it will be similar to that water which has been distilled through glass vessels. 

<summary>The author demonstrates that the perceived medicinal effects of rose water, such as expelling worms, are actually caused by copper contamination from the distillation process. He provides a chemical experiment using "alkali of urine" to precipitate the metal and prove his point.</summary>

<keywords>rose water, distillation, copper, alembic, alkali of urine, toxicology, alchemy, Hippocrates, chemistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how acidity in distilled waters (like rose water) reacts with the metals of the distillation vessel. The previous page ended with an experiment showing how copper can be precipitated out of the water using an alkali, forming a green sediment.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>
<header>TACHENIUS</header>

...however, while it is green, melt it with **borax** <term>a flux used in soldering and metallurgy to help metals melt</term>, and it returns to copper.

<margin>
*All vegetables contain a volatile alkali; they are cooling, and like all wound-herbs, they generate milk in nursing mothers; for acids are the obstacles to milk.* <note>original: "olera omnia continēt alcali volatile..."</note>
</margin>
Not only do wound-herbs and cooling herbs possess a hidden **nutritive alkali** <term>alcali nutritiuum; a salt Tachenius believed was essential for bodily growth and health</term>, but indeed all vegetables do. For this reason, they are craved by the stomach when there is an excess of acid. This alkali is the reason why those who eat cooked **cabbage** before a bout of heavy drinking rarely become intoxicated; for just as the alkali of this herb tempers the intoxicating acid of the wine in the stomach, so too in a glass vessel it absorbs and consumes it.

<margin>
*Specific remedies.* <note>original: "Specifica media."</note>
</margin>
But as for why certain **simples** <term>medicines made from a single herb or substance</term>—such as the juice of **yarrow** for hemorrhoids, **fumitory** for jaundice, **butcher's broom** for urinary issues, **guaiacum** and **sarsaparilla** for the **venereal disease** <note>The "French Disease" or syphilis, commonly treated with these New World plants in the 17th century</note>, and many others—are specifically beneficial for certain conditions: and in what exactly the power of purgatives consists? To recount those things here now would be too laborious. It is enough to have "passed the torch" <note>an idiom for passing on knowledge to the next generation</note>: by this writing at least, we have been compelled, even against the grain <note>original: "invita minerva," a Latin idiom for working against one's natural inclination or the difficulty of the subject</note>, to make manifest the salts which have remained hidden until now.

<margin>
*Objection.* <note>original: "Obiectio."</note>
</margin>
I hear whisperers here, claiming that with dissolved lead, the **volatile alkalis** of vegetables, demon-

...I contend to show, since even various impurities precipitate **lead** <term>plumbum</term> from this solution (which is certainly what we admitted before), yet with this difference: that lead [when treated] with salt falls in the form of a powder, while the liquid floating on top remains clear. Distill it, and you will have **vinegar** <note>original: "acetum"; likely acetic acid which was used to dissolve the lead initially</note>. That which was carrying the dissolved lead falls out of the vinegar because salt has a greater affinity for vinegar than the metal does. 

But if you mix the **alkali** of herbs, or rather drip the juice of said herbs into that lead solution, then it does not fall precipitously in the form of a powder; instead, it becomes like a **thick pap** <note>original: "instar puls"; a consistency like porridge or mush</note>. If you then distill the liquid floating on top, you will draw from it not vinegar, but tasteless water—a manifest proof that the alkali has absorbed the acidity.

How much light this **Hippocratic** doctrine <note>Tachenius viewed Hippocrates as a proto-chemist who understood the balance of "acid" and "alkali" in the body</note> adds for the physician, both in composing 
<margin>
solution
</margin>
**medicines** <term>medicamentis</term>, so that one ingredient does not hinder the other (indeed, the power of **simples** <term>medicines made from a single herb or substance</term> can be wonderfully exalted by this science), as well as in [understanding] diseases...

<summary>Tachenius demonstrates that adding plant juices (alkalis) to a lead solution neutralizes the acid into water, proving his theory of acid-alkali balance. He argues this chemical knowledge is essential for physicians to create effective medicines and understand pathology.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius's discussion on the chemical properties of plants and their medicinal uses. Having previously discussed how lead reacts with acids and alkalis, he now uses the common nettle as a primary example to show how chemical "hidden virtues" differ from simple elemental qualities.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>TACHENIUS</header>

...for healing: let those who know how to distinguish flavors and modestly invert them judge what has been said thus far. 
<margin>The powers of the Nettle.</margin>
It will be enough to show this by the single example of the **nettle** <term>urtica</term>: which, when fresh and wet, burns and blisters the skin, but when dried, it is astringent and stops bleeding. The juice of this same plant coagulates when added to dissolved **lead** into a black mass; this mass, in turn, is immediately dissolved again in **fixed alkali** <term>alcali fixo; a stable alkaline salt like potash</term> into a dark, yet transparent color. From this, we learn that dried nettle, boiled in an **alkalized lye** <note>original: "lixivio alcalizato"; a solution of alkaline salts used for cleaning or chemistry</note> (the "sweet" kind, as they call it), prevents **graying hair** <term>canitiem</term> if the hair is smeared with it every day. Furthermore, when mixed with **vitriol** <note>original: "vitriolo"; a sulfate of a metal, often used in making inks and dyes</note>, it thickens into yellowish lumps, which, by the **spirit** <note>an acidic distillate</note> of that same vitriol, are again dissolved into a clear liquid.

*Of this kind, I believe, were the hidden virtues of roots about which **Solomon** spoke, and the differences of shrubs, which he learned from Wisdom, the artisan of all things, discoursing from the **hyssop** <term>hyssopo</term> to the **cedar of Lebanon** <term>cedrum libani</term>; for it is not believable that he poured forth words merely about primary qualities and elemental degrees.* <note>Tachenius argues that Solomon’s biblical wisdom regarding plants was based on deep chemical/alchemical knowledge rather than just simple "hot" or "cold" Aristotelian qualities.</note>

<summary>Tachenius explains the chemical transformations of nettle juice when reacted with lead, alkali, and vitriol, suggesting these "hidden virtues" are the true basis of Solomon's ancient botanical wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>nettle, urtica, alchemy, lead, fixed alkali, vitriol, gray hair, Solomon, hyssop, cedar of Lebanon, chemistry</keywords>

### HIPPOCRATES.

...of such things, which our botanists promise to measure by the tongue <note>Meaning identifying a plant's properties solely by its taste</note>; regarding these, I say, one cannot speak of "hidden virtues."

<margin>(a) In the *Aphorisms*</margin>
This is what **Arnald of Villanova** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311) was a celebrated physician, alchemist, and translator</note> intended, even if for other reasons his works require correction; he says (a) that where **simples** <term>simplicia; medicinal ingredients consisting of only one natural substance, usually a plant</term> are readily available, it is a deception if anyone uses **compounds** <term>compositis; medicines made by mixing many different ingredients together</term>.

When I was formerly devoting my efforts to this study, vigorous help was provided to me by the distinguished and most skillful botanist and most diligent **pharmacist** <term>pharmacopola; an apothecary or preparer of medicines</term>, Giovanni Maria Ferro. He worked at the "Sign of Health" <note>original: "sub sanitatis signo"; likely the name of a specific, well-known pharmacy in Venice where Tachenius lived</note> in this city, where with singular care and dedication he gathered all kinds of plants in great heaps. I truly owe very much to his industry and am deeply grateful to him.

<margin>Powder for quartan fever</margin>
Through the help of this study, I also discovered a compound powder which, if it does not surpass the strength of **Cinchona** <note>original: "chinellæ"; also known as Jesuit's bark, the source of quinine, which was then a relatively new and miraculous discovery for treating malaria</note>, is at least its equal in treating **quartan fever** <term>quartana; a form of malaria where the fever returns every four days</term>. This is attested by those who purchased it at the "Sign of Health" and put it into practice.

### The spiritual representation of plants.

## Chapter 20.

<margin>(a) Chapter 23</margin> From the things already stated and shown, the foundation emerges upon which the **spiritual regeneration** <term>regeneratio; often called "palingenesis," this was a famous 17th-century alchemical concept where the ghost-like form of a plant was made to appear from its burnt remains</term> of plants ought to proceed. Joseph Duchesne <note>original: "Quercetanus" (c. 1544–1609), a prominent French physician and alchemist who championed the use of chemical medicines</note> (a) mentions this in his short book against the "Anonymous" writer. He says that he saw this himself *at the house of a certain physician in Krakow, who knew how to prepare the ashes of any plant so elegantly, and most precisely preserve their spirits—the authors of all their faculties <note>Meaning the chemical "spirits" responsible for the plant's medicinal properties</note>—of which he kept more than thirty in different glass vessels, closed with a **hermetic seal** <term>hermetico sigillo; an airtight seal made by melting the glass neck of a flask closed, named after the legendary father of alchemy, Hermes Trismegistus</term>. If someone asked to be shown a rose or a **marigold** <note>original: "calendulam"</note>, he would take the ash of that plant in its vessel—for instance, the vessel marked with the title of "Rose"—and move the bottom of the flask toward a lamp so that it might warm up a little. Then, that most fine and impalpable ash would, of its own accord, give forth the visible appearance of a rose, which one could see gradually growing, flourishing, and taking on its full form...*

<meta>catchword: of the whole [totius]</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the concept of plant palingenesis, describing an experiment where the "spirit" or "shadow" of a rose is resurrected from its ashes when heated.</summary>

<margin>
The common people burn by fire. The Hippocratic [chemist] by water.
</margin>
To a Hippocratic philosopher <note>Tachenius identifies himself as a "Hippocratic" chemist, meaning he bases his chemical theories on the ancient principles of balance found in the Hippocratic corpus</note>, this **spiritual resuscitation of plants** does not seem so very difficult, especially when it proceeds not by a violent fire, but by a gentle one, imitating nature as the Master teaches. I have devised a method which, however, I have not yet been able to confirm by experience—owing to the unsuitability of this location for such study—but I will set it down here for the curious.

I have shown plainly <note>original: "ad oculum," literally "to the eye"</note> from Hippocrates that **ACID** and **ALKALI** are nature’s architectural instruments, present in all sublunary things <term>sublunary; in the traditional pre-modern view of the universe, this refers to the Earth and everything below the moon, which is made of the four elements and is subject to change and decay</term>. When these are not subject to the hand of a craftsman, but to the governance of nature alone, then that subject grows and flourishes, as is testified by the most humble flint stone, of which I spoke in its proper place. If, therefore, it grows and flourishes, it is necessary that acid and

<margin>
<b>(b)</b> I have shown elsewhere that the Spirit is the bond joining the soul with the body.
</margin>
...alkali possesses a certain something **(b)** which directs its operations toward its intended ends; I call this the **RECTOR** elsewhere. Here, call it what you will, provided we understand the reality of it, for I will pick a fight with no one over mere words. These three, Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical texts were attributed</note>, that most ancient philosopher, called **Body**, **Spirit**, and **Soul**. And so, these three lie hidden in that earthly dwelling which represents to us the form of a seed. 

<margin>
Hermes, Pythagoras, and later Hippocrates taught that Nature is similar in every thing.
</margin>
Take, for example, the seed of this plant, the poppy <note>original: "papaveris"</note>, in full bloom. Let half a pound of it be gathered under a clear sky. It must be preserved in a temperate place until the coming spring. Then, in the evening, you must watch for a clarity in the sky that promises a serene night. During this night, the seed must be exposed upon a large glass plate with a raised rim so that nothing can fall off. This plate should be placed in a meadow or a garden fertile with various flowers and herbs (for unless the earth is fertile, you will only collect a sterile dew). 

In the morning, before sunrise, carefully remove the seed—now soaked with dew—from the glass plate and place it into a vial suitable for this purpose **(a)**, its mouth well-sealed with wax.

<margin>
<b>(a)</b> "They were small vessels," says Quercetanus. <note>Joseph Duchesne, known as Quercetanus (1544–1609), was a famous Paracelsian physician who claimed to have witnessed the "palingenesis" or rebirth of a rose from its ashes.</note>
</margin>

...you must preserve it, so that the dew does not evaporate. This dew is so light because of the <term>spirits</term> <note>In alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile, active substances that give life and motion to matter.</note> with which it is impregnated in the air. (I will show in the next chapter that these spirits are secretly <term>acid</term> and <term>volatile</term>, and are indeed the hidden food of life <margin>(a) Thus says the Cosmopolite <note>original: "Cā Coſmopolita." The <term>acid</term> and <term>alkali</term> <note>Tachenius’s central theory was that all things are composed of Acid and Alkali in various proportions.</note> acquire new powers from the dew and from the influence of the heavens above, which the **RECTOR** <note>The "governing principle" or internal intelligence of the substance described on the previous page.</note> will begin to direct. Thus, the process of exposure and storage should be repeated until the seeds show signs of germination and vitality.

...store [the dew] in a glass vessel, sealed very well. While it rests, it will deposit its dregs (formed from the tiny dust flying through the air). Pour as much of this pure dew over the seeds as will rise about a finger’s breadth, more or less, above them. Then, place it in a **vapor bath** <note>original: *balneum vaporosum*. A method of gentle heating using steam or water vapor to mimic the natural warmth of the earth.</note>. (For **Raymond** <note>Referring to Raymond Lully, a Majorcan philosopher and legendary figure in alchemy.</note> teaches (a) that what nature accomplishes through the heat of the Sun and the stars, the same can be done by the heat of fire—provided it is so tempered that it does not overpower the **motive and formative power** <note>The internal "architectural" force that directs the growth and shape of a living thing.</note> which resides in the matter influenced from above). Once the mouth of the vial is sealed with melted sulfur, the **acid** and the **alkali**, in the presence of moisture and heat, then begin to act upon one another. They shall be liberated from the earthy body of the seed in which they were enclosed. In the course of several days, this natural composition will turn green at the surface, just as stagnant waters do when affected by the surrounding heat of summer while sitting in the shade. Under that green skin, the union of the faculties is then perfected; these faculties, which are volatile, I have shown to exist together with the **ruler** <note>original: *rectore*. A guiding spiritual or chemical principle that governs the transformation.</note> from the inseparable acid (which of itself knows not how to die (b))...

...which union we may call **joint fermentation**.

The dwelling-place, or the body of the seed, will be a dead substance. Over the course of several days, it absorbs into itself the greenness—that is, the **bubbles of fermentation**—along with the excess liquid, just as we have seen in stagnant ponds blooming with green. Gradually, once it has reached **maturity** <note>In alchemy, maturity refers to the point where a substance has been fully "digested" or transformed by heat.</note>, it turns into a very fine powder similar to ash.

When this little vessel has grown warm, the lightness of the dew—as **Quercetanus** <note>Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a famous French physician and alchemist who claimed to have seen plants regrow from their ashes.</note> undoubtedly saw—having fermented together with the **ruler** <note>The "rector" or internal guiding spirit of the matter.</note> and the powers of the seeds, will raise up the **image of a poppy** <note>original: *papaveris iconem*. <margin>Salt-nitre <note>original: *hali nitrum*. A term used by Tachenius for a volatile salt or atmospheric impurity, often associated with the sea air.</note> is formed from the exhalation of putrid urine and various mixed substances joined together.</margin>

If the mouth of the vessel is sealed by melting the glass, I believe it will never perish. However, because practical work does not always follow theory word-for-word, I require a skillful and clever experimenter for this task. As I have said, thus far Venice has not been a convenient place for me to conduct this trial, nor will it be, due to the lack of fertile and pure dew, which is contaminated by **salt-nitre** (a) <note>Tachenius frequently complained that the salty, corrosive air of the Venetian lagoons interfered with delicate chemical operations.</note>.

### Chapter 21.

Alkali and <term>volatile acid</term> <note>Acidic components that evaporate or "fly away" when heated.</note>, within the plant family, are burned together by fire into a <term>fixed salt</term> <note>The stable, solid residue remaining after a substance has been burned at high heat.</note>, or into one that is fully alkalized. He also rebukes Hartmann <margin>Johannes Hartmann (1568–1631), the first professor of chemistry at a European university.</margin> (b) as the worst of writers, saying that nothing is taught more foolishly than to mix alkaline salts with acids. He says this as if he himself had not taught how to mix <term>viperine alkali</term> <note>An alkaline salt extracted from the bodies of vipers, believed to have potent medicinal properties.</note> with the acid spirit of salt <note>original: *spiritu salis*—known today as hydrochloric acid.</note>; and also how to mix the <term>spirit of vitriol</term> <note>Sulfuric acid.</note> with the alkalized salt of wormwood <note>Potassium carbonate derived from the wormwood plant.</note>, under the title of "coagulated spirit of vitriol" (c). <meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s investigation into the chemical composition of plants. Having previously critiqued other chemists' methods, he now explains his own theory on how "fixed salts" are created through the interaction of acid and alkali during combustion.</meta>

<header>TACHENIUS</header>

### What is to be Chosen.

<margin>The air causes things to fly away <note>original: *volatizat*—the process of turning a solid into a vapor or gas.</note>.</margin>
It must be understood that no vegetable matter possesses a <term>fixed salt</term> <note>A stable, alkaline salt that remains in the ashes after combustion and does not evaporate.</note>, of whatever kind it may be, unless it is burned with an actual fire. This must be done in such a way that the <term>acid</term>, through the process of searing, is able to act upon the <term>alkali</term>, and conversely the alkali upon the acid, allowing them to seize one another; I have already demonstrated this with the juice of wine and its <term>tartar</term> <note>A sediment found in wine casks, used by early chemists as a source of potassium carbonate.</note>. Rotten wood will serve as my witness: even if you were to reduce this to ashes with the greatest effort and by any fire you like, you will not extract any salt from it. For the acid and the alkali—both being <term>volatile</term>—have been consumed by the air, leaving behind nothing but a useless <term>cadaver</term> <note>original: *cadaver*—a term used by early chemists to describe the inert, "dead" matter left over after the active "spirits" or salts have been removed.</note>. However, if they are burned before they have rotted, then you will achieve your purpose.

<margin>This action deserves to be called "fermentation"; for the instruments of nature are indeed transmuted.</margin>
Therefore, the principal study and labor in this work will be to ensure that the herbs are fresh and not at all wilted. Then, surely, by burning them together, the innate moisture dissolves the acid and the alkali as it boils. Once dissolved, they act upon each other; one seizes the other, and both are <term>fixed</term> <note>The process of making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates when heated.</note> according to the specific property of the compound. However, the part that flies away with the flame, and from the a-

<meta>The text cuts off here at the catchword "cido," which completes the word "acido" (acid) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Tachenius explains that fixed salts are produced by the reaction between a plant's internal acids and alkalis during combustion. He warns that if a plant rots before burning, these volatile components escape into the air, leaving only a "dead" residue without medicinal salt.</summary>

...[not] seized by the acid, it turns into soot; from which you will extract a <term>volatile alkali</term> <note>An alkaline substance, like ammonia, that evaporates or "flies away" easily when heated.</note> by a simple method, but never a <term>fixed salt</term> <note>A stable salt that remains in the ashes and resists evaporation by fire.</note>—unless, by cleverness, it is reduced to stability <note>original: *constantiam*—referring to the state of being "fixed" or non-volatile.</note>.

This action deserves to be called **fermentation**; for indeed, both instruments of nature are transmuted into a single artificial one. Just as in the art of **soap-making** and **glass-making**, [the alkali] is turned into something neutral by the acid—its opposite; so it also happens in the human body when it is applied or administered by a wise and Hippocratic physician to its opposite. For the operation is the same, as the Master (a) <note>Referring to Hippocrates, the ancient Greek "Father of Medicine."</note> taught.

Let **Wormwood** <note>original: *Absinthium*—a famously bitter herb used in ancient and early modern medicine to treat digestion and parasites.</note> be our example. After this burning, these ashes are placed in a large, shallow earthen pot, or upon an iron plate or sheet with raised edges. <meta>This page continues Tachenius’s practical instructions for the chemical extraction of salts from Wormwood. The previous page ended with the initial heating of the plant matter; here, he details the purification of the resulting ashes into a medicinal salt.</meta>

[iron spa]tula, always moving the ashes until they become perfectly white. Place these into a pointed linen bag, or if there is only a small amount, into a pointed paper filter, and pour common water over them; when this water runs through, pregnant with salt, it is called **lye** <note>original: *lixivium*—a liquid solution made by leaching ashes, which extracts the alkaline salts.</note>. Coagulate this filtered lye in an iron frying pan (one not greased for cooking in the kitchen) by rapid boiling, and thicken it into a mass; and when it thickens like honey, stir it constantly with a spatula, and it will be dried into a grey powder. 

Immediately place this powder into an unglazed pot <note>Unglazed earthenware was preferred because chemical residues would not react with the pot's surface as they might with lead-based glazes.</note>, with the door of the **wind-furnace** <note>original: *fornaculo venti*—a furnace designed to use air currents to achieve specific, high temperatures.</note> closed, and cover it with small coals mixed with dust. Heat it gradually, barely to a brownish-red heat, but not so much that it melts; then let it cool. 

Throw the salt into a glass vessel so that it may be dissolved in sufficient water (a)

a. One ounce of salt to a pound of water settles best in this way.
</margin>
stir it several times with a wooden stick. Then allow the dissolved solution to rest for two or three days. Pour it off, clear of the dregs, without much shaking, and in a leaden or glass vessel placed in a **sand-bath** <note>original: *in arenam*—a method of heating a vessel evenly by surrounding it with hot sand.</note>, allow the water to evaporate without boiling, until a salt-crust <note>The catchword *cuti-* suggests the next page continues with *cuticula*, the "skin" or crust that forms on a liquid as it crystallizes.</note>

...until a film <note>original: *cuticula*—literally a "little skin," a thin crust that forms on the surface of a liquid when it becomes saturated through evaporation.</note> grows over the surface: then remove the vessel, and on the following morning collect the shining grains of salt, which must be washed with clear water in a quick movement and dried. A pound of this ash will yield nearly four ounces of the purest salt, but the ashes of dried herbs—four pounds of them—will scarcely yield an ounce of salt.

### TACHENIUS

...I have pointed out, which is sufficient. The **Reformer** <note>original: *Reformator*—Tachenius likely refers here to a specific chemical authority or a practitioner of the "new" chemistry who he believes over-processes materials.</note> teaches that the ashes must be boiled, the **lye** <note>original: *lixivium*—the solution obtained by leaching ashes</note> thickened to dryness, and afterwards committed to the fire of **fusion** <note>original: *fusionis*—the process of melting a substance into a liquid state using extreme heat.</note> without discretion (a). 

<margin>
(a) The Supplement <note>original: *mantissa*</note>, page 789.
</margin>

In this way, instead of a vegetable salt, an empty and caustic **alkali** always results. For **salt of wormwood** <note>original: *sal absinthij*</note>, in its fundamental nature, is salty because of its acid and is not entirely "fixed" <note>A "fixed" salt is one that does not evaporate or change when heated; Tachenius argues that wormwood salt should retain some of its volatile, medicinal qualities.</note>, as I have shown. But if it undergoes fusion, then both flavors <note>the acidic and the salty</note> act upon each other and are fixed into an alkali, just as happens with all salts prepared artistically from acids. 

Therefore, by the regulation of **fire** (b) alone, things are altered; they change their name, their virtue, and their efficacy. 

<margin>
(b) Fire is the public professor.
</margin>

This is the reason why the strengths of salts prepared by an ignorant person are never consistent. When they are indeed alkalized, they are praised by the unskillful—for example, salt of wormwood—for having been useful in **colic** <note>A term for severe, fluctuating abdominal pain.</note> (which was then caused by an acid, which the alkali had "drunk up" or neutralized). However, when the salt is prepared by an expert so that it remains salty-acid and still **volatile** <note>meaning it can still evaporate or react readily, preserving its "life" or "spirit."</note>, it is beneficial for the stomach and for a long-standing **obstruction** <note>original: *oppilatione*—a medical term for a blockage in the internal organs, such as the liver or spleen.</note>. In that case, it serves as the base for a powder for a **cachectic** <note>original: *cachectico*—referring to cachexia, a state of physical wasting and malnutrition caused by chronic disease.</note> patient...

# HIPPOCRATES.

<margin>a cachectic powder <note>original: *puluis cacheticus*—A medicinal powder used to treat "cachexia," a condition of physical wasting and general ill health.</note></margin>
(a) Of **Quercetanus** <note>original: *Quercetani*—Refers to Joseph Duchesne (c. 1544–1609), a famous French physician and "chymist" who followed the teachings of Paracelsus.</note>. They attribute the cause to harmful **humors** <note>The four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed in ancient medicine to determine health and temperament.</note>.

In this way, many things become **fixed** <note>A "fixed" substance is one that remains solid and does not evaporate or fly away when exposed to high heat.</note> if they are treated with the appropriate heat, according to the endurance of the material and its specific requirements—things which were otherwise **volatile**. Certainly, the fixation of blood requires one kind of heat and set of instruments, while another kind of skill is needed <margin>All things happen to them through Divine necessity. **Hippocrates**, Book 1 *On Diet*.</margin> when **nitre** <note>original: *nitrum*—Saltpeter, a key ingredient in gunpowder and many chemical experiments.</note> and **arsenic** are fixed. Another skill is needed when all the nitre is turned into "earth" by its scent alone; yet another vessel and fire are required when **Mercury** <note>Quicksilver.</note> by itself takes on a degree of fixity; and still other requirements exist when **antimony** and **sulfur** are fixed. Thus, there is also a different operation for salts, all of which are volatile, as I have demonstrated in the case of rotted wood.

<margin>The ingenious Venetians.</margin>
This is the reason why the most wise and prudent fathers of **Venice** order that the fresh wood, from which they construct their warships, be submerged in water for many years within their most ornate and well-equipped **Arsenal** <note>original: *Armamentario*—The Venetian Arsenal was a massive, world-famous complex of shipyards and armories that pioneered mass production.</note>. They do this so that the air cannot penetrate the wood, and under the water, the timbers become so "fixed" that they rival the hardness of stones.

But if indeed they are allowed to weaken in the open air...

...begin, before they are submerged, they ought to be moderately charred, so that the **acid** and **alkali** <note>Tachenius believed that all matter was composed of these two fundamental principles. Their "melting" together represents a state of chemical balance or "fixation."</note> may melt into one another and become as if encrusted; in this way, they are not corrupted nor do they rot for centuries. This is how stakes are encrusted when they are driven into the ground for the foundations of great buildings, so that they do not decay.

Gardeners on these islands <note>Tachenius practiced as a pharmacist in Venice, which is built on wooden piles driven into the marshy ground.</note> reduce herbs that have been dried in the air into ashes, and they sell these ashes to pharmacists in good faith. When the pharmacists extract little or no salt from them, they rebuke the gardeners, saying the ashes were worthless. The gardeners reply that while transporting the ashes, seawater must have washed the salt away. Many easily believe this, being ignorant of the true cause.

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s discussion on the chemical transformation of substances. ### HIPPOCRATES.

...[re]former, then they change <margin>When one breaks off the wings and the earth is prepared for the water bath. a. On Exodus.</margin> their nature and are turned into a glass-forming alkali <term>alkali: a basic salt; here, Tachenius refers to its ability to fuse into glass when heated</term>, as Zoar <note>Likely referring to the *Zohar*, a foundational work of Jewish mysticism, or an alchemical authority associated with it.</note> noted (b); which, however, before they passed through the fire, was neither present in the solids nor fixed. This substance, in turn, through inversion, not only takes on volatility <note>The ability of a substance to evaporate or turn into gas.</note> but is even reduced into the elements, as I have shown regarding the **alkali of tartar** <term>alkali of tartar: potassium carbonate, often derived from the crusty deposits in wine barrels</term>.

But if indeed alkalis are cooked with their own distilled oils in the right proportion and for the necessary time, by an ingenious and hidden circulation <note>A process of continuous distillation where the vapor returns to the liquid.</note> in a suitable vessel, they produce a **wonderful essence** <note>original: *ens mirabile*—a term for a highly potent or perfected medicinal substance</note>. Thus, oil of Cinnamon, absorbed <margin>Oil is thus salt.</margin> by its own alkali (though it be stripped of its "garments" <note>An alchemical metaphor for removing the external or impure parts of a substance to reveal its inner essence.</note>), achieves as much as is hoped for in **paralysis** and similar diseases. Oil of tartar, being volatile and fatty, becomes **soap** when combined with its own alkali in the right proportion.

When a certain one-eyed man <margin>Evil does not depart from the house of the ungrateful.</margin> <note>original: *monoculus*</note> once heard this example regarding Tartar from me by word of mouth for the sake of information, he immediately cooked **viperine alkali** <note>original: *alcali viperinum*—an alkali derived from the remains of vipers, used in early modern medicine</note> with oil of tartar—substances of a different nature—in a crude manner, or rather with even less...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the chemical interaction between oils and alkalis. Tachenius is explaining that oils contain a hidden acidity, and that by removing this acidity—either through distillation, heat, or the action of alkalis—the oil becomes a more effective preservative or medicine.</meta>

### HIPPOCRATES.

<margin>Oil of bricks <note>original: "Oleum laterinum," a traditional remedy made by distilling bricks soaked in oil</note> is dry and warming.</margin>

**Alkalis** act upon it and consume it; otherwise **soap** would never be made. For distilled oil of olives corrodes and dissolves silver imperceptibly when it is cast into it for a short time; this would not happen unless **acidity** were present within it. The remaining thick oil preserves iron from rust better, insofar as the aforementioned acid has been removed through distillation. Under the force of fire, it turns into an alkalized charcoal, which is recognized by the eye upon the pouring on of a certain **acidic spirit** <note>original: "acidi spiritus"; the translator refers here to the bubbling or effervescence that occurs when an acid hits an alkali</note>. This is the reason why the Overseers of the **Armory** <note>original: "Armamentarii"; likely referring to the famous Venetian Arsenal, as Tachenius practiced in Venice</note> of this most noble city, before they order weapons to be anointed, allow the oil to evaporate to nearly half its volume over a gentle fire.

<margin>Thick olive oil resembles **Alkali**.</margin>

Likewise, the common people, when suffering sharp pains (which mostly arise from acids), seek to be anointed with the oil that burns before sacred images, and this mitigates the pain. The superstitious people of **Greece** <note>Tachenius likely refers to the Greek Orthodox community in Venice or the Levant who used oil from icon lamps for healing</note> attribute this grace to the image for which the lamp burns. The cause, however, is that the oil, having thickened in the **lamp**, has lost its thinner **acidity** through the flame

<meta>This page continues Tachenius's explanation of why oils used in lamps or aged for long periods become effective medical treatments. He argues that the heat of the lamp or the passage of time removes the "acid" from the oil, leaving behind a "thick" substance dominated by "alkali," which can then draw out painful acids from the human body.</meta>

# TACHENIUS

part: being thick and void of acid, yet intolerant of new acid, it seeks to be saturated once again. It finds this acid in the aching limb, extracts it, and receives it into itself; thus, the pain ceases, or at least is mitigated.

<margin>Why old oil becomes rancid.</margin>
The common people believe that very old oil takes on the nature of a balsam. This is because oil kept for a hundred years in the **Castle of Brescia** <note>original: "Castello Brixiensi"; Brescia is a city in northern Italy, then part of the Venetian Republic</note> relieves many of pains caused by **acid**. In this oil, due to its great age, the **alkali** has partially suppressed the acid. This is also why it eventually becomes rancid. Therefore, venerable antiquity decreed that old oil should be used in medicinal compositions for external use.

<margin>Rotten apples.</margin>
For the same reason, rotten apples are more cooling than cooked ones; this is why we call them **anodynes** <note>original: "anodina," from the Greek for "pain-reliever." Tachenius suggests that as the apple decays, its internal chemistry changes to neutralize pain-causing acids.</note>.

<margin>Butter.</margin>
Fresh butter contains more alkali than acid; therefore, it is more cooling than oil. For this reason, the **School of Salerno** <note>original: "scola Salernitana," the famous medieval medical school whose health precepts were widely quoted</note> says:

> *Butter soothes, moistens, and relaxes without causing fever.* <note>original: "Lenit & humectat soluit sine febre butirum."</note>

For that reason, butter does not coagulate in milk unless the milk first turns sour <note>original: "acescat"; becomes acidic</note>. Furthermore, if a **fixed alkali** is added during the making of butter,

# HIPPOCRATES.

...which consumes the **acid**, butter is not formed; for acid must coagulate **alkali**.

Thus also **alkalized salts** flow and dissolve very easily in the air <note>this refers to "deliquescence," where a salt absorbs enough atmospheric moisture to turn into a liquid</note>, but as soon as acid is poured on so that the alkali is overcome, they harden and coagulate.

<margin>Acidic sulfur.</margin>
**Sulfur**, in its oiliness, contains a hidden acid; it dissolves most readily not only in oil but also in alkali, yet never in an obvious acid, however powerful. But if it is burned under a **glass bell** <note>original: "vitria campana"; a bell-shaped glass vessel used by early chemists to capture vapors</note>, then that hidden acidity turns into smoke, which is condensed into a most acidic juice <note>This "juice" is what we now know as sulfuric acid, produced by burning sulfur and collecting the vapors</note>; and the alkali, which previously received the sulfur in a friendly manner, is now destroyed by it, and a "new creature" is made, entirely different from the first.

Moreover, sulfur is praised by **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, an ancient Greek physician and the father of pharmacology</note> for ailments of the lungs; to which others add that sulfur is the "soul" of the lungs. Understand that when it is evacuated by the **Hippocratic** method and fixed into a tasteless and milky earth <note>This refers to the preparation of "Milk of Sulfur" (lac sulphuris), a fine white precipitate used in 17th-century medicine</note>, it then becomes impatient with its emptiness and desires to be satisfied again by its own kind (a): so greedily—

Reduce the charcoal left behind into ashes; from these, with common water, extract the **alkali**, which its own vinegar again drinks in and absorbs. Thus <margin>All in all.</margin> ***all things are in all things***: that is, you will find acid and alkali everywhere.

->HIPPOCRATES. 161<-

...saturated: just as I have shown similar things to be done by the art <note>The "art" refers to chemistry or alchemy</note>; for it drinks into itself the stinking and corrosive acidity of **aqua fortis** <note>original: "aqua fortis"; literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid</note>, which suffocates by its smell alone 
<margin>The frequent smell of **aqua fortis** generates **atrophy** <note>original: "atrophia"; a wasting away of the body</note> along with the most bitter pains. In the same way, that malignant, poisonous, stinking, corrosive, and suffocating acid—which in acute diseases fluctuates here and there through the veins and exhausts patients without pause—is immediately absorbed by **camphor** <margin>camphor</margin> when given by mouth; the **vital spirit** <note>In historical medicine, the "vital spirit" was believed to be a subtle substance that carried life-force and heat from the heart through the arteries</note> becomes purer and more lively and acquires vigor: which among practitioners, **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose medical encyclopedia was the standard text in Europe for centuries</note> was the first to observe; whence he rightly calls and celebrates it as a **theriac** <note>original: "theriaca"; a famous ancient poly-pharmaceutical traditionally used as a universal antidote</note> against "hot" poisons: the vital spirit, he says, "acquires from it a refinement and a powerful luminosity, and for that reason it brings joy and strengthens [the heart]": I add, that the common saying does not correspond to this.

<margin>*from the book of precepts.*</margin>
Camphor thus dissolved in **aqua fortis** <note>original: "aqua forti"; nitric acid</note>, and precipitated or rather reduced with simple water, becomes a **soap**. When women’s hands are anointed with this and afterwards washed with water, it consumes that sharpness which irritates the skin and renders them soft and milky: a singular ornament for the female sex.
