<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s discussion on the chemical nature of substances, transitioning from camphor to antimony. He is illustrating his theory that substances are composed of an "acid" and an "alkali," and how they interact with the human body and the atmosphere.</meta>

### HIPPOCRATES.

<margin>Antimony:</margin>
...mony, <note>Completing the word "Antimony" from the previous page.</note> devoid of its own and innate **acid**, consists in a subtle, poisonous state of almost no weight. This is demonstrated by its "glass" <note>Glass of antimony: a translucent, reddish-yellow substance made by roasting and melting antimony ore.</note> or the powder called **Mercury of Life** <note>original: "Mercurius vitae"; a potent chemical compound (antimony trichloride) used as a medicine.</note>. From this, when mixed with an acidic wine (which is like it, rather than a sweet wine), a poison is extracted that the stomach loathes. Therefore, when this wine is taken by mouth, it is rejected by the vital force with great violence through vomiting and purging. By accident, it sometimes removes diseases of the **first region** <note>The gastrointestinal tract.</note>. Hence, Helmont <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a famous Flemish chemist and physician.</note> (a) <margin>(a) From *On Fevers*, chapter 15, section 6.</margin> remarks: "As long as Antimony provokes vomiting, it is not the remedy of a good man."

But when that poison is removed from the antimony, either by the flame of a fire or by ignited **saltpeter** <note>original: "nitro"; potassium nitrate, used in alchemy as a powerful reactant.</note> (which I have discussed in its proper place), then the **alkali** <note>In this context, the reactive "base" of the mineral.</note> of the antimony is saturated again with those aforementioned acidic spirits. However, since these spirits come from the saltpeter or the flame, they are not inherent to the antimony itself. This is so much the case that, over the course of days, it eventually draws its own likeness from the air, (b) <margin>(b) The exhausted mineral of **vitriol** <note>Metal sulfates, such as copper or iron sulfate.</note> in Carinthia refills itself in the open air within a few years; as does the exhausted mineral of saltpeter in Padua; therefore...</margin> just as I said regarding flint, which draws from the air not the life of a dog <note>original: "vitae Canis"; this may be a metaphorical reference to a base or animalistic vitality, or a specific alchemical concept regarding the "starry" influence of the Dog Star, Sirius.</note>, but nourishment similar to itself...

<summary>Tachenius discusses the chemical properties of Antimony, arguing that its emetic (vomit-inducing) effects are due to a "poisonous" state that can be altered through chemical processes. He also introduces the idea that minerals can "refill" their potency by absorbing similar substances from the open air.</summary>

# TACHENIUS’S

<margin>The **World-Soul** <note>original: "anima mundi"</note>, which is called the multiform spirit by the wise.</margin>

...as I have shown in the proper place: so that, whereas it previously gently induced sweats and urine, it now excites vomiting just like the **glass of antimony** <note>original: "antimonii vitrum"</note> itself. Therefore the flame, as well as the acidity of the **saltpeter** <note>original: "nitri"</note>, just as they are not poisons, so they are not inherent to the now evacuated and empty antimony. And so, antimony reduced to an **alkali** <note>In 17th-century chemistry, an "alkali" was the reactive base that neutralized "acids."</note> and stripped of all taste—which they call **diaphoretic** <note>original: "diaphoreticum"; a medicine used to induce sweating to purge the body of "ill humors."</note>—if it finds anything poisonous in the human body, it attracts it and becomes saturated by it. For nature rejoices in a nature similar to its own (b), and it retains that poison and rejoices in it more than if it were wandering here and there in the human body.

<margin>b. For this reason, the alkali of **Arsenic** is praised by **Basil** <note>Basil Valentine, the legendary alchemist to whom Tachenius often refers.</note> as a treasure of health; however, I do not advise anyone but the learned to handle it.</margin>

Therefore, this empty and diaphoretic Antimony is useful in no diseases except those which manifest some **acidic poison**, whether hidden or manifest, such as the **plague**, **burning fevers** <note>original: "causones"; a term for high fevers accompanied by intense thirst.</note>, **spotted fevers** <note>original: "petechiales"; fevers characterized by purple skin spots, often associated with typhus.</note>, and various internal **abscesses** <note>original: "aposthemata"</note>, wherever they may exist. When their poisonous breath is absorbed by the Antimony, the pores then open spontaneously, and it becomes a diaphoretic; otherwise, it never does.

<margin>c. In the very ancient *Repetition of the Stone*, page 80 in my copy.</margin>

Hence **Basil** (c) says of that powder:

### HIPPOCRATES.

...possesses remarkable powers for exterminating many long-standing and ulcerated diseases, and so on. For the acidities of these diseases are truly **putrefactive** <note>tending to cause rot or decay</note> and **escharotic** <note>original: "escaroticae"; caustic substances that burn the flesh and produce a scab</note>; this powder truly absorbs and consumes them, and, as I have said, the contraction of the pores is relaxed. (The common folk call this action "drying"; for this reason, it is also an ingredient in that plaster which **Paracelsus** <note>The influential 16th-century physician who pioneered the use of minerals in medicine.</note> calls by the barbaric name **oppodeldoch** <note>original: "oppodeldoch"; a famous medicinal plaster or salve used for healing wounds and soothing pains.</note>. And although pearls, corals, and those remedies called **Cardiaca** <note>remedies intended to stimulate or support the heart</note> also consume the acids that have arisen around the heart, these [acids] are nonetheless not putrefactive, nor are they joined to a hidden poison. Therefore, the acid which the empty and **alkalized** <note>chemically transformed into a "base" or alkali to neutralize acids</note> antimony absorbs is one thing <margin>Heat is the general term used by the common folk for this.</margin>. Another is the acid that pearls absorb during palpitations of the heart. Another is what **crab’s eyes** <note>original: "oculi cancri"; these are not literal eyes, but calcium carbonate stones found in the stomachs of crayfish, highly valued in the 17th century as antacids.</note> absorb in those who are wounded and bruised. Another is what **goat’s blood** <note>original: "sanguis hirci"; traditionally dried and powdered as a remedy for sharp chest pains.</note> absorbs in those suffering from **pleurisy** <note>original: "pleuriticis"; an inflammation of the lining of the lungs.</note>. Another...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how various alkaline substances neutralize specific harmful acids in the body. It follows a list that previously included iron and the bezoar stone, now transitioning into the mechanical and chemical ways the body reacts to foreign irritants.</meta>

<page-num>167</page-num>

### HIPPOCRATES.

...in the body, just like a wedge driven by a hammer. They do not notice that once the foreign flavor is removed by a small stone (or something similar), the contraction of the pores is relaxed, and then the whole body breathes through its own pores, as **Hippocrates** taught. This proves the **Old Man’s** <note>A respectful reference to Hippocrates, the "Father of Medicine."</note> doctrine: consider a fly in the eye; the eyelids contract immediately with great force, not because the fly itself pulls them shut, but because the senses recoil at the foreign intruder. As soon as the fly is removed, however, the eyelid relaxes again with softness.

I say there is another acid which **Paracelsus’s lily** <note>original: "Paracelsi lilium"; an alchemical preparation, typically a tincture of metals like antimony or gold, named for the "lily" or purity it was meant to restore.</note> [absorbs] in **dropsy** <note>original: "Hydrope"; a historical term for edema or swelling caused by excess fluid in the body.</note>. 

Another is what the **spiritual salt of Tartar** <note>original: "spirituale sal Tartari"; potassium carbonate refined from wine lees. It was called "spiritual" when purified through alchemical processes to make it more potent.</note> [absorbs] in **hypochondriac melancholy** <note>A condition believed to be caused by digestive vapors from the upper abdomen (the hypochondria) affecting the mind and mood.</note> and all other conditions full of such afflictions. 

Another is what **bone-glue stone** <note>original: "osteocolla"; a type of limestone deposit found on plant roots, which was crushed and used in traditional medicine to help heal broken bones.</note> [absorbs] in bone fractures. 

Another is what **corals**, with their specific alkali, [absorb] in **gonorrhea** <note>In the 17th century, this term was used more broadly for various types of abnormal bodily discharges or "flows."</note>; they consume and absorb it, etc. 

However, the stomach’s own natural and evident acid which I have demonstrated—by which it digests and transforms food into a vital **acid chyle** <note>original: "chilum"; the milky fluid produced in the stomach during the first stage of digestion.</note>—is not consumed by the aforementioned simple substances (indeed...

<meta>The text cuts off here, continuing with the catchword "nec" (neither/nor) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>Tachenius explains that while specific substances like coral, bone-glue, and alchemical salts neutralize harmful acids associated with diseases, they do not destroy the stomach's natural acid necessary for digestion. He uses the metaphor of a fly in the eye to illustrate how the body's "senses" react to irritants by contracting and relaxing.</summary>

In this way, as my teacher Hippocrates shows, nature itself proceeds. It takes food filled with volatile alkali and, with the acid of the stomach, first converts it into a clearly acidic chyle <term>chyle</term><note>A milky fluid consisting of lymph and emulsified fats, formed in the small intestine during digestion.</note>. Then, in the subsequent "stomachs" <note>original: "ventriculis"; Tachenius uses this term to refer to the various stages or chambers of the digestive and metabolic process.</note>, the alkali is coagulated according to the specific properties of the hidden acid which those cavities harbor within themselves. 

<margin>Local ferments, which they call "mummy-like" <note>original: "mumialia"; in early modern medicine, "mumia" referred to the vital or balsamic force inherent in a living body or its parts.</note>.</margin>
Thus, the alkali from the feces of the bowels—even if it were purified a hundred times—will spontaneously become foul, as I have shown in the appropriate place. The odor is inseparable from the alkali of urine, yet the alkali of a healthy man's sweat does not stink.

<margin>By this same path, the "soap of the wise" <note>original: "sapo sapientum"; a specialized alchemical solvent or substance believed to be more potent than common soap.</note> is also prepared.</margin>
Similarly, the formation of a stone <term>calculus</term><note>A mineral deposit or "stone" formed in the body, such as a kidney or bladder stone.</note> is its own specific process, as I showed in my discussion of its dissolution and hardening. It is necessary—and reason dictates—that the acid which hardens the alkali in the bones is different from the acid in the blood, or that in the veins, the hair, or the nails. Otherwise, indeed, any substance could be generated from any other substance without order.

So too does soap made with oil differ from that...

<margin>
**Calcination of Gold** <note>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical state, often to reduce it to a powder.</note> in the **dry way**. <note>original: "via sicca." In alchemy, the "dry way" involves high heat and crucibles, as opposed to the "wet way" involving liquid solvents.</note>
</margin>
From **nitre** <note>saltpeter</note> and **common salt**, four ounces of each, and two ounces of **alum**, let a powder be made. To this add at least two drachms of **gold leaf**; mix the gold with the salts and cook in a small glazed pot until dry. Toward the end, increase the fire until it breathes out a scent like **aqua fortis** <note>original: "aquae fortis." Literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid.</note>; in this way, the saltiness of the mixture erodes the gold. [Put] the white mass into a small [pot...]

He is refuting the "reformer" mentioned on the previous page, specifically arguing that salts do not weaken acids but are necessary for certain reactions, such as the creation of "purple gold." The text transitions from the "dry way" (using heat) to the "wet way" (using liquid solvents).</meta>

<header>TACHENIUS</header>
<page-num>178</page-num>
<meta>catchword: Hinc (Hence)</meta>

...[capsu]le <note>The text likely completes the word "capsula" from the previous page, referring to the small glazed pot.</note>, dissolve it in common water. Let the dissolved substance rest in a glass vessel until the dregs of the salts settle to the bottom; then pour off the clear liquid by tilting the vessel. Into this clear solution, add drop by drop a certain dissolved **fixed alkali** <note>A stable alkaline substance that does not evaporate when heated, such as potassium carbonate.</note>; for example, **liquor of Tartar** <note>A solution of potassium carbonate.</note>, which immediately absorbs the more subtle acid spirits, and instantly the **gold** falls as a purple-colored powder. In this operation, the salt did not hinder the acid spirits, as the reformer <note>The rival theorist Tachenius is criticizing.</note> teaches; rather, the alkali of tartar, by absorbing the acids, destroyed the chemical compound.

<margin>
**Calcination of Gold** in the **wet way**. <note>original: "via humida." A chemical process using liquid solvents rather than dry heat.</note>
</margin>
In the same way, but with greater force, water distilled from **nitre** <note>saltpeter</note> and **alum**, in which **common salt** has been dissolved, will dissolve gold; for unless the salt were dissolved in the water, it would not corrode the gold. Therefore, salt does not destroy the acid **aqua fortis** <note>Nitric acid; literally "strong water."</note>, neither in the dry nor in the wet way; instead, it adds vigor to it, making it similar to **sal ammoniac** <note>Ammonium chloride; when added to nitric acid, it creates "aqua regia," the only solvent capable of dissolving gold.</note>. Hence, **Basil Valentine** <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, supposedly a Benedictine monk, whose works were highly influential in the 17th century.</note> says:

->*What is found in the eagle*<- <note>original German: "VVas im adler VVird gefunden." In alchemical symbolism, the "Eagle" often represents the volatile spirit or sal ammoniac.</note>
->*Clearly*<-
->*That is also within me*<-
->*Wholly and completely.*<-

<summary>Tachenius describes the chemical precipitation of gold into a purple powder (Purple of Cassius) and argues that common salt enhances the power of acids to dissolve gold, rather than weakening them as his opponent claims.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Tachenius, alchemy, gold calcination, Purple of Cassius, fixed alkali, liquor of Tartar, aqua fortis, sal ammoniac, Basil Valentine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s argument that salts are essential to the action of acids in dissolving gold. He is moving from theoretical explanation to a specific practical experiment in Chapter 23 to demonstrate that salts and acids work in harmony rather than being opposites.</meta>

<header>HIPPOCRATES <note>This refers to the title of Tachenius's work, *Hippocrates Chimicus*, where he frames his chemical theories as a revival of ancient Hippocratic wisdom.</note></header>
<page-num>179</page-num>

Hence it is clear that **gold** is not dissolved except by salty substances. But why does the **acid spirit of salt** <note>original: "acidus salis spiritus"; this refers to hydrochloric acid gas or its aqueous solution.</note> dissolve this? I have stated and demonstrated this in its proper place: namely, that the spirit, through its corrosive action, returns to that which it was from its birth—that is, into salt.

->*Obscure things become clear.*<-

### Chapter 23.

I shall present another experiment, most pleasing to the eyes, by which I will show that salty substances are not the opposites of acids.

<margin>Tincture of Gold.</margin>
Take three pounds of dried **vitriol** <note>Sulfates of iron or copper, commonly used in early chemistry to produce strong acids.</note>, two pounds of **nitre** <note>Saltpeter, or potassium nitrate.</note>, six ounces of **flowers of sulfur** <note>A fine powder produced by heating sulfur until it turns to vapor and then collecting the deposits.</note>, and five and a half pounds of **common salt**. Add as much **brick dust** <note>Used as a physical separator to prevent the salts from melting into a solid mass during the heating process.</note> as is sufficient so that the salts do not melt together with the sulfur—let there be six pounds of this powder. Mix these fine powders thoroughly and distill the water <note>In this context, "water" refers to the acidic liquid produced by the distillation.</note> using a **sand bath** <note>original: "ex arena"; a vessel of sand heated to provide a steady, even temperature to the glass equipment.</note>. Into this water, without any prior **rectification** <note>The process of purifying or strengthening a spirit through a second distillation.</note>, place gold coins. Within a few hours, in a warm place, you will see the water tinted with a golden color.

<margin>*Sulfur of Antimony.*</margin>
Thus also simple strong water <note>original: "aqua fortis"; a historical name for nitric acid, a highly corrosive mineral acid.</note> made from niter and alum, or vitriol, does not corrode or dissolve raw antimony; but add to this water as much fused salt—that is, clean and pure <margin>*Antimony ore from the Rauris mountains <note>A region in the Austrian Alps famous for its gold mines during the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>, with red veins or spots, when dissolved in this manner, yields gold.*</margin> and pulverized—as it can dissolve. Pour this water over the antimony, which has been at least coarsely crushed, about the size of millet grains; immediately, with gentle heat, it acts upon the antimony, whose regulus <note>original: "regulum"; the metallic part of a mineral that remains after the sulfur has been removed.</note> it corrodes. Immediately add cold water to this, and you will see the sulfur separated, which floats on top of the water like pitch. Note, however, that you should not attempt to dissolve more than about two drachms <note>original: "dragmas"; an ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.9 grams.</note> at a single time; otherwise, in a larger quantity, the sulfur is also corroded by the water and will turn into dregs.

Purify <note>original: "edulcora"; literally "to sweeten," but in historical chemistry, it means to wash a precipitate with water to remove salts or acids.</note> this sulfur with cold water, and in appearance it will be similar to common sulfur, except that it turns slightly green. And this separation does not succeed without salt; therefore, it did not hinder the acids.

**TACHENIUS**

...so that you may be instructed by his own writings, where he offers himself.

<margin>a. Mantissa, page 801.</margin>
### The true preparation of Corrosive Sublimate of Mercury (a)

"Good wine," [the poet says] "needs no ivy"; <note>original: "Vinum vendibile Hedera non indiget"; an old proverb meaning that a good product needs no advertisement. In antiquity, an ivy bush hung outside a tavern signaled that wine was for sale.</note> therefore, why he adds *the true preparation* to this title, I do not see! I suspect that the reformer, in his usual manner, is deceitfully imposing upon us with this empty promise; let us go out to meet him.

Mercury, he says, is correctly **sublimed** <note>To heat a solid until it turns into vapor and then back into a solid, a common way to purify chemicals.</note> with salt and **vitriol** <note>original: "vitriolo"; iron or copper sulfates used to produce strong acids.</note> [it is a wonder that he does not fear the destruction of the acidic vitriol by the salt], but because he found it written thus by others, he at least adds: *The Vitriol must be cautiously **calcined**, <note>To heat a substance to a high temperature to remove moisture or volatile parts.</note> lest it be deprived of its acid spirit.* He seems to think he is advising something of great importance here. As also: that the sublimation should be repeated two or three times with new species—namely salt and vitriol. But for what purpose is this repeated sublimation with new materials? For once the Mercury is saturated with **acid spirits**, <note>original: "acidis spiritibus"; the volatile acidic vapors released during the heating of the vitriol.</note> why is the labor repeated? Especially if it is true—as it is most true in nature—that all things are governed by certain numbers, weights, and measures. <note>A reference to the Book of Wisdom 11:20, a foundational concept for early chemists who believed God created the world through precise mathematical and chemical laws.</note>

<summary>Tachenius critiques a contemporary's method for preparing corrosive sublimate, arguing that the "reformer's" instructions for repeated sublimation are redundant and show a lack of understanding of chemical saturation.</summary>
<keywords>corrosive sublimate, mercury, vitriol, sublimation, calcination, chemical saturation, 17th-century chemistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s critique of a contemporary "reformer's" chemical methods. Having just questioned the purpose of repeating the sublimation of mercury with salt and vitriol, Tachenius now employs classical literature and scripture to argue that such repetition is both scientifically redundant and a waste of resources.</meta>

# HIPPOCRATES. 187

...according to that saying of the poet:
> *There is a measure in things, there are, in short, fixed limits,*
> *beyond and short of which the right cannot find a place.*
<note>original: "Est modus in rebus, sunt certi denique fines. Quos ultra citraque nequit consistere rectum." This is a famous quote from the Roman poet Horace (*Satires* 1.1.106), advising moderation and recognizing natural boundaries.</note>

In the same way, the nature of mercury, once it has been saturated with **acid spirits**, <note>original: "acidis spiritibus"; volatile acidic vapors derived from minerals like vitriol (sulfates).</note> absorbs and drinks up as much as it can carry and as much as it requires from the first **sublimation**. <note>The process of heating a substance into vapor and collecting the solid deposit that forms upon cooling.</note> Even if it is then sublimed a hundred times, either by itself or with new **species**, <note>original: "speciebus"; here meaning chemical ingredients or reagents.</note> the sublimed mercury always remains the same. Therefore, to repeat the labor is to lose time and materials uselessly. This is what the Wise Man says in the Proverbs: (a)

<margin>Chapter 20, verse 10.</margin>
> *Diverse weights and diverse measures are both alike an abomination before God.*
<note>original: "Abominabile apud Deum pondus & pondus, mensura & mensura." Tachenius uses this biblical warning against dishonest trade to mock the "reformer" for his inconsistent and redundant chemical measurements.</note>

I know that many desire a method for subliming mercury because of the fear that the mercury sold here and there might have **arsenic** mixed into it. However, as it is a dangerous commodity, arsenic cannot be added to mercury during its sublimation because of the **subtle poison** <note>original: "subtile venenum"; "subtle" here refers to the fine, easily inhaled, and insidious nature of the toxic vapors.</note> which it exhales during the heating process, suffocating the workers. I shall demonstrate that the poison is subtle from my own danger. <note>Tachenius likely refers to a personal lab accident where he was nearly poisoned by toxic fumes.</note>

<meta>catchword: Once,</meta>

<summary>Tachenius argues that mercury has a natural limit for acid absorption, making repeated chemical processing wasteful, and warns of the deadly respiratory risks of arsenic vapors based on his own experience.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s discussion on the chemical nature of substances, transitioning from a theoretical critique of mercury sublimation to a personal, cautionary account of his dangerous experiments with arsenic. He recounts a near-fatal poisoning incident while following the instructions of Johann Agricola.</meta>

# TACHENIUS. 188

<margin>Arsenic</margin>
Once, at the urging of **Johann Agricola**, I attempted to **sublime** arsenic so many times that it would finally remain **fixed** at the bottom of the vessel.
<note>Johann Agricola (1590–1668) was a prominent German physician and chemist. Sublimation is the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then collecting the solid deposit that forms as it cools. Tachenius was trying to make the arsenic "fixed," meaning stable and non-volatile under heat.</note>
At that time, driven by a desire for knowledge, I feared neither smoke, nor vapors, nor any other danger. After many sublimations, when I had opened the vessel, I breathed in a scent that was so smooth, sweet, and pleasant to the palate that, to my wonder, I had never experienced anything like it before. 

But after half an hour, I felt my stomach aching and cramping, accompanied by convulsions in all my limbs. I breathed with difficulty and passed blood in my urine with unspeakable burning. Immediately after, I was seized by **colic** pain, and I remained entirely bent over for the space of an hour and a half. 
<note>Colic refers to severe, spasmodic pain in the abdomen.</note>

Restored by **milk and oil**, I felt somewhat better; nevertheless, a slow fever, similar to a **hectic** fever, persisted through the entire winter. 
<note>Milk and oil were traditional antidotes used to coat the stomach and induce the expulsion of poisons. A "hectic" fever (febris lenta) was a term for a lingering, wasting fever.</note>
I eventually extinguished this with decoctions of healing herbs and by eating cabbage sprouts, softened in boiling water and seasoned with **orange juice**, oil, and a little salt. Indeed, na—
<note>original: "cimarum brassicæ... Auranciorum succo." Cabbage was often used in medicinal diets, and citrus juice was prized for its "cooling" and "cleansing" properties in humoral medicine.</note>

<summary>Tachenius recounts a harrowing personal experiment where he nearly died from inhaling arsenic fumes while attempting to "fix" the element. He describes his symptoms of poisoning and his eventual recovery through a specific diet of milk, oil, and seasoned cabbage.</summary>

<keywords>arsenic poisoning, Johann Agricola, sublimation, fixation, early modern medicine, alchemy accidents, medicinal diet, colic</keywords>

<margin>
Geber on **Silver**, chapter 18, and the son of B. on the properties of the seven metals, regarding **Jupiter** <note>In alchemy, "Jupiter" is the planetary symbol and code name for the metal Tin.</note>. 
<term>Tin</term> produces **silver**.
</margin>
But despite the danger, there are those who burn pure **tin** <term>stannum</term> into a powder, which can be reduced back into **tin** by no common art, unlike other metals; however, with **arsenic** <term>arsenicum</term> it becomes **slag** <term>scoria</term> <note>The dross or stony refuse separated from metals during the smelting or refining process.</note>, a part of which, by a singular skill, transforms into pure **silver** <term>argentum</term>. This art of separation was known and practiced by **Sigismund Wan**, a citizen of **Wunsiedel** in the **Vogtland** <note>A historical region in Germany covering parts of Saxony, Thuringia, and Bavaria. Wan was a historical figure known for his wealth derived from mining and metallurgy.</note>, with such great profit that in the year 1464, he built and endowed a most magnificent hospital there, as **Caspar Bruschius** <note>A 16th-century German humanist and historian known for his chronicles of German territories.</note> relates; this may still be seen today, along with the epitaph of the aforementioned citizen.

<margin>
In the second volume of the *Chemical Theater* <note>original: *Theatrum Chimicum*, a massive and famous 17th-century compendium of alchemical writings.</note>, page 39.
</margin>
Moreover, that **silver** is made from **tin** with **arsenic** is confirmed by **Clavius** in his *Apology* against **Erastus** <note>Thomas Erastus (1524–1583) was a physician and critic of Paracelsian alchemy; Clavius here defends the chemical possibility of certain transmutations.</note>, which should be consulted. And having explained these things regarding **arsenic**, it is enough for those who are curious.

Now I return to **mercury** <term>mercurium</term>, from which I had digressed, which is sublimated in this proportion.

### OF TACHENIUS
<meta>catchword: of</meta>

A single pack of **Mercury** or **Quicksilver** weighs exactly 280 pounds, just as they are transported in sheepskins from **Idrija** <note>original: *Hidria*; a town in modern-day Slovenia famous for one of the world's largest mercury mines.</note>. To prepare the mixture, take 20 pounds of **sublimated mercury**, 200 pounds each of **common salt** and **saltpeter**, 50 pounds of reserved **caput mortuum** <term>dead head</term> <note>The solid residue remaining after a chemical process like distillation or sublimation.</note>, and 400 pounds of **rubified vitriol** <note>Vitriol (iron sulfate) that has been roasted until it turns red, indicating the formation of iron oxide.</note>. All these ingredients must be reduced to powder separately under a treading millstone.

->The mixing is done in this way.<-

<margin>Common sublimation of Mercury.</margin> The **quicksilver** and the **sublimated mercury**, along with a portion of the **common salt** and **saltpeter**, must first be thoroughly mixed together. Even if the salts have been made perfectly dry, they will nevertheless become moist from this mixture. In this way, the mercury "dies" <note>In alchemical terms, mercury is "killed" or "dies" when it loses its liquid, running form and is fixed into a solid powder or compound.</note> by being mixed with these specific substances. Then, add the remaining portion of the salts and continue to mix with an iron shovel until the mercury no longer appears "alive" <term>vivus</term> anywhere. This mixture is extremely damp; if you were to add the **rubified vitriol**—which is very dry—it would absorb the moisture in an instant, and the ingredients would simultaneously take on the hardness of stone. Therefore, instead of the vitriol, it is necessary to add the **caput mortuum** or the reserved residue...

concerning the first sublimation, or concerning **aqua fortis** <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>Literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid.</note>, it is all the same; in this way, the mixture is moderately dried, which you must now agitate through a sieve. Afterward, you can conveniently mix the sieved powder with **rubified vitriol**. Now you have the mixture, with which you should fill 16 large glass vessels (let a fourth part remain empty). After 24 hours, the odor has vanished, and then the **Mercury** begins to be sublimated (collect the **aqua fortis**, which is strengthened by other species and serves for the precipitation of **Mercury**). Continue with that heat...

### TACHENIUS

...[continuing from the previous page]... until you see the **Mercury** in the form of a cake, separated from its **dead head** <term>dead head</term> <note>original: *capite mortuo*; the useless chemical residue or dregs left over after a process like distillation or sublimation.</note> at the top, which usually occurs on the fifth day if you have worked well.

<margin>The Reformer's error.</margin>
Once the materials have cooled, the glass vessels crack spontaneously because of the heavy weight of the **sublimate** (collect the fragments separately, for they have some sublimate on them which you should scrape off, and with which **running Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurius currens*; liquid, metallic mercury.</note> must be **killed** <term>killed</term> <note>In alchemy, to "kill" mercury means to "mortify" it, or change its state from a liquid metal to a stable powder or compound.</note> another time). Take care that the ash does not fall through the cracks onto the cakes, which they carefully take up with both hands and set aside; they call these **panes** <note>original: *panes*; literally "loaves."</note>, each of which will weigh about 25 pounds, more or less, depending on the capacity of the vessel and how much mixture it held. Thus, from 280 pounds of **quicksilver** <note>original: *Mercurij viui*</note> and 20 of **sublimate**, you will collect three hundred and eighty pounds of **sublimate**. Thus the **quicksilver** increased in weight by 80 pounds; not indeed from the acid of the **rubified vitriol** <term>rubified vitriol</term> <note>Iron(III) sulfate that has been heated until it turns red; used as a reagent in various chemical processes.</note>, as the **reformer** <note>Tachenius frequently argues against a "reformer"—likely a reference to a specific contemporary author whose chemical theories he found flawed.</note> thinks—which is why he teaches it should be cautiously **calcined** <term>calcined</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to a high temperature in air to achieve decomposition or oxidation.</note>—but from the **common salt** <note>original: *sale communi*</note> alone, which I prove as follows.

<meta>This page continues the technical argument regarding the chemical composition of mercury and the causes of its weight gain during processing. On the previous page, the author began refuting the idea that mercury's weight increase comes solely from vitriol; here, he demonstrates through experiments with saltpeter and red vitriol that without the addition of common salt, no weight is gained, reinforcing his theory about the role of saline acids.</meta>

<header>HIPPOCRATES.</header>

<margin>Sweet red-colored sublimated mercury.</margin>
If the same **Mercury** <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: *Mercurius*; here referring to the quicksilver or liquid metallic mercury being discussed.</note> is sublimated with the same proportion of **saltpeter** <note>original: *nitri*; potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in many alchemical and chemical reactions.</note> and **rubified vitriol** <term>rubified vitriol</term> <note>original: *vitrioli rubificati*; iron(III) sulfate that has been roasted until it turns a deep red color.</note>, but without **common salt** <note>original: *sale communi*; sodium chloride. The author is highlighting that the absence of salt prevents weight gain.</note>, then a red Mercury rises, and it has acquired neither weight nor any corrosive quality.

<margin>Common precipitated mercury.</margin>
Likewise, if the same Mercury is precipitated with **aqua fortis** <note>original: *aqua forti*; literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid.</note> made from saltpeter and vitriol, it then becomes a shining red, yet acquires no weight. The reason is that the sulfurous saltpeter lifts the sulfur of the vitriol; together these act only upon their own kind—that is, upon the external sulfur of the Mercury—and thus nothing can grow in weight, nor take on a corrosive power.

->*Minerva moves her hand.*<-
<note>original: *Minerva manum movet*; a Latin proverb suggesting that while one might have the favor of the gods or wisdom (Minerva), one must still apply physical labor and "move the hand" to achieve the result.</note>

### TACHENIUS

<margin>Sulfur turns Mercury red.</margin>
...as long as it is pure, [place it] in a flat-bottomed vial with a very narrow neck, and cook it in a sand bath, first with a slow and moderate fire, and later with a stronger heat, to such a degree that the **Mercury** <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: *Mercurius*; here referring to quicksilver or elemental mercury.</note> strives to rise. If this happens by a simple movement, ensure that it falls back down. In this way, without any other addition except for external fire, there is produced a shining **red precipitate** <note>original: *præcipitatus rubeus*; likely red mercuric oxide, historically known as *mercurius præcipitatus per se* when made by heat alone.</note> similar to that which was precipitated with **aqua fortis** <note>original: *aqua forti*; nitric acid.</note> made from **vitriol** <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: *vitriolo*; metal sulfates, usually iron or copper sulfate.</note> and **saltpeter** <note>original: *nitro*; potassium nitrate.</note>. It acquires no additional weight, nor is it diminished, insofar as a small portion of its own sulfur has "mortified" it <note>original: *mortificauit*; a term in early chemistry meaning to change the metallic form or "kill" its quickness/fluidity.</note> and turned it red, just as I showed regarding the sulfurous saltpeter. It is certainly a property of sulfur to turn Mercury red, as I also demonstrated in the preparation of **cinnabar** <term>cinnabar</term> <note>original: *cinnabaris*; mercury sulfide, a bright red mineral.</note>, which also acquires no additional weight. This must be carefully considered by our colleagues, for it is of great importance.

Mix this red precipitate—whether made by itself or under any other condition—with **common salt** <note>original: *sale communi*; sodium chloride.</note>. Urge this mixture with a moderate fire, and it then rises into a **sublimate** <note>original: *sublimatum*; a solid substance produced by heating a material until it vaporizes and then collecting the deposit.</note>...

Thus that precipitate, which **Crollius** <note>Oswald Croll (c. 1563–1609), an influential German chemist and physician who promoted the use of chemical medicines.</note> calls **Turpeth mineral** <term>Turpeth mineral</term> <note>original: *Turbith minerale*; a yellow, basic mercuric sulfate used historically as a powerful medicine to induce vomiting.</note>, insofar as it is prepared with **spirit of vitriol** <term>spirit of vitriol</term> <note>original: *spiritu vitrioli*; sulfuric acid.</note> or sulfur, does not turn red, because very little of the sulfur of vitriol is elevated during distillation without **saltpeter** <note>original: *nitro*; potassium nitrate.</note>; however, due to the affinity it possesses, by acting upon the sulfur of the Mercury, it turns yellow, and it gains no weight once the alum-like taste has been washed away with hot water.

For just as all **medicines** <term>pharmaca</term> <note>original: *pharmaca*; drugs or medicines, from the Greek word implying both a cure and a poison.</note> are poisons in relation to our bodies, and not to the waste products, they consume us instead...

### TACHENII

<margin>Sulfur turns Mercury red.</margin>

For this reason, **Helmont** <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneer of pneumatic chemistry who was often critical of traditional mercury-based medicines.</note> says: "As long as common mercury can be **revived** <note>original: *reuificari*; the process of returning a mercury compound back to its liquid, metallic state.</note>, it is not the remedy of a good man." Rather, as I have said, because of its own **sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>In alchemical theory, "sulfur" was the internal principle of combustibility and color, not just the chemical element S.</note>, no matter how diligently it may be treated by the common method, it always remains the same Mercury.

And even if the sulfur, by great ingenuity and art, should be sequestered from it, the Mercury—as if impatient of a vacuum—clothes itself again with a new and similar sulfur in a short space of time. <margin>b. On Diet, Book 1.</margin> As the master (b) teaches: "separately, it is sufficient neither for itself, nor for any other." Indeed, beyond experience, this is confirmed by **Geber** <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), the foundational authority of medieval alchemy.</note> and the crowd of other wise men. Hence, quite elegantly, <margin>c. page 90.</margin> the Author of the **Golden Age** <note>likely referring to the *Aureum Seculum Redivivum* (The Golden Age Restored), a famous alchemical text by Adrian von Mynsicht.</note> (c) [writes]: **Saturn** <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for lead, though here used allegorically for the beginning of the chemical process.</note> the grandfather...

<summary>Tachenius argues that mercury cannot be truly transformed into a safe medicine because its internal alchemical "sulfur" inevitably returns, making it remain mercury regardless of preparation. At that point, the blood there turns sour and rots, smelling more foul than any stench, with such contagion that even the lips, through contact with it, [break out].

### HIPPOCRATES. 205

...however <note>Completing the word *autem* from the previous page's catchword.</note> **quicksilver** <term>mercurium vivum; liquid elemental mercury</term> with substances that mitigate pain, sometimes with good success. He therefore ordered the mercury to be "mortified" <term>mortificare; the process of "killing" or neutralizing the liquid state of mercury, usually by grinding it with fats or saliva until the globules disappear</term> with saliva and **oil of laurel** <note>original: *oleo laurino*</note>, which happens almost in a moment, and afterwards to mix it with the remaining ingredients.

<margin>The common crowd judges few things by truth, but many by opinion.</margin>

The "reformer" greets this "mortification" by Vigo with a loud, mocking laugh <note>original: *cachinno*</note>. He overturns the text and the opinion of that excellent man; indeed, he even adds insults and slanders. In place of quicksilver, he teaches one to add a corrosive substance without any washing or use of fire to drive off the "spirits" <note>referring to volatile acidic vapors</note>. Instead, while it is still a liquid, he orders **lard** <term>axungiam; animal fat, specifically pig fat, used as an ointment base</term> and oil to be added, as if a corrosive acid would "die" or be neutralized simply by being placed in fat.

From this it appears that the reformer of the **Augsburg Pharmacopoeia** <note>The *Pharmacopoeia Augustana* was one of the most authoritative European medical recipe books of the era.</note> does not have a single spark of discernment. Surgeons, for the purpose of consuming "foreign flesh" <note>original: *carne peregrina*; likely referring to necrotic tissue, warts, or "proud flesh" in a wound</note>, mix **Vigo’s precipitated mercury**—which is almost sweet to the taste—into fats and ointments, not so that its corrosive power is destroyed, but so that the medicine does not fall off the wound.

### 206 TACHENII

...ed <note>Completing a word from the previous page, likely "sublimated" or "discreet."</note>, though sweet and tasteless to the tongue, when mixed into an ointment, it consumes the flesh; certainly this caustic and gnawing [substance] of the reformer causes ulcers, and in the very place where it ought to soothe pains, it will generate new ones. This is the "artifice" that the reformer teaches us, about which that line from **Plautus** <note>Titus Maccius Plautus, a Roman playwright; this quote is from his play *Amphitruo*.</note> can best be said:

> *I will go to the physician, and there I will give myself over to death by poison.* <note>original: "Ibo ad medicum, atq; ibi me toxico morti dabo."</note>

However, **sublimated mercury** <term>mercurius sublimatus; mercury(II) chloride, a highly toxic corrosive</term> is "revived" <note>Returned to its metallic or non-corrosive state.</note> when the corrosive force is removed from it; besides **alkalis** <term>alcalia; basic substances that neutralize acids</term>, **antimony** <note>A brittle, silvery-gray metalloid often used in 17th-century medicine.</note> also performs this. **Simple aqua fortis** <term>aqua fortis simplex; nitric acid</term> does not dissolve this [antimony] because of the admixed sulfur, as I have shown above, but once salt is added <note>Creating *aqua regia*, a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids.</note>, it acts upon it immediately and separates its sulfur so that it floats on the surface like pitch; and this occurs in the **humid way** <term>via humida; chemical reactions performed in liquid solutions rather than by dry heat</term>.

<margin>**Butter of Antimony** <note>original: *Butyrum Antimonij*; antimony trichloride, a thick, oily substance.</note></margin>
<margin>Mercury, see 149</margin>

This substance is so sensitive that if even a single drop of water falls into it, the acidic spirits immediately join with the water, and the dissolved antimony turns white. Yet, if you cast common salt—whether natural or artificial—or other salty substances into this same "butter," even in large amounts, nothing will move; it will not even become cloudy, far from being destroyed.

### Chapter 26.

Whenever any acid is destroyed, it occurs not because of salts, as I have said, but because of **alkalis** <term>Alcalizata; basic substances that neutralize acids</term> or things that savor of their nature. The acid is then transformed into a different character, depending on the specific type of acid involved.

To many it will seem that I am putting forth paradoxes in this place by saying that the flame of fire is acidic. Indeed, I repeat: fire comes closest to the nature of salt, consisting of alkali and acid [b]. For that reason, fire is biting, sharp, vinegary, cutting, fine, penetrating, pure, incorruptible, and non-combustible; it is that very thing which preserves [substances] from corruption. Lead is burned with dry wood into **minium** <term>red lead; lead tetroxide ($Pb_3O_4$)</term>. If there are one hundred pounds of lead, after it has been converted into minium, one hundred and ten pounds are found. The question is asked: from where did the minium acquire those ten pounds? Certainly, nothing was added to it except the flame alone; this flame, therefore, took on a physical body within the lead. Furthermore, this body, which increased the weight of the lead from the flame, is of an acidic taste (a). The following experiment proves this.

<margin>
a It is known to everyone that volatile alkali is distilled from soot.
</margin>
Distill using a glass **retort** <term>a glass vessel with a long downward-pointing neck used for distillation</term> over an open fire from that very same wood with which you burned the lead; a liquor of an acidic taste will drip out. This is the same substance which, outside the retort in the form of a flame, licked the lead, mixed with it, and became "fixed" <note>rendered solid or non-volatile</note> in the **alkali** <note>Tachenius views the lead itself as the alkaline base that captures the "acid" of the fire</note>.

Melt the minium back into lead, and you will have one hundred pounds, just as you had before. Thus, those ten pounds were neither minium nor lead, but the acid expelled from the wood. <margin>Lead provides colors and glass.</margin>

### Chapter 27.

<margin>Tincture of corals.</margin>
With an open fire, **reverberate** <term>reverberatio; a process of heating a substance in a furnace where the flames are "reverberated" or deflected back onto the material</term> whole corals—for example, twelve ounces—for six days and nights (to the skilled, the heat of the fire serves as it does for the distillation of the **spirit of vitriol** <term>concentrated sulfuric acid</term>), and you will find fifteen ounces. That is, they have increased by three ounces, and are converted into a very fine powder. This increase is nothing else but the **acid from the flame**, which dissolves the corals, and is coagulated and fixed with them—after the manner of acids—into a salt. When you pour **distilled vinegar** <term>acetum distillatum; a purified acid used as a solvent</term> upon this salt, it does not emit bubbles <note>The "bubbles" are carbon dioxide being released; Tachenius correctly observes that the chemical state of the coral has changed so it no longer reacts with vinegar in the same way.</note>, as usually happens with corals that have not been reverberated; for they are truly saturated with acid. ->Chapter 28.<-

Regarding the properties and 
actions of iron, and how
it performs them in the human body,
there are many opinions among
<margin>Book 3, part 2, section 2, chapter 7.</margin>

<summary>Tachenius explains that iron acts as a remedy by neutralizing "astray" salts (acid-alkali imbalances) that cause blockages in organs like the liver and spleen. --- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s chemical explanation of how iron functions within the human body. He uses the analogy of "precipitation"—a process where a dissolved metal is forced out of a solution by another metal—to explain how iron interacts with internal "salts" and "humors." The text specifically references the famous "transmutation" of iron into copper observed in the mines of Goslar, clarifying it as a chemical reaction rather than magic.</meta>

<page-num>220</page-num>
->### TACHENIUS <-

...liquid <note>original: "acidulis"; completing the thought from the previous page</note> **acid**, it is separated (by the power of the alkali) from the mixture. In this separation, the aforementioned salts or humors flow toward the iron and adhere to it; just as we have seen happen with **silver** <term>Latin: argentum</term> dissolved in **aqua fortis** <note>nitric acid</note>. In this solution, if plates of **copper** <term>Latin: cuprum</term> are cast in, then immediately the acidity of the aqua fortis abandons the silver and corrodes the copper instead; the silver then clings to the copper like a dense, crumbling mass. 

<margin>
I do not speak to that man who, in his whole life, has looked at nothing but **solid excrement** <term>Latin: solidum stercus</term>.
</margin>

Again, if you place a plate of **iron** <term>Latin: ferrum</term> into that solution (which has now turned green because of the copper), the liquid immediately drops the copper and corrodes the iron; the copper then clings to the iron. (We see this when the **vitriolic water of Goslar** <note>A famous mining town in the Harz Mountains of Germany, known since the Middle Ages for its metal-rich springs</note> corrodes iron; the copper falls out of the water and takes the place of the iron, because that **vitriol** <note>metallic sulfate</note> contained "immature" copper. This red powder, when melted by fire, turns into copper. Many learned men—though not those trained in **Hippocratic doctrine** <note>Traditional medical theory based on the balance of the four humors</note>—believed this **precipitation** <term>Latin: præcipitatio</term> was a **transmutation** <note>The alchemical change of one metal into another</note> of iron into copper). Mechanical logic and necessity dictate that the same thing happens within the human body, especially since nature remains consistent in all things; so that...

<summary>Tachenius explains the medicinal action of iron by comparing it to the chemical precipitation of metals (silver, copper, and iron) in acids. He argues that internal bodily humors are drawn to iron just as minerals in the mines of Goslar react with metal plates.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 220 regarding the chemical reactions of metals—specifically iron—within the human body. <page-num>221</page-num>
->### HIPPOCRATES <-

The old man speaks <note>original: "loquitur senex"</note>. For unless the waste humor were of a certain semi-acid nature and taste, iron would not be dissolved within the body into a green or black liquid, as we observe daily with our eyes, and as I have shown in the proper place. And unless the waste products are tinted in this way, then iron is administered <note>given as medicine</note> recklessly; for the iron does not find a suitable humor that can dissolve it. In such a case, the iron actually constricts <note>tightens or causes constipation</note> the body, even if the "opening" **saffron of Mars** <term>Latin: crocus martis; a chemical preparation of iron oxide used to treat blockages</term> created by the reformer <note>likely referring to Paracelsus or a similar chemical physician</note> had been taken by mouth. The same
<margin>
The use and abuse of acids.
</margin>
must be understood regarding **acid spirits** <note>concentrated mineral acids</note>, such as those of **vitriol** <note>sulfuric acid</note>, sulfur, nitre, and salt. Unless these find a "stray" **alkali** <term>A substance that neutralizes acid; Tachenius believed all bodily functions were a balance of acid and alkali</term> in the human body—one that has been cast out by the vital force and is therefore diseased—which can receive these acid spirits into itself, then they do more harm than good. For by their excess, they then infect the food nearest the stomach with their own acidity, rendering it unfit for nourishment. A physician friend in Rome in the year 1656, during the time of the **plague** <note>The Great Plague of Rome, which killed approximately 150,000 people</note>, used **spirit of sulfur** <note>sulfuric acid</note> in great abundance for the sake of protection; he ended up **arthritic** (a), and
<margin>
(a) A remedy for arthritis [is found] in alkalis.

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius's debunking of Leonhard Thurneysser’s "Golden Nail" trick. <page-num>223</page-num>

<sig>K 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: ritif-</meta>

[Hippocrates] confirms that gold does not adhere to iron; indeed, molten gold corrodes the iron in a moment and turns it into rust [a).

<margin>
a Just as common sulfur does, so gold turns iron into rust.
</margin>

Gold is joined to iron, however, by the medium of **precipitation** <note>The process of a substance settling out of a solution; here, Tachenius refers to "cementation" where one metal coats another</note>, as I have said, specifically on the foundation by which iron precipitates copper. Therefore, cut an iron nail into two parts, moisten the end, at least with saliva, and immediately touch this part with **copper vitriol** <term>Latin: vitriolum cupreum; blue vitriol or copper sulfate, which reacts with iron to leave a thin coating of metallic copper</term>, and at that very point, the end of the iron turns red and has now acquired the nature of copper [b].

<margin>
b It is impossible to transition from one extreme to another without a medium, as the Philosopher <note>Aristotle</note> testifies in the 6th book of *Physics*.
b Glue of gold <note>original: "Auri gluten"; referring to the soldering agent</note>.
</margin>

Moreover, gold is very easily joined to copper. And so, to this copper-coated iron, fit a point made of gold, and with **borax** <term>A mineral salt used as a flux in soldering to help the metals join</term> and gold **solder** <term>Latin: solidatura; a fusible metal alloy used to join less fusible metals</term> (which is made from copper coin [c]; it is better if a little silver and gold is melted in by eye), melt them together in a suitable coal fire in the usual way, and you will have a "golden nail" of such great fame. 

Thurneysser tinted this nail with rust, with which he hid the gold, and offered it to the Great Prince (without doubt) to be handled with his hands; the nail, being thus masked, from a...

*when legitimately and properly administered (a); indeed, it even behaves as the* LIQUOR ALKAHEST *and not like other acid spirits, which suffer change while dissolving, and are destroyed and converted into another substance, etc.”*

Would that Basilius, when he taught how to distill this vinegar, had feared impostors, just as Hip- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, leading into "Hippocrates" on the next page.</note>

...pocrates <note>Completing the word "Hippocrates" from the previous page's catchword.</note>, Paracelsus, and the rest [of the great physicians] were the Reformer, he would not have peddled this as the **Liquor Alkahest**; indeed, he would not have celebrated it with so many praises, nor would he have so fraudulently imposed it upon the world. He advises that this **vinegar** <note>original: *acetum*; Tachenius is revealing that the "secret solvent" is actually just common vinegar.</note> should be purchased at any price, and adds these magnificent words, which, moreover, you should mark with a deep pebble <note>original: *alto notabis calculo*; a Latin idiom meaning to mark something as particularly important or memorable.</note>, namely:

<margin>It is a disgrace for a teacher when his own fault refutes him.</margin>

*"Enjoy this secret, friendly reader,"* he says: *"and out of favor for me, in exchange for this faithful communication, remain benevolent. O Jupiter! Come hither, most keen searchers; a secret is revealed to you that has never been committed to writing by anyone."*

But nature laughs! To teach shameful lies instead of the truth! There are those who, without great labor, time, or expense, distill a similar and more powerful liquor by collecting the **mother of vinegar** into a large vessel and distilling it; yet they do not proclaim it to be the **Alkahest**.

Let us now see the subject from which the Reformer distills this wonderful solvent, which he appropriates for himself with a worthy name, calling it... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "lat-", likely for "appellat" (he calls).</note>

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s exposure of a rival chemist (the "Reformer"). The previous page ended with Tachenius preparing to reveal the actual substance the Reformer uses to create his supposed "universal solvent." This page explains that the "Spirit of Venus" is merely distilled vinegar recovered from copper.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>TACHENIUS</header>
<meta>catchword: quo-</meta>

...and by this name, our **spirit of Venus** <note>original: *spiritus noster veneris*; in alchemy, Venus is the symbol for copper. The "spirit" refers to the acidic vapor or essence derived from it.</note>.

That substance is **verdigris** <note>original: *ærugo*; the green pigment or crust that forms on copper.</note>, or **green copper**, which is made in this way: Copper plates are buried with grape skins <note>original: *vinaceis*; the remains of grapes after pressing, which turn into vinegar.</note> that are already turning sour; this acidity, by corroding the copper, coagulates with it into a **salt** <note>here meaning a chemical compound, specifically copper acetate.</note>, just as happens with every acid through the process of corrosion, as I have demonstrated visibly regarding lead and coral. 

The Reformer purifies that green salt with **distilled vinegar**; from there, clearly, nothing else can distill out but vinegar, as **Basil** <note>Referring to Basil Valentine, a legendary alchemical authority whom the Reformer likely cited to gain credibility.</note> candidly admits in the place cited above. It is just as happens with **spirit of vitriol** <note>sulfuric acid</note>: by corroding copper, **vitriol** <note>blue vitriol or copper sulfate</note> is then formed; if that is distilled, you will draw out nothing else but that same spirit of vitriol, or the liquid <note>original: *stagma*; a technical term for a distilled drop or essence.</note> which you poured on it in the first place. This follows the rule: *No one gives what they do not have* <note>original: *nemo dat quod non habet*; a logical and legal maxim implying that a substance cannot produce a "spirit" more powerful than what was used to create it.</note>.

As for the remaining **caput mortuum** <term>caput mortuum: literally "dead head"; the useless solid residue or dross left in the flask after distillation.</term>, or the dregs: melt them in a crucible, having added a little **nitre** <note>saltpeter</note> and **tartar** <note>potassium bitartrate, used here as a flux to help the metal melt.</note>, and then the copper returns to exactly what it was before it was changed into green copper with vinegar. 

Behold the "secret and arcane matter" which the Reformer, with such excessive generosity, wished to communicate for the public good, and...

<vocab>spiritus veneris, ærugo, æs viride, cuprum, vinacea, sal viride, acetum distillatum, Basilius, spiritus vitrioli, vitriolum, caput mortuum, nitrum, tartarum</vocab>

<summary>Tachenius demystifies the "Spirit of Venus," proving it is merely distilled vinegar recycled through copper (verdigris). He argues that the Reformer is not performing "high alchemy" but a simple chemical loop that yields nothing new.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, chemistry, copper, verdigris, vinegar, Spirit of Venus, Basil Valentine, distillation, caput mortuum, chemical critique</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Otto Tachenius’s critique of the "Reformer." <page-num>229</page-num>
->HIPPOCRATES.<-

<margin>Secrets made public lose their value. <note>original: *Arcana publicata vilescunt*</note></margin>
...[he] persuades [the reader] to buy it at any price <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding the "Reformer" marketing his chemical secrets.</note>. He says, "Enjoy this secret, dear reader," and so on. Away with these impostures! Even the common people laugh at them, and those who know better have no faith in them; they know that secrets are not to be uncovered except through **parables**, **allegories**, **metaphors**, **veils**, and similar figures of speech. This is how **Hippocrates** <note>Though known as the "Father of Medicine," alchemists often claimed Hippocrates as an initiate into their hidden mysteries.</note> and venerable antiquity commanded and practiced. "Speak not in the ears of a fool," says the wise man <note>A reference to the biblical Book of Proverbs 23:9.</note>, "for he will despise the wisdom of your words." So writes **Giovanni Pico della Mirandola** in his book *On the Dignity of Man*. **Pythagoras**, **Plato**, **Boethius**, **Macrobius** in his *Commentary on the Dream of Scipio*, **Cornelius Tacitus**, and more—all agree with one voice that secrets must not be revealed, so that the unworthy may be properly barred from them.

But come now! Is it not possible, with less labor and expense, to use simple **distilled vinegar** <note>original: *aceto distillato*; Tachenius’s central argument is that the Reformer’s expensive "solvent" is just common vinegar.</note>, since it is one and the same thing? Do not the authors of the **Tirocinium** <note>Refers to the *Tirocinium Chymicum* (1610) by Jean Béguin, one of the first and most famous chemistry textbooks, which simplified laboratory processes for students.</note> teach how to prepare the **salt of pearls** <note>term: *salt of pearls*: A medicinal preparation made by dissolving pearls in an acid (like vinegar) and then evaporating or precipitating the result.</note> and **corals** with distilled vinegar, and then to wash it? Yet this preparation is found to be useless, for it is far too acid...

### OF TACHENIUS

...[the authors] are coagulated with the solute, as I have shown several times above. But to summarize briefly: the *Vinegar and Spirit of Venus* <note>original: *Spiritus veneris*; a potent acid obtained by distilling copper acetate (verdigris), which Tachenius argues is no better than high-quality vinegar.</note> of the "Reformer" are nothing but water impregnated with a volatile acid salt; if it corrodes anything, it is immediately coagulated, and afterward, through combustion, it is fixed into an alkali.

What kind of special advantage does the "Reformer’s" magistery <term>magistery: a term for a concentrated medicinal preparation or a "master" essence of a substance.</term> of corals and pearls have over others found in introductory textbooks <term>Tyrocinia: likely referring to Jean Beguin’s *Tyrocinium Chymicum*, the standard 17th-century textbook for beginning chemists.</term>? The spirit of vitriol <note>sulfuric acid</note> or sulfur, which precipitates both, teaches us this: the reason is that a more powerful acidity seizes the dissolved substance and undergoes coagulation within it. Because of this, it even increases in weight, and it cannot be recovered from that state except by the use of violent fire.

<margin>a. *Cicero, Pro Rabirio Postumo, 21: "If anyone has committed perjury once, he should not be believed afterwards."*</margin>

Are these the "inventions" written from "true reason and experience" (b) <note>Tachenius is mockingly quoting the Reformer’s claims of originality.</note> that have supposedly been seen or noticed by no one until now? And all this, he claims, is for the solace of the human race,

## Mixed Medicines: Many Healthy and Many Harmful.

Indeed, the combining of simple substances according to the whim of an ignorant man makes fools of Kings and kills them; 
<margin>a Hippocrates, Book 1, Aphorism 1.</margin> 
and the opportunity for healing (a) *is fleeting in a moment, causing it to pass by through uncertain conjectures.* <note>Original: *occasioq: medendi... in momento labilis.* Tachenius is quoting the famous first aphorism of Hippocrates: "Life is short, art is long, opportunity fleeting, experiment perilous, judgment difficult."</note> I add this one thing: I advise that those who combine recipes like sorcerers should be shunned and repelled; they trade in our ailments with their monstrous compositions and cast lots upon our very lives. 
<margin>A single substance cannot possess two species at once—a point our readers should note carefully.</margin> 
It is impossible, I say, or at least difficult—as I demonstrated in the case of the nettle <note>Original: *vrtica*. Tachenius likely refers to a previous argument where he used the humble nettle to show that even a "simple" plant has complex chemical properties.</note>—to be able to establish anything certain except through opinion and conjecture alone. However, whenever individual things do not achieve the intended purpose, then they certainly require compounding, as I have clearly shown with wound-healing potions, tinctures, and inks; and I will demonstrate this further with a single example. Take two parts juice of sage and one part vitriol of Mars <term>Vitriol of Mars: An old chemical name for iron(II) sulfate. When mixed with tannins (found in plants like sage), it creates a black precipitate, the basis for traditional iron gall ink.</term>; when these two are mixed, they suddenly turn black. This is because the hidden alkali <term>Alkali: In 17th-century chemistry, an "alkali" was the opposite of an "acid." Tachenius was famous for his theory that all things are composed of "acid and alkali."</term> of the sage absorbs the acid of the vitriol. After thi...

### HIPPOCRATES. ###

[Absyn]thium <note>The word begins on the previous page as "Absynthium" (Wormwood).</note> ponticum <term>Pontic wormwood: *Artemisia pontica*, a species of wormwood known for being less bitter and more aromatic than common wormwood.</term>, as it is less odorous, they chose; and at the same time, they cooked it with spike <term>Spike: Refers to spike lavender (*Lavandula latifolia*), used here for its medicinal properties despite its strong scent.</term> in old wine, so that it might pass away entirely along with the spirit of the wine, to which odor is very easily joined. This is proven by the addition of the juice of quinces; for unless this had been the intention of the ancients, they would have called into use quince-wine <note>original: *cydoniten*</note> and wormwood-wine <note>original: *absynthiten*</note> in place of this syrup, as can be seen in
<margin>a) Book 5, chapter 49.</margin>
Dioscorides (a). What remained, they protected from white mold with sugar, and thickened it into a syrup.

In the same way, various wines, if mixed together and drunk—as is accustomed to happen at banquets—not only bring great labor and distress to the human Governor <term>Governor: Translation of *Rector*. In Tachenius's chemical philosophy, the "Rector" or "Governor" is a vital spiritual force or "archeus" that directs the functions of an organ, in this case, the stomach.</term> (whom I showed above in Chapter 15 to be sitting at the mouth of the stomach, according to
<margin>b) On Humors.</margin>
Hippocrates (b)) for the sake of their unification, but moreover, they cause intoxication very 
<margin>c) Giovanni Boccaccio also observed this in the Decameron, Day 7, Novel 2.</margin>
easily (c), and like a frenzy, they are sometimes thrown back through vomiting in delicate people. However, when the juices of those same grapes, though multiform and varied, are united through fermentation, then from the different "governors" there becomes a single, healthy one that does not harm the sto— <note>The text cuts off mid-word, "lædens" (harming), which matches the catchword "dens" at the bottom of the page.</note>

<meta>catchword: [læ]dens</meta>

<summary>Tachenius explains the pharmaceutical rationale behind the ancient preparation of Wormwood Syrup, using an analogy of mixed wines versus fermented blends to illustrate how the stomach's "Governor" processes substances.</summary>

<keywords>syrup of wormwood, Pontic wormwood, spike lavender, quince juice, Dioscorides, the Governor, stomach, fermentation, Hippocrates, Boccaccio, pharmacy</keywords>

--- Page 294 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 237 regarding the "Governor" (Rector) of the stomach and the chemical unification of substances. Tachenius is concluding his argument that medicinal compounds, like Theriaca, function as a single unit once properly prepared and fermented.</meta>

<page-num>238</page-num>
<header>TACHENIUS</header>
<meta>catchword: con-</meta>

...[thick]ened drink: just as from various simples <term>Simples: Individual medicinal herbs or minerals used as ingredients in a compound medicine.</term>, united through fermentation, Theriaca <term>Theriaca: A famous ancient "universal antidote" made of dozens of ingredients; Tachenius viewed it as a perfect chemical union.</term> is made: over which a single Governor <note>original: *rector*. In Tachenius's philosophy, this is the internal vital force that directs chemical processes.</note> predominates. For this reason, it is praised by Avicenna <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina): A highly influential Persian physician (c. 980–1037) whose works were central to European medicine for centuries.</note> for its great virtue, as I noted in the preface.

<margin>Far be it from me to be unaware that an unknown thing, demonstrated for the use of one's neighbor, is not without slander <note>Tachenius is acknowledging that introducing new or "unknown" chemical theories often invites criticism.</note>.</margin>
Since, however, it is not my purpose here to "show a pebble on the seashore" (as the saying goes), where it is well known there is an infinite number; especially since this style of writing is repugnant to my nature and temperament: I am weary of lingering longer on such trifles, wherefore I now lay down my pen.

Behold the matter; with this, the reformer has come onto the stage, before he recognized Nature as similar in every thing.

<margin>In the acid dwells the specific Governor, which gives form to the alkali.</margin>
Now for our people, as a corollary, three words in your ear: **acid gives form to alkali** <note>original: *acidum informat alcali*. This is the central tenet of Tachenius's "Acid-Alkali" theory of chemistry and medicine.</note>. Through these: the most ancient foundations of the oldest medicine and of the newest viper salt <term>Viper salt: A chemical preparation derived from the flesh of vipers, which Tachenius believed held the "volatile salt" necessary for curing many diseases.</term> [are found], if you have understood me, in absolute agreement, by a perpetual and immutable likeness, by the decree of Nature and my [dear] Hippocrates, by harmonic and mechanical reasoning, and by the highest necessity, among themselves

<summary>Tachenius concludes this section by asserting his central chemical theory: that all matter and medicine function through the interaction of "acid" and "alkali." He emphasizes that a single "Governor" or vital force oversees these chemical unions, as seen in complex remedies like Theriaca.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Tachenius, acid and alkali, Governor (Rector), Theriaca, Avicenna, viper salt, Hippocrates, pharmaceutical fermentation</keywords>

--- Page 295 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument from page 238. Having discussed the "Governor" of the stomach and the chemical union of substances like Theriaca, the author now addresses his critics and closes the work.</meta>

<page-num>239</page-num>
<header>HIPPOCRATES <note>original: *Ippocrates*. This header likely identifies the authority under which this section of the medical argument concludes.</note></header>

I have shown that [nature and art] ought to agree. Since our so-called friend <note>Tachenius is likely referring to a specific contemporary critic or rival whose chemical theories he has been refuting throughout the text.</note> was once unable to grasp this consensus of nature and art from examples, nor could he perceive it, he rose up and, like a rabid dog, attacked, bit, and slandered, placing himself far above others, as is evident throughout his writing. For thus every bird sings according to the tune of its own beak, following that saying of **Terence** (a): "Nothing is more unjust than an ignorant man, who thinks nothing is right except what he does himself" <note>original: *Homini imperito, nunquam quicquam iniustius, qui nisi quod ipse facit, nil rectum putat.* This quote is from the Roman playwright Terence's comedy *The Brothers* (*Adelphoe*), a standard classical reference used to point out the arrogance of the unlearned.</note>.
<margin>a In *Adelphoe*, Act 1, Scene 2.</margin>

->T H E   E N D.<-

<summary>In this concluding passage, the author defends his chemical theories against a hostile critic, using a classical quote from Terence to dismiss the opponent's narrow-mindedness. He emphasizes the harmony between natural processes and the "art" of chemistry.</summary>

--- Page 296 ---
<meta>This page contains the official printing license (imprimatur) for the work concluded on the previous page. Having finished his chemical defense, Tachenius now includes the legal authorizations required by the Venetian Republic to publish the book.</meta>

### We, the Reformers of the University
### of Padua <note>original: *Riformatori dello Studio di Padova*. This was the powerful magistracy in Venice responsible for overseeing the University of Padua and controlling the censorship and licensing of books.</note>.

Having seen by the testimony of the Father Inquisitor <note>The Church official responsible for ensuring the text contained no heresy.</note> regarding the book entitled *Otto Tachenius’s Chemical Hippocrates* <note>original: *Ottonis Tacchenij Hypocrates Chimicus*</note>, that there is nothing therein against the Holy Catholic Faith, and likewise by the certificate of our Secretary, nothing against Princes or good morals, we grant license to Combi and La Noù <note>Sebastiano Combi and Giovanni Giacomo La Noù were prominent booksellers and publishers active in Venice during the mid-17th century.</note> to be able to print it, provided they observe the established orders, etc.

->Dated April 3, 1666.<-

[ Andrea Contarini, Knight, Procurator, Reformer.
[ Battista Nani, Knight, Procurator, Reformer <note>Battista Nani (1616–1678) was a famous Venetian diplomat and historian who served as an official Reformer of the University.</note>.

->Angelo Nicolosi, Secretary.<-

<summary>This page presents the official publication license granted by the Venetian authorities in 1666, confirming that Tachenius’s chemical work passed both religious and secular censorship. It names the prominent Venetian publishers Combi and La Noù as the authorized printers.</summary>
<keywords>imprimatur, University of Padua, Venice, censorship, Inquisition, Otto Tachenius, book history, 1666, Battista Nani, legal license</keywords>
